in a longer-term historical perspective, but rather how to present the current
situation to the world. Th ere was little room for emotional recollections from
the past, a past that had to be silenced or, better yet, forgotten. Political mea-
sures accompanied such eff orts: the pardons of repressors issued by President
Carlos Menem in Argentina, the defeat of the national referendum to repeal
an amnesty law in Uruguay, the transition made with authoritarian “moor-
ings” and “enclaves” in Chile, a modern, booming Brazil … all part of a

Perspectives on the Past • 29
vision of the future in which the past was just that, something painful per-
haps, but over and done.
Despite the apparent calm and apathy, however, other things were brew-
ing that would modify the political and cultural scenario in the following
years. In fact, the 1990s saw a great number of events associated with set-
tling accounts with past repression in the region. First of all, after recovering
from the disheartening pardons in Argentina and the negative outcome of
the Uruguayan referendum, the human rights movements in these countries
continued to push for justice in courts while making great eff orts to pro-
mote commemorative activities. Furthermore, a new generation came of age
during this decade, bringing new demands. Organizations of children of the
disappeared emerged and gained public visibility, with novel ways of protest-
ing, including the performative innovation of the escraches in Argentina and
Uruguay and the funas in Chile (Bonaldi 2006; Sempol 2006; Stern 2010).
Second, the presence of the human rights movement and its interaction
with new and diverse social groups (sexual minorities, ethnic minorities,
women’s and student movements, victims of economic rights violations,
i.e., the unemployed, the homeless, etc.) expanded the fi eld of demands
related to human rights violations.9 Myriad social actors set out to demand
justice, recognition, and state action, jeopardizing the reiterated attempts
to present a (neoliberal) “normality.” Th e image was one of human groups
challenging the state and demanding—with varying intensities and levels of
success—that it acknowledge violations to rights both past and present and
act accordingly.
Th ird, international legal pressure mounted during the 1990s. European
judges and courts investigated the role of Argentina’s military in the disap-
pearance of citizens from European countries. In March 1990, a French
court sentenced former military captain Alfredo Astiz to life imprisonment
(in absentia) for the disappearance of two French nuns. In Italy, the judicial
branch also sought justice for Italian victims of the Southern Cone dictator-
ships, holding trials and hearing witness testimony. Starting in 1996, Spanish
courts initiated legal proceedings for crimes against humanity committed in
Argentina. Numerous people presented their accusations and testimonies
(Anguita 2001). Later, in 1998, the courts of Spain attracted international
attention with the Pinochet case, which in turn shed light on Operation
Condor (Boccia Paz 1999; Dinges 2004). Th e 1992 discovery of the “Terror
Archive” in Asunción (González 2002b) provided documentation of this
regional coordination of repression, a fi nding that had enormous repercus-
sions in the region.
Th e presence of the dictatorial past and its political importance forged
new spaces for public visibility. In Argentina, a watershed moment came
in 1995 with the confessions of former military captain Adolfo Scilingo

30 • Th e Struggle for the Past
regarding the “death fl ights” (Verbitsky 1995), leading the head of the army
to acknowledge the institution’s role in the repression and issue an apology.
In Chile, the key year was 1998, when Pinochet stepped down as com-
mander in chief of the army, was appointed senator, and later that same year
was arrested in London. In Uruguay, social activism reignited in 1996—the
year President Julio María Sanguinetti began his second term—with the
March of Silence on 20 May, a date that became emblematic for memory of
the repression (Marchesi 2002).10 A few years later, in 2000, President Jorge
Batlle had to reply to the international calls for justice in the Gelman case.11
In Brazil, the authoritarian past was not a prominent theme in public debates
during those years, though Congress did enact a law on disappeared persons
in 1995, acknowledging the state’s responsibility and authorizing the pay-
ment of reparations to relatives.
During this period, the state tried to keep the legacy of the authoritarian
past under wraps. It was necessary for the countries to present themselves
as “normal” in a present characterized by a neoliberal agenda and structural
reforms. Th e governmental responses to demands by social movements and
international courts were aimed at “overcoming” or “settling” accounts with
the past or, in some cases, promoting reconciliation and dialogue (Chile),
peace (Uruguay), or silence (Brazil). In the case of Argentina, where social
movements were particularly tenacious, the state resorted to a strategy of
compartmentalization. Besides paying reparations to survivors and relatives,
it created the National Genetic Data Bank to help recover the children kid-
napped or born in captivity, at the behest of the Grandmothers of the Plaza
de Mayo. At the same time, it limited its recognition of international lawsuits.
Furthermore, President Menem proposed a “Monument to Reconciliation”
at the beginning of 1998, which never came to fruition due to strong public
protest. It would seem that, as in Germany, the search for normality led the
countries of the Southern Cone to introduce policies aimed at minimizing
public interest and visibility of the issue.
Th irty Years Later
In the twenty-fi rst century, to continue with the German analogy, it could
be argued that the very meaning of “normalization” has shifted: what is
“normal” for a country, government, or society is not trying to forget or
remain indiff erent but to face the past head on and open up the box of vio-
lence and repression. Of course, this cannot be achieved without confl icts
or attempts to do just the opposite. Th e state strategies of the 1990s did
not have the expected eff ect of closure, and the state then took on the task
of confronting the past by forming investigative commissions, starting legal
inquiries, paying reparations, and introducing policies on commemoration,

Perspectives on the Past • 31
archives, museums, and memorials. Th ese state policies were the result of the
demands and collective actions of the human rights movement, especially of
victims and their relatives.
Th us, in Uruguay in 2000, a Peace Commission (Comisión para la Paz) was
formed to “consolidate peace among Uruguayans” and thus settle accounts
with the past. Th is commission was entrusted with seeking information about
past repression, and had to do so without any collaboration on the part of the
armed forces. Far from settling accounts with the past, the commission’s report,
released in 2003, sparked demands for trials and new investigations, reigniting
attempts to fi nd and identify the bodies of victims. Th e electoral victory of
the Broad Front (Frente Amplio) in 2004 and the swearing in of President
Tabaré Vázquez paved the way for new investigations (to identify corpses, for
example) and increased social visibility of the topic. Th e government’s inten-
tion was to open investigations in order to somehow achieve closure. However,
over the next few years, the interaction between social movements and the
state—and the infl uence of what was happening in Argentina—led to trials,
new investigative commissions and reports,12 new memorials, and a museum.
Although the eff orts to repeal laws granting impunity have not been successful,
the questions associated with the dictatorial past remained relevant and present
on the political agenda.
In Argentina, during the fi rst years of the twenty-fi rst century, there was
a wave of “truth trials.” Due to the fact that laws guaranteeing impunity and
amnesty prevented criminal trials from being held, the justifi cation for these
trials was the “right to the truth” of victims’ relatives and society as a whole.
Th e Inter-American Court of Human Rights recognized this right, and the
trials were held to investigate what had become of each victim and inform
the families. Once again, in this type of trial the country pioneered ways to
address pending issues vis-à-vis past repression (Andriotti Romanin 2013).
Shortly afterward, Argentina’s Congress overturned the legislation limiting
legal action against perpetrators. Once the Supreme Court declared these
laws unconstitutional in 2005, new lawsuits were fi led and criminal proceed-
ings began anew.
During this same period, the state introduced a range of memory poli-
cies, maintaining an active political and social agenda linked to the past.
Signs were put up at numerous clandestine detention centers, many of which
opened to the public; the date of the coup d’état (24 March) became an offi -
cial holiday; and eff orts to identify and recover children kidnapped during
the dictatorship continued stronger than ever.
In Chile, the state took a signifi cant step when it established the National
Commission on Political Imprisonment and Torture (Comisión Nacional
sobre Prisión Política y Tortura) in 2003. Th e commission received some
thirty-seven thousand reports of political imprisonment and torture under

32 • Th e Struggle for the Past
the Pinochet regime. In the prologue to the report, which was published in
2004, President Ricardo Lagos stated,
Th ere is no global precedent for a report such as this, as it reconstructs the
full picture of our country’s abjections thirty-one years later, while seeking to
lay the foundations for rebuilding our memory. It is an act aimed at return-
ing victims’ dignity and an attempt to heal the wounds of our national soul.
(Comisión Nacional sobre Prisión Política y Tortura 2004)
Following the report, a policy of economic reparations was introduced.
At the same time, and within the existing legal limitations, criminal pro-
ceedings and trials multiplied, with charges piling up against even Pinochet
until his death. Th e opening of the impressive Memory Museum (Museo
de la Memoria) in 2010, the reopening of investigations by the National
Commission on Political Imprisonment and Torture and its subsequent 2011
report, the payment of reparations to victims, and the identifi cation and
opening of detention centers are public practices and policies clearly high-
lighting the recent past as part of the present.
In Brazil, more than thirty years after the beginning of the dictatorship, a
Truth Commission (Comissão Nacional da Verdade) was established in 2011.
A few years earlier, the Justice Ministry established an Amnesty Commission
(Comissão de Anistia) and published the report of the Special Commission
on the Murdered and Disappeared (Comissão Especial sobre Mortos e
Desaparecidos Políticos) in 2007. Th e government’s acknowledgment of dic-
tatorial crimes through the establishment of the Truth Commission had
eff ects at the local, state, and institutional level, since a number of uni-
versities, unions, local governments, professional associations, and others
created their own commissions to investigate dictatorial repression within
their respective institutions (Jelin 2016). Th ere was also a Truth and Justice
Commission (Comisión de Verdad y Justicia) in Paraguay, which began
investigations in 2004.
In short, it would seem that a “normal” government is expected to face the
past and promote measures aimed at truth, justice, and acknowledgment. As
in Germany, there is some degree of “ritualization” and even “routinization”
in the entire process of confronting the past, as well as other connotations
connected to the ideas of “truth” and “justice.” Soothing memory policies
are introduced in an attempt to “tame” struggles. Trade and tourism entre-
preneurs may also seize on the recent past in proposals often riddled with
ambiguities and ambivalence. What is important here is the fact that these
proposals exist and trigger social activism. Such activism suggests the recogni-
tion of the confl ictive nature of such proposals, the diverse actors and their
orientations and interests, and the multiple voices that any democracy should
heed and integrate.

Perspectives on the Past • 33
Can Accounts Be Settled?
Does this mean that we are advancing toward a fi nal settling of accounts with
the recent past? Is a defi nitive solution possible, so that the past becomes
history and ceases to be a present-day confl ict? Th e “past” is neither closed
nor stable, and therefore the answer is no. At the start of the transition, the
debate was focused on human rights violations under dictatorship and the
immediate demands for “truth” and “justice.” As time passed, both the actors
and the interpretations of this past changed. Th e defi nition of the past and its
periodization also changed. Th us, after four decades, social debate and state
acknowledgment in Argentina honed in on the period prior to dictatorship,
especially the issue of responsibilities in the armed struggle, and on the civil-
ian responsibilities and ties to the military during dictatorship. In general, no
defi nitive resolution is possible because, as Hannah Arendt noted, there are
crimes and damages that, due to their magnitude, are irreparable, and any
attempt at closure is thus condemned to failure.
Ultimately, no defi nitive resolution is possible because the timeline of
memory is not linear. Th e idea that, as time passes, the past recedes and
its presence fades does not always apply: the past may refuse to pass, and
it can also return with renewed strength. Th is is because the human rights
movement does not allow people to forget, because new generations ask and
reinterpret, because there is no satisfactory or timely solution for demands.
Perhaps what is “normal” is for memory to remain open and always be sub-
ject to debate, in a process of continuous revision that yields no fi nal conclu-
sions.13 In many historical junctures, and in many state and social proposals,
there appears to be an intention to close, stitch, or heal. Not doing so gener-
ates anxiety and unrest. However, this open nature of the labors of memory
is what makes it creative and productive, and for that very reason, a topic of
dispute and of study, never fi nished, always confl ictive.
Notes
Th e title of this chapter is a tribute to the memory of Norbert Lechner, who provided a sharp,
in-depth analysis on the complex levels of building democracy. Lechner’s book, La confl ictiva y
nunca acabada construcción del orden deseado (1986a), is a fundamental reference in this fi eld.
1. Th e list of Nazi-linked events commemorated includes Kristallnacht (the Night of Broken
Glass) in 1938 (commemorated in 1988), the Wannsee Conference, where the fi nal solu-
tion was contrived in 1942 (commemorated in 1992), the 1994 commemorations of the
Allied invasion of Normandy in 1944; the end of the war, commemorated in 1995; and
several others.

34 • Th e Struggle for the Past
2. In this section, I summarize the arguments of Olick (2003), who focuses his attention
on the history of the “offi cial memory,” that is, the accounts about the Nazi past that the
German state gradually elaborated over the years following the end of World War II.
3. Th ough the most commonly accepted version of Allende’s death is by suicide, with refer-
ence to his “farewell” message, there is an alternative claim, namely that the president was
assassinated. Controversies and struggles over how the past is interpreted—and over the
“events” themselves—show up regularly in these matters.
4. Starting in the 1960s, and especially in the 1970s, the armed forces of Latin America
received training and support from the United States for the “fi ght against subversion”
in the region. Declassifi ed U.S. State Department documents have revealed the deep
involvement of CIA agents, as well as the international connections of the armed forces
in each country. Th e most important repository of these documents is open to public
consultation at the National Security Archive, www.nsarchive.gwu.edu.
5. Th e name evokes a historical process of country organization during the middle of the
nineteenth century known as the “National Organization Process.” In aim to estab-
lish historical continuities, the military now claimed that they were “reorganizing” the
nation.
6. Hershberg and Agüero (2005) off er an analysis of what the military in each country ini-
tially envisioned as their mission, and of the subsequent transformations in the meanings
they themselves attributed to their repressive practices.
7. In the case of Paraguay, the lengthy Stroessner dictatorship was never touted as a savior
of the nation. Its power was centered on brute force and repression, eliminating the need
for symbolic legitimation. Since its transition in 1989, Paraguay’s political trajectory has
been unstable, characterized by confl icting attempts at “business as usual” (on the fi gure
of Stroessner in commemorations, see González 2002a), attempts at institutional consol-
idation (the constitutional reform of 1992, for example), and eff orts to hold accountable
those who committed human rights violations under the dictatorship, especially after the
discovery of the “Terror Archive” in 1992 (González 2002b).
8. Comisión de verdad (truth commission) and justicia transicional (transitional justice),
both common terms by the end of the twentieth century, were not part of conventional
vocabulary at the beginning of the 1980s when the Argentine transition occurred. Social
and political actors there experimented with institutional tactics and forms as they went
along, without knowing how useful they would prove. As the years passed, many of these
tactics became institutional tools recognized internationally as ways to manage the past
in the aftermath of dictatorship and violence. In 1983, when Argentina established its
commission to investigate disappearances, nothing of the sort had ever existed (Sikkink
2008).
9. Th is expansion can be seen, for example, in the transformations of the groups participat-
ing in the commemorations of the coups on 11 September in Chile (Candina Palomer
2002) and 24 March in Argentina (Lorenz 2002).
10. Held in Montevideo, the March of Silence commemorates the murder of Uruguayan
legislators Zelmar Michelini and Héctor Gutiérrez Ruiz, and Tupamaro activists Rosario
Barredo and William Whitelaw, on 20 May 1976 in Buenos Aires.
11. Th e “Gelman case” was the process of recovering the identity of Argentine poet Juan
Gelman’s granddaughter, who was born in captivity in Uruguay. Th e international cam-
paign demanding a response from the Uruguayan government bore fruit when President
Jorge Batlle took offi ce, agreed to meet with Juan Gelman, and participated in the
announcement of the discovery and restitution of the identity of Macarena Gelman.

Perspectives on the Past • 35
12. In 2005, the executive branch signed a cooperation agreement with the Universidad de
la República to carry out a historical investigation about the detained-disappeared. Th e
result was a four-volume report, based on an exhaustive review of archives and other
sources (Rico 2010).
13. With regards to the case of Germany, Olick makes a similar argument: “Here I can only
say that this is what normalization of the German memory means to me: recognition that
the debate is continuous, that there are no fi nal lines, zero-hours, or caesuras in history
or in memory, only continual reassessments. Th ese reassessments are beholden to past
assessments as well. We cannot reassess the past without reassessing our past assessments,
nor can we reassess past assessments without reassessing the past” (Olick 2003: 285).

Figure 2.1. Latin American social science books of the 1960s and 1970s. Photograph by
Agustina Triquell. Published with permission.

Chapter 2
BUILDING A RESEARCH FIELD
Memory and Gender in Latin American Social Sciences
8
Every few years, the idea arises in academia to take stock, to look at developments
of a theme or fi eld of study during a few decades to observe what has happened
and how ideas and questions have been transformed. Th ese exercises are usually
carried out by older scholars who have been in the intellectual fi eld for quite some
time and who tend to feel that later generations are not fully aware of what was
done before them, as younger people tend to defi ne “the new” as that which is new
to them. Th ese exercises also serve to identify gaps, especially when comparing the
research carried out in one place or region with other places, other traditions,
other protagonists.
In 2002, my colleague Charles Wood of the University of Florida had the idea
of putting together one of these state-of-the-art projects: the turn of the century
seemed like an auspicious occasion to review what had happened with the concep-
tualizations and analyses in the social sciences in and about Latin America. To do
this, he summoned a group of colleagues—almost all of whom worked in North
American institutions, some in Latin America—and commissioned each one
to analyze a specifi c fi eld or topic: population, urbanization, citizenship, social
movements, family, state, etc. When he reached out to me, I realized that three
areas that were central to my research (social movements, family, citizenship) were
no longer available, as they had already been assigned to other colleagues. Chuck
was adamant about having me participate in the project, and thus we had to fi nd
a topic. What could I talk about? As I was already involved in the investigation
and training of young people in the fi eld of memories of dictatorship, we decided
that I would work on this issue—on the emergence of the concern for memories

38 • Th e Struggle for the Past
and human rights in the region. Th e book was published (Wood and Roberts
2005), and my chapter was included in it (Jelin 2005a). It had also been pub-
lished in Spanish the year before (Jelin 2004).
In 2006, I was invited as a keynote speaker at the National Congress of
Sociology in Colombia. Like my Colombian feminist colleagues and friends, I
thought that this was a good opportunity to put gender issues in the spotlight of the
discipline as a whole, bringing forward topics that were generally relegated to the
academic spaces of feminist women. As I usually panic in front of the empty sheet
(or screen), I decided to review my writings about the emergence of the fi eld of
studies on memory and human rights instead of starting from scratch, wondering
if there were parallels with the history of the incorporation of a gender perspective
in Latin American thought. What I discovered in that inquiry was that rather
than parallel developments, the two themes are part of the same process. Of course,
this assessment is permeated by my own subjectivity, since I was and am part of
both processes, as these issues are linked and interwoven in my trajectory. Th e text
for that presentation more than a decade ago, extensively revised now, refl ects that
unique yet two-sided process.
In a sense, the genealogy and the development of these subjects represent a jour-
ney through my own intellectual biography. I explicitly chose to present them in
a more impersonal way, as point and counterpoint of ideas and paradigms rather
than as an autobiographical text. An alternative route would have been to focus
on the names and networks of colleagues, face-to-face encounters and debates,
challenges, and feelings that were present at diff erent times, as well as the various
routes taken over the years. Th e autobiographical element is undoubtedly present,
as in the long run my sense is that all writing is autobiographical to an extent.
Should this be disclosed? Or should it be covered by the mantle of scientifi city
and objectivity? Moving beyond my own biography, this case may present a key
question regarding the history of social ideas in the region: what are the links—or
disconnections—between gender and memory as fi elds of knowledge, regardless of
whether or not these are combined in the concerns of the same person?
***
How did a gender perspective and a concern with human rights and social
memory emerge in the social sciences in Latin America? Did these fi elds
follow independent paths? Or are they part of one and the same intellectual
process? In order to fi nd the historical and analytical anchors for such devel-
opments, I will take an against-the-grain approach to the development of
the social sciences in Latin America since the mid-twentieth century. Both
gender and memories are interdisciplinary fi elds that integrate institutional,
symbolic, and subjective dimensions. One of my hypotheses is that these two
fi elds did not develop independently from one another but are interrelated;

Building a Research Field • 39
another is that the introduction of gender and memory perspectives entailed
a paradigmatic shift that challenged the prevailing perspectives in social
thought in the region. In terms of the academic and intellectual community,
these processes imply a dispute over approaches—a dispute that could well
be analyzed with the set of theoretical and methodological tools provided by
social confl ict theory.
My intention is to trace the conceptual antecedents and the historical con-
ditions for the development of these new perspectives since the 1960s. Th is
does not refer solely—or predominantly— to changes within the academic
fi eld of the social sciences. Instead, as generally occurs with social thought in
the region, it involves processes that have been, and continue to be, tied to
the challenges that sociopolitical transformations pose for the social sciences.
In fact, the origin of these concerns can be traced to their links to social and
political struggles. At the same time, it is important to emphasize that both
the social processes as well as the changes in the conceptualizations of the
social sciences in the region are not isolated or inward-looking. Instead, they
occur within the framework of, and in reciprocal relations with, global aca-
demic and political processes.
Th e 1960s
In the 1960s, as the social sciences were established in Latin America, their
main focus was to understand the challenges posed by the economic and social
development of “peripheral capitalism.” Th e ideas of the United Nations
Economic Commission for Latin America and the Caribbean (ECLAC) were
critical during this period, fi rst in the thinking of Raúl Prebisch, and later
in the notion of dependence (Cardoso and Faletto 1979, among others).
Th e issue of social modernization linked to the processes of industrialization
and urbanization dominated social analysis, including conceptualizations
and empirical studies on marginality, social mobility, and the trickle-down
eff ect. Th ese views coexisted and contrasted with Marxist visions of capital-
ist development, where class confrontation and class struggle were seen as
the key drivers of change. Two issues specifi c to Latin America attracted the
attention of scholars: populism and marginality. Populism was a way of pro-
cessing political modernization that stood in contrast to formal democracy.
Studies on populism would later delve more deeply into the ways in which
charismatic political leaders formed bonds with their social base. At the same
time, the social science debate on marginality focused on whether it was
a temporary, passing situation or if it refl ected a structural phenomenon.1
Marginality and populism were treated as threats or obstacles to either the
success of modernization or capitalist development and class struggle.

40 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Within the framework of these concerns, there was an evident blind
spot regarding relations between the “sexes” and women’s place in society:
“gender,” in fact, was not a term used in that period. If women mattered, it
was in relation to fertility trends. Th ere was a good deal of concern regarding
persistently high fertility rates despite rapid urbanization across the region.
Within the academic community, although women scholars had a visible
presence in the social sciences, the expectation was for them to focus on the
topic of fertility. A few pioneering studies addressed women’s rising participa-
tion in the workforce and their increasing educational attainment, both of
which fi t easily within a narrative of modernization. For its part, high fertil-
ity could also be “explained” by women’s more traditional attitudes or their
religiosity.
However, other developments and other realities were in the making in
the region, linked to global processes and movements. In fact, a wave of
political activism surged in the second half of the 1960s, with new actors
and new demands. Th e Cuban Revolution had had a major impact on left-
ist movements, providing previously unknown perspectives and strategies.
Th ese were days of a booming student movement (in Brazil, for example), the
“new” labor movement, and the vast protests of 1968 (Tlatelolco in Mexico,
the passeata dos cem mil in Rio de Janeiro, the Cordobazo in Argentina).2 It was
a time in which social forces overlooked in the previously dominant models
of analysis—those centered on economic and class structural determina-
tions—suddenly erupted in the public sphere. In the dominant paradigms
at that time, from Marxism to modernization theories, ties to the political
system were interpreted without the mediation of institutions, actors, or
social movements. Yet these were emerging in the sociopolitical scenario,
demanding that social scientists come up with new interpretive frameworks
and conceptualizations to understand these movements from the perspective
of the demand for rights and the struggles for the expansion of citizenship.
During the fi nal years of the decade, a new feminist wave emerged, ini-
tially in the core countries and soon expanding to other parts of the world.
Th e challenge of this fl edgling modern feminism was twofold, as it needed
to understand and explain the forms of women’s subordination and propose
strategies in the struggle for change. What was the nature of this subordina-
tion? How should it be understood? Th e relationship between research and
action was undoubtedly one of the principal concerns.
One of the fi rst milestones in this feminist trajectory was the discovery
of women’s social invisibility: in domestic work, hidden from public view and
not recognized or valued, and as the rearguard of historic struggles, “behind
great men.” Th e recognition of the value of domestic production and the
role of women in the reproduction of social existence would become a key
topic of the 1970s. In Havana, Cuba, Isabel Larguía and John Dumoulin

Building a Research Field • 41
laid out the theoretical framework for understanding these reproductive tasks
from a Marxist perspective (Larguía and Dumoulin 1976). It was necessary
to make visible the invisible. Recognizing and naming grants social existence,
and that visible existence appeared to be a requirement for self-assertion
and revindication. Hence, there was a need to conceptualize and analyze the
everyday, the antiheroic, the social fabric that sustains and reproduces social
life. Intense theoretical debates among Marxist feminists ensued in the core
countries and the region: what do women produce when they work for their
family and household? Who reaps the benefi ts of their work?3 Th e recogni-
tion of the housewife as a worker sparked a political debate as well. Should
housewives be recognized as workers with labor rights? Should they be paid
or receive social security? Or should gender relations be transformed within
the household instead? Studies and explorations on the nature of domestic
work unveiled the invisibility and subordination of women. It seemed that
this awareness opened diff erent paths toward changing their status.
Nonetheless, this debate, a central one to the formation of a gender per-
spective, did not alter mainstream social sciences in the region. Instead, it
can be characterized as a development that remained—or helped to build—a
segregated space, one made up of scholars and militants who began to revin-
dicate feminism and the struggle for women’s rights.
It seemed that, given that women’s subordination was rooted in the dis-
tinction between the public and private spheres, women should emerge from
their households and participate in public life, a predominantly masculine
world to that date. Secular trends—the increase in educational attainment
and in women’s participation in the workforce—showed that this was already
happening. Starting in the 1970s, the percentage of women in the Latin
American workforce spiked (Valdés et al. 1995).
Yet, what happened when women joined the labor market? Reality indi-
cates that they had few opportunities to access “good” jobs and that they
suff ered wage discrimination and occupational segregation. Th is relegated
them to “women’s work,” tasks that reproduce and expand traditional roles,
i.e. domestic work and service occupations such as secretaries, teachers, and
nurses. In short, segregation and discrimination were the rule. Access to the
world of work—and, to a lesser extent, to other forms of participation in
the public sphere—thus promoted a specifi c type of resistance: the struggle
against discrimination and for equality vis-à-vis men.
With this new stage came a novel focus that involved two lines of action.
Th e fi rst entailed searching for recognition of women’s roles and the struggle
to achieve better conditions for the tasks women were assigned under the
traditional gender division of labor. Th e second was transforming these roles
and conditions. It was clear that the gender division of labor was tantamount
to women’s subordination and lack of autonomy and therefore oppressive,

42 • Th e Struggle for the Past
given that women were the “property” of the pater familiae. Th e theoretical
discussion and the practical consequences of the history of the patriarchy—
a concept that allows family relations to be connected with broader social
relations, focusing the attention on power—were a milestone in the social
sciences of the 1960s and 1970s. Women’s liberation entailed a transformation
of patriarchy as a social system (Valdés 1990).
Women leaving home to work or to participate in collective actions and
organizations with other women—especially in popular-sector and marginal
neighborhoods—and women learning to express their needs and demands
(Blondet 1990; Caldeira 1990) seemed to promise a freer future. If the
household-patriarchy was responsible for oppression, eff orts had to be made
to end it. Th e experience of the 1970s and 1980s would show that, though
liberating, such actions could also serve to reinforce subordination. Women
were not paid for their community work, be it at soup kitchens, in coop-
erative attempts at childcare, or in other neighborhood activities, nor did
these represent more autonomy or grant decision-making or management
power (Barrig 1994). Women generally did this work as an extension of their
household tasks. It could thus easily become invisible work, a reproduction
of subordination and even clientelism. On the other hand, then and now,
when poor women engage in paid work, their daily work shift doubles—and
for women involved in community work, triples—a fact that can no way be
read as liberating and generally leads to exhaustion and burnout. What all
this means is poorly paid, unstable work, without social benefi ts, work where
women’s labor rights go unrecognized, and where they suff er segregation and
discrimination.
Th e 1970s: Tragedy and Hope
Th e 1970s witnessed bloody dictatorships in the Southern Cone and, at
the same time, a growing mobilization of women, as seen in the fi rst World
Conference on Women, held in Mexico in 1975. Th e emergence and vis-
ibility of two phenomena, both previously unheard of, brought changes in
many fi elds.
State repression of youthful revolutionary activism, and fi erce political
confrontations in the early 1970s combined with guerrilla warfare in several
countries, culminated in the cycle of dictatorships in the Southern Cone.
Brazil fi rst, followed by Uruguay and Chile, and fi nally Argentina, joined
the more traditional dictatorships like that of Stroessner, Paraguay’s president
from 1954 to 1989. State terrorism and illegal repression in the region cap-
tured the world’s attention and fostered meaningful changes in the models
and frameworks utilized to interpret what was happening.

Building a Research Field • 43
Although there were global precedents, it was as a result of the mili-
tary coups of the Southern Cone that international networks of activists
and human rights organizations involved themselves in the protests against
state repression and state terrorism, as noted by Keck and Sikkink (1998).4
Furthermore, in the 1970s, dictatorial repression led women out of their
households, and right from the onset, women were among the leaders of
the human rights movement. Th e commitment of many of these women
did not stem from ideological convictions or from strategic political cal-
culations in the struggle against dictatorship: it was emotional. Th ese were
women directly aff ected by repression—mothers, grandmothers, relatives of
victims—demanding and asking about relatives who had been jailed, dis-
appeared, tortured, or murdered. Th e names of these women’s organiza-
tions, with words like madres, abuelas, viudas, comadres, familiares (mothers,
grandmothers, widows, godmothers, relatives) indicate the signifi cance of
their family ties. Having lost their fear, these women were willing to take
any risk to achieve a goal that was more private and personal than public or
political, at least initially: to get information about—and recover—a son or
daughter. Seemingly, there was nothing heroic about this early stage of the
movement. It was an amplifi ed dramatization of woman’s role as a loving,
dedicated family caretaker. What happened afterward was another chapter of
this history.
Th e women who left their homes in search of information about their
family members were driven by personal tragedy. Th ough by now famil-
iar, these stories are still heartbreaking, and weave a common thread: des-
peration and confusion, the search for help, the eff orts to build contacts
to avoid losing hope, the meeting and mutual understanding with other
women in similar situations, the engagement with activists from the human
rights movement, the building of a trajectory of struggle. And little by little,
these private demands centered on sons and daughters transformed into a
public and political demand for democracy (among others, Schirmer 1988;
Valdés and Weinstein 1993). Th us, it was women who organized and led the
amnesty movement in Brazil in 1978 and women who were central to the
human rights movement in Argentina.
At the practical level of the struggle against dictatorship, the nascent para-
digm of human rights coincided with the presence of women. Yet wom-
en’s involvement in the human rights movement was an expression not of
demands for women’s equality but of more traditional familism and mater-
nalism, a topic we return to in chapter 5. Th ere was a signifi cant distance
between activists in the human rights movement and feminists, leading to
some misunderstandings and disagreement.5 In fact, the expectation among
feminists was that, as women, human rights activists would also spontane-
ously express the demands of women’s rights. Although there were some

44 • Th e Struggle for the Past
connecting threads, this insistence hindered the dialogue between the human
rights movement and the nascent feminist movement.
In terms of international ties, communication between women of the
North and South, at the Mexico conference in 1975 and later on, led many
feminists from the North to understand that their analyses, needs, and
demands could not be extrapolated to the rest of the world. Th ey learned
that even among women, otherness exists; that there are other women in dif-
ferent, and especially diffi cult, situations. Victims of human rights violations
and their relatives were privileged recipients of female solidarity and assis-
tance. Th is explains the interest that women in the human rights movement
sparked within the international feminist movement. Th e Mothers of the
Plaza de Mayo became a symbol and an emblem: women who, from pain,
suff ering, and their traditional roles as mothers, subverted the social and
political order and thus revealed the transformational potential of women.
From a feminist perspective, the next question was whether this public
expression of private pain could transform mothers into women conscious of
gender demands, and whether this would predispose them to fi ght for them.
Although there are no systematic investigations, evidence indicates that
human rights activists have the same range of ideological positions as the
general population of women. Th ey are as feminist—or as un-feminist—as
the women around them.
Th e 1980s: Democracy, Citizenship, and Social Movements
In 1978, the Latin American Council of Social Sciences (Consejo Latino-
americano de Ciencias Sociales, or CLACSO) organized a seminar in Costa
Rica on the “Social Conditions of Democracy.” Th ere was clearly an urgent
need for scholars and intellectuals in the region to refl ect on exit paths and
strategies to bring dictatorial regimes to an end. Truth be told, it was a
foundational moment for political science in the region: the very notion of
democracy was at stake (Ansaldi et al. 1985).6
Th e concept of democracy was the heart of academic debate; it was also
the guiding idea of the political project opposed to authoritarianism and dic-
tatorships. Centered on the political regime, this line of thought converged
with the international mood, as the human rights discourse was becom-
ing hegemonic. Th e long-awaited democracy incorporated the defense of
people’s basic rights as a core focus, privileging the political system and
relegating the analysis of economic and social conditions. From then on, the
emphasis was on the “transition to democracy” and its “consolidation,” with
debates centering on the diff erences between formal democracy and substan-
tive democracy (Lesgart 2003). For our topic and purpose here, what matters

Building a Research Field • 45
is that the references to human rights and the insistence on social participa-
tion led the question of citizenship to emerge, though political scientists paid
it little attention (Lechner 1988; Jelin 1996).
Toward the beginning of the 1980s, the fi eld of political science delved
into the transformations in state institutions, as well as democratic forms
of participation and of exercising power. At the same time, and though it
was not initially linked to this focus, a new fi eld of academic interest and
analysis caught the attention of researchers: forms of social protest, social
movements, and instances of collective action outside the political system
and existing institutional channels. Th e source of this interest was new social
actors (youth, urban, and ethnic movements; women’s and human rights
movements) and new collective practices among both these nascent move-
ments and other established ones (workers, peasants) that had become visible
during the 1970s and 1980s. New and old, all these movements had more
localized, specifi c demands and goals. Th us, at the World Conference on
Women held in Mexico in 1975, the big surprise was the activism and social
fervor among women at the parallel forums and conferences. Since then,
these parallel activities—organized by social movements through their NGO
networks—have become a mainstay of major international conferences (on
population, the environment, women, human rights, social development,
etc.) and later at the World Social Forum.7 In fact, since the mass mobiliza-
tions of the 1970s, the presence of social movements in the public sphere has
held steady in the region.8
With the transition processes of the 1980s and the institutionalization
of democratic mechanisms by local governments, the place of social move-
ments in sociopolitical dynamics became more explicit and visible. Many
urban movements were incorporated as legitimate, social actors recognized
by local governments. At the same time, the social and political agenda of the
transitions incorporated several demands of the women’s movement and the
human rights movement. Since then, the social critique of feminism gradu-
ally began to penetrate corporate organizations, trade unions, business orga-
nizations, the state, and even some sectors of the Catholic Church. Th e issues
that were raised in the 1980s—discrimination against women, demands for
equal opportunity policies (and years later, gender quotas and the demand
for gender parity in the distribution of political posts), transformations in
the legal structure—gradually expanded into the social fabric and the public
sphere. Over time, certain specifi c violations of women’s rights gained social
and political recognition, including domestic and gender violence, human
traffi cking for prostitution, and, in the twenty-fi rst century, femicide.9 Th e
recognition of sexual and reproductive rights has increased in the region, and
public debates in several countries are now centered on the question of legal-
izing abortion. At the same time, a wide range of social sectors appropriated

46 • Th e Struggle for the Past
the human rights discourse, thus expanding it beyond militant groups with
demands related to the recent dictatorships.
Th is expansion of topics draws into question what constitutes the success
or failure of a social movement. Th ough the human rights movement could
have weakened or even split apart after transition, as occurred in Argentina
during the fi rst half of the 1990s—as we will see in chapter 3—society at
large came to embrace its topics and concerns. Th e defense of human rights,
the recognition of gender subordination, and the urgent need to address both
were incorporated into a broader interpretive framework of democracy. Th is
can be taken as an indicator of success: society as a whole appropriated these
fundamental issues, though organizations that addressed them specifi cally
were weakened along the way.
In a mid or longer-term perspective, the profi le of social demands voiced
by collective movements has gradually changed (Calderón and Jelin 1987).
It is important to note that at their peak, the worker and peasant move-
ments had projects for “total” social transformation. Th ings have changed
enormously since the turn of the twentieth century, when European mili-
tants and union organizers were coming to the Americas with the idea of
instilling “worker consciousness” and internationalism. Th eir eff ective strat-
egy involved getting jobs as workers at new local factories in order to make
their arguments to workers face-to-face and distribute anarchist and socialist
publications. During this period, European immigrants played an extremely
signifi cant role in worker organizations and trade union presses in both
Argentina and Brazil. Most were Italian, though there were also Spaniards,
Portuguese, and French (Fausto 1976; Godio 1972; Oved 1978). In the fol-
lowing decades, worker struggles combined specifi c demands with projects
for transformation at the national scale.
Starting in the 1970s, as the drawbacks to import substitution industri-
alization became evident and authoritarian regimes expanded, the sphere
of action of social movements—and the attention they received from aca-
demia—began to change. Until then, the social sciences in Latin America
had been concerned with issues related to power and the political will for
political transformation. From this perspective, collective expressions by
popular sectors outside institutional channels were interpreted mostly as
pre-political protests or as the embryos of popular participation, waiting
to be channeled through political parties. Now, “discontent with politics”
was becoming visible, and researchers began examining the inside of these
movements to discover new ways of doing politics (Lechner 1982). In these
circumstances, however, the main issue was still power, and power continued
to be the interpretive perspective.
Th e paradigm shift in the analysis of the social movements occurred when
it became possible to see them not only as new ways of doing politics but

Building a Research Field • 47
also as new forms of sociability and new patterns of social organization (Evers
1985). Researchers had to pay more attention to the microsocial processes of
reciprocal recognition and the construction of new collective subjects due to
the presence of multiple—and highly diverse—social actors. Th ese collective
identities implied both recognizing one’s belonging to a group while also
diff erentiating oneself and one’s group from others. Th is also implied rede-
fi ning the borders between the public and private spheres. Social processes
had to be observed not only from the vantage point of major political events
or structural economic processes but also in their everyday expressions, in
the social relations of daily life. What mattered was that the most specifi c,
concrete aspects of banal, everyday routines often led people to challenge the
basic principles of social organization (Calderón 1986; Escobar and Alvárez
1992). In their public presence and in their practices, these diverse, heteroge-
neous movements and actors combined the logics of asserting their collective
identity on the symbolic level with their specifi c interests and demands (Jelin
1985).
Th e analysis of social movements in the region and refl ections on how
they came to be were not isolated from conceptual developments in the social
sciences of the core countries. European thought about “new” social move-
ments was doubtlessly important. As mentioned above, what characterized
the Latin American analysis was precisely the fact that these highly visible
protagonists in the public sphere proposed new ways of doing politics and
new forms of sociability and subjectivity. Th ey were also expressing new rela-
tionships between the political and the social, and between the public sphere
and private life. Everyday social practices had to be taken into account along
with ideological and institutional-political practices. As in many other cases
in which new questions are posed, it was necessary to fi gure out whether
this was, in fact, something new, or whether the dominant paradigms in
the social sciences up to that time—which emphasized economic processes
and the political system—had simply created a blind spot. And in this new-
found appreciation and research into everyday practices, in this liminal space
between public and private, some of the concepts that originated in feminist
thought took hold: the shifting nature of the distinction between public and
private, the power of microsocial relations, and the construction of subjectiv-
ity (Jelin 1990).
Th ese were the years of the progressive liberalization and the transitions of
the Southern Cone dictatorships, of demands for democratization in Mexico,
and of the search for ways to end political violence in Central America.
Political science focused on the performance of the political system and its
institutions (congress, political parties, state reforms, public policies). Th is
left a niche for researchers interested in the multiplicity of social actors
and in cultural and spatial diversity, a niche to be taken up especially by

48 • Th e Struggle for the Past
anthropologists and sociologists who were ready to examine processes “from
below.”
In this perspective “from below,” which was connected to the incipient
processes of democratization, citizenship—an uncommon term in social sci-
ence literature until that point—became a core concept.10 After transition,
the question about the historical expansion (and also reversal) of diff erent
types of rights turned critical. In contrast to the model presented by Marshall
(1964) for England, in Latin America the expansion of the welfare state and
social rights in the postwar period had not always been preceded or accom-
panied by an expansion of civil and political rights, let alone by a subjec-
tive sense of rights and citizenship. In fact, the formal recognition of social
and labor rights in the region did not always follow the recognition of civic
and political rights (Collier and Collier 1991). At the same time, though
political rights were recovered in the 1980s transitions, extensive violations
of civil rights also characterized this period, as Caldeira has shown for Brazil
(Caldeira 1996).
A brief conceptual detour is necessary here, so as to clarify what notion
of citizenship was under construction. Whenever a concrete, specifi c right is
invoked in the discussion of citizenship rights, the positivization of natural
rights is a potential danger (Habermas 1991). Th e risk is that the concept will
be objectifi ed and citizenship rights will be identifi ed with a set of observ-
able practices: voting in elections, exercising freedom of expression, receiving
public benefi ts (healthcare or education), or any other concrete practice.
Although such practices drive the struggles to expand rights in specifi c his-
torical situations, the concept of citizenship is necessarily more abstract.
From an analytical perspective, it refers to a confl ictive practice connected to
power, one that refl ects struggles about who can say what in the process of
defi ning shared problems and how these problems should be addressed (Van
Gunsteren 1978). In short, both citizenship and rights are in a constant pro-
cess of construction and reformulation.
Th is perspective rests on the premise that the basic right is “the right to
have rights” (Arendt 1973; Lefort 1987). In this regard, two qualities—self-
maintenance and expansion—are key to the conception of citizen action:
“Th e actions of citizens are those that tend to maintain, and where possible
to enhance, the future exercise of citizenship” (Van Gunsteren 1978: 27;
Lechner 1986b). Th e content of the revindications, the political priorities,
and the spheres of the struggle can be quite varied, yet in terms of building
citizenship, all have similar consequences as long as the “right to have rights”
and the right to publicly debate norms and laws are reaffi rmed.
In this approach, which was being developed in Latin America in the
1980s, the notions of human rights became fundamental along with the
consideration of subjectivity and the processes of building subjects of rights

Building a Research Field • 49
(individual but also collective, an especially powerful demand among indig-
enous groups). Th e debate in the region centered on the dilemmas and ten-
sions between individual and collective rights; between equality and the right
to diff erence; between universal rights and cultural pluralism; and between
the state’s public responsibility and the defense of privacy and intimacy
(Jelin and Hershberg 1996; Jelin 1993). Once again, feminist debates had an
important infl uence here. In Chile, for example, the slogan of the women’s
movement that opposed dictatorship was Democracia en el país, en la casa
y en la cama (Democracy in the country, at home and in bed), as Julieta
Kirkwood describes in academic and activist terms (Kirkwood 2010 [1982]).
Empirical research on the topic emphasized the distance between legally
recognized citizenship rights, on the one hand, and state and social practices
that point more to relations of dependency than to an awareness of citizen
autonomy or subjectivity on the other (Jelin 1996; Auyero 2001). Th e con-
cept of empowerment, initially brought forth in feminist thinking, is also a
signifi cant part of this approach. Empowerment refers to greater individual
and collective autonomy, to the capacity to mobilize collectively, and to
the struggle to transform structures of discrimination and inequality (León
1997). In more general terms, the social sciences in the region gradually came
to focus on the paradoxes, tensions, contradictions, and gray areas in the
analysis of social realities. It was a slow and challenging incorporation of an
approach initially developed in feminist thought, as the social sciences had
previously been so forcefully dominated by structuralist and deterministic
approaches.
Th e 1990s: Neoliberalism and Beyond
During the 1970s and 1980s, the social sciences in the region developed new
topics and interpretive frameworks for analyzing the public sphere, the relation-
ship between state and society, and the mechanisms and articulations between
material conditions, institutions, subjectivity, and the symbolic-cultural level.
Th ese concerns arose from political preoccupations with democracy, the devel-
opment of new social movements and their focus on everyday life, the thinking
about citizenship, and the constitution of citizenship as a subjective identity,
a concept also nurtured by the struggles of human rights movements. Th ey
were additionally the result of converging analytical perspectives: feminism and
gender relations, the human rights paradigm, the perspective brought about by
social movements, the social construction of citizenship, and subjectivity.
In the 1990s, the convergence of feminist thought and the conceptualiza-
tion of the struggles for rights took place at a specifi c historical juncture: the
upswing of neoliberalism. In this context, it became necessary to address the

50 • Th e Struggle for the Past
peculiar and evolving articulation of societal actors and the state. An analysis
of Brazil that could easily be applied to other countries is particularly relevant
in this regard. Dagnino (2004) posited the idea of a “perverse convergence”
of the democratizing and participative impulses during the transitions after
dictatorship, and the imperatives of the neoliberal mandates of a minimalist
state that prevailed in the 1990s, with the backing of multilateral aid agen-
cies. Under both proposals, civil society must be active and assertive. What
is perverse about this convergence is that while some tried to encourage
citizen participation in the construction of democracy, others were working
to shrink the state and transfer the oversight of social and public aff airs to
“civil society.” Both political undertakings (democratization and neoliberal-
ism) employed the same terminology: participation, civil society, citizenship,
democracy. Yet their meanings and intentions were entirely diff erent.
In fact, neoliberalism had its own interpretation of human rights, citizen-
ship, and feminism. Some of the most outstanding features of this period
and ideology included limiting state action and leaving social issues to be
resolved by civil society; a strong belief in free market; and fostering diver-
sity, as expressed by multiculturalism. Th e concern over subjectivity was
well suited to ideologies focused on individualism. Undoubtedly, there was
a powerful impetus in this direction in several countries of the region in the
1990s. Yet there was also resistance to this ideological attack, a reassessment
of approaches, and a search for new alternatives. Th e emphasis began to shift
toward a new set of issues and concerns, such as the recognition of the col-
lective rights of indigenous people in constitutional amendments passed in
several Latin American countries over the course of the decade (Gargarella
1997).
In academic and intellectual circles, the reaction against neoliberal hege-
mony—which proposed standardized modus operandi that overlooked
contexts, specifi c histories, and cultural heritage—took shape in analytical
approaches that included the historicity of social phenomena: the past, pres-
ent, and future as a combination of past experiences and horizons of expec-
tation, to borrow Koselleck’s words (2004). In more concrete terms, the
subjective formation of citizenship and rights, along with the individual and
collective dynamic of social demands, required going beyond the conjunc-
ture to encourage visions that combined multiple temporalities. Looking
toward the future, the challenge was to think about how to build democracy
and equality, be it in the formal working of institutions, the substantive
content, or the processes of social empowerment. In terms of the past, it was
about fi nding ways to settle accounts with social structures and mechanisms
of domination that discriminated and excluded signifi cant parts of the popu-
lation, and with the terrorist, repressive states of recent history. Th e connec-
tion between the two was the strong belief in the idea that no future could be

Building a Research Field • 51
built if past crimes went unpunished. Th is is the point where the question of
social memories, the core of this book, emerges.
Th e concern over memories came up in the Southern Cone during the
democratic transitions of the 1980s, when societal actors felt the urgent need
to confront their very recent past. Th is need could be felt in the institutional
and political fi eld, in social movements, and among victims and survivors.
Th e usual tools of political democratic institutions did not appear to suffi ce.
Th e suff ering and “private” pain exceeded the intimate realm and spilled out
onto the street. A few years later, some of us in academia began explorations
and interventions to open up the fi eld of research and debate on memories.
At that time, the guiding idea was to intervene in the intellectual fi eld across
the region, in order to advance topics and questions for research, refl ection,
and citizenship action. Th e challenge was to develop a conceptual model and
engage in rigorous research on processes that had taken place very recently
and those still underway. For that purpose, it was also necessary to train
young researchers on these topics and help build networks and working
groups in this fi eld—a fi eld we then loosely referred to as “memory studies.”11
Th is was a time when memorialization of the recent past was emerging in the
public sphere of the countries in the region in a variety of ways. Given this
societal eff ervescence, it was impossible for engaged intellectuals to ignore
these developments: the social role of memories beckoned.
One additional consideration links the study of memories to feminism: the
recognition of ambiguities, tensions, and contradictions in the social milieu.
In reference to life in Nazi concentration camps, Primo Levi referred to a
moral “gray zone” where there are no unequivocal, explicit, absolute, or cate-
gorical criteria. One cannot delve into memories without acknowledging this.
In turn, contemporary feminist thought addresses the dilemmas and para-
doxes that are at the core of gender relations. Th e fact that women demand
equal rights based on their gender identity creates an unresolvable paradox,
one that comes back time and again in the history of social struggles (Scott
1996). As Fraser notes, contemporary political visions put forward policies of
redistribution and recognition as mutually exclusive, while diff erent combina-
tions of these principles are enabled by feminist thought, which acknowledges
ambiguity, contradiction, and paradox. Rooted in contemporary philosophy
and feminist historiography (Fraser 1997; Scott 1996), this perspective takes
shape in Latin America in the analysis of other tensions arising from demands
of equality and diff erence, such as those anchored in race and ethnicity. Th ese
spaces of debate and construction—on temporality, memory, and the para-
doxes of equality and diff erence—allow other historical periods, such as the
Spanish Conquest and the genocide of the indigenous people, to be incorpo-
rated along with other actors who have traditionally been silenced and hidden
in the “offi cial histories” written by the winners of historical battles.

52 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Th e Turn of the Century: New and Old Challenges
At this point, this account should continue by presenting the main developments
in the twenty-fi rst century. Th is period is full of activities, events, and ideas
expressed by multiple actors in many diff erent scenarios. Th ere are contributions
in conceptual and academic terms. Th ere are social mobilizations of old and new
actors. Th ere are also innovative approaches in the fi eld of memory, in gender
perspectives, and in the interrelations among them. However, I fi nd it impos-
sible to address them at this point in this overview of the fi eld. Th e themes and
the questions analyzed in the rest of the book are at the core of these convergences
and tensions. In fact, the following chapters refl ect these concerns: the disruption
of longstanding silences that opens up new or sometimes forgotten interpretations;
the new questions that challenge what used to be perceived as certainties; and the
challenges raised by new actors in other institutions and places. Given that these
concerns are present throughout the book and guide its development, this initial
chapter must necessarily remain somewhat incomplete and open.
Notes
A previous version of this chapter was published in 2019 as “Th e Foundations of a New Issue:
Gender, Human Rights and Memory,” in Key Texts for Latin American Sociology, ed. Fernanda
Beigel (London: Sage), 89–100.
1. Th is can be seen, for example, in the debate between Nun and Cardoso, reproduced in
Nun (2001).
2. A discussion of these events in Latin America, the regional and global context when they
occurred, and their memories over time can be found in Jelin (2018). Th is text was part
of a broader initiative to mark the fi ftieth anniversary of the 1968 mobilizations around
the world, centered on questions about their memories, legacies, and impacts (Della
Porta 2018).
3. An analysis of the way in which the gender dimension was incorporated into class analy-
sis during that period can be found in Jelin (2017b).
4. Although human rights violations have existed always—especially if we consider the
prevalence of violence against women and the limits on their freedom—the human rights
movement emerges and receives its name as part of the widespread violations committed
by agents of the state during Southern Cone dictatorships in the 1970s. Earlier viola-
tions, including the silent, everyday violence against women and the genocides of indig-
enous people in Central America, especially Guatemala, rarely attracted international
attention.
5. Something analogous occurred among women in poor neighborhoods: they entered the
public space with demands related to their family responsibilities but did not frame them
in a feminist or gender-based paradigm.

Building a Research Field • 53
6. For a systematic presentation of the development of political thought on democracy
in the 1980s in Latin America, see Lesgart (2003). As the author notes, the interaction
and debate among political intellectuals in exile, especially in Mexico, was crucial in this
development.
7. During the 2010s, they erupted again during the Arab Spring and in protests against
globalized capitalism in both Europe and North America, from Seattle and Athens all the
way to Wall Street.
8. At the time, several major research projects on these movements were underway. One
comparative analysis at the regional level that deserves mention is the program on
popular participation sponsored by the United Nations Research Institute for Social
Development (UNRISD) (on the women’s movement, see Jelin 1990) and the research
program carried out by the United Nations University and CLACSO (Calderón 1986).
9. Femicide refers to the gender-motivated murder of women and girls, often in the context
of domestic violence or sex crimes.
10. As a starting point for the historical revision of the notion of citizenship in Latin America,
Bryan Roberts notes a drastic rise in the number of publications on the topic. An inven-
tory of the Benson Latin American Collection at the University of Texas at Austin (per-
haps the most important Latin American library in the world) reveals only three titles
in Spanish and Portuguese with the keyword “citizenship” between 1966 and 1973.
Between 1993 and 2000, a total of 139 titles included that keyword, most of which were
studies on Brazil (Roberts 2005: 137).
11. Th e program, organized and sponsored by the Latin America committee of the Social
Science Research Council, was called “Collective Memory and Repression: Comparative
Views on Democratization in Latin America’s Southern Cone.” Th e title suggested
our approach to the topic at that time, which would later be revisited and revised.
Nearly sixty scholars from six countries—Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Paraguay, Peru, and
Uruguay—received training in the program, which lasted from 1998 until 2005. One of
the outcomes was the “Memorias de Represión” series, which consisted of twelve books.
A critical analysis of the program from an epistemological perspective is provided by
Cruz (2016).

Figure 3.1. Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo in a rally at the dawn of the transition. Photograph

Chapter 3
EXPLORATIONS, CERTAINTIES,
AND UNCERTAINTIES
Th e Human Rights Movement and
Building Democracy in Argentina
8
Temporal distance alters memories. It erases some and highlights others—often
those that are reinforced by images, photographs, and movies. Th e process of
reviewing past moments that one has lived through but also studied entails
making discoveries, facing new enigmas, and having to change meanings and
beliefs. Although some facts may be known, a new visit implies paying atten-
tion to details that did not seem important before and that now take on new
signifi cance. Critically rereading interpretations made twenty or more years ago
invites one to review and put into perspective the multiple layers of historicity of
the events themselves, social memories, one’s own memory, and the shifts in the
interpretive frameworks.
Many have told and analyzed the history of the Argentine human rights move-
ment in dictatorship and in the transition. Th ere has been a succession of testi-
monies; descriptions; documentary analyses; diverse interpretations in political,
subjective, or symbolic keys; rereadings based on the discovery of new documents
or in the uncovering of new data. Th e enigma of how the actors lived and under-
stood their circumstances, however, remains and is recreated time and again. It
is relatively easy to make ex post (anachronistic) history, attributing motivations
and meanings based on what was known later—a knowledge based on docu-
ments, memories and testimonies of protagonists, and historical investigations of
specialists. Here my intent is to recreate the historical moment when the actions

56 • Th e Struggle for the Past
took place so as to uncover the perplexities and beliefs of the actors themselves at
the moment they took action while trying to make sense of what was happening.
What did the protagonists of the internally heterogeneous and diverse human
rights movement know at each moment when actions had to be taken? What did
they believe? How did they feel?
One of the key issues in research on social movements is to investigate how actors
take—or fail to take—advantage of the political opportunities they encounter in
their confrontation with institutions and their engagement with other actors.
However, instead of facing or confronting a given set of externally determined
political opportunities, I found that the Argentine human rights movement was
in itself the most signifi cant actor in generating and building such new political
opportunities at a crucial moment in history.
Given my own civic-political commitment and my intellectual and academic
interests, I have been close to what came to be known as the “human rights move-
ment” in Argentina since its initial moments toward the end of the dictatorship.
I studied its development over time, fi rst examining it from a framework of
analysis of new social movements, then framing it in the struggles for citizenship
and human rights, and fi nally focusing my attention on issues of memory. Th is
last step was in response to the movement itself as its actions in the public sphere
evolved into an active and militant practice anchored in the (changing) senses of
the past. Th e layers of interpretation were overlapping and evolving, while the
pace of action was accelerating and the sociopolitical stage was shifting. Th is pat-
tern explains the perhaps unconventional way of presenting the interpretations in
this volume through a reference to the historicity of the interpretation itself.
An example of one such shift in interpretation was that during dictatorship and
the transition I took for granted and never questioned the name of the movement.
It was unquestionably the human rights movement. Years later, I started using
quotation marks and referring to the “human rights movement,” or prefacing
the reference with a “so-called” human rights movement, indicating the distance
between the way the actors referred to themselves—in anthropological jargon, the
“native” perspective—and analytical conceptualizations in which human rights
had a specifi c meaning, diff erent and somewhat distant from native talk. A signifi -
cant question lies behind this seemingly minor shift, namely the scope and content
of the rights that the movement is addressing at each time and place.
Th e following text has a long history. In the 1980s, research on the human
rights movement was part of the study of social movements in the Argentine
transition (Jelin 1985; Jelin 1987). A few years later, the human rights move-
ment was the focus of my attention as part of a collective research project on the
place of the trial of the former commanders in the early years of the constitutional
government (Acuña et al. 1995). Th e text published then (Jelin 1995) was
updated, summarized, and reissued several years later in a collective volume on
recent Argentine history (Jelin 2005b). Almost a decade later, in connection with

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 57
another collective project on the initial moments of the transition, the section of
the text dealing with that period was recovered and, with a new specifi c investiga-
tion of the actions of the human rights movement at that time, was incorporated
into another collective volume (Feld and Franco 2015; Jelin 2015). And it was
revised, rewritten in part, and reorganized once again for this book.
Th e result of these layers of revisions is a text that does not maintain a single
narrative genre throughout, nor does it incorporate the same density of informa-
tion and sources in its diff erent sections. Th e fi rst sections on the formation of the
human rights movement during the dictatorship and its actions during the years
of the transition are the product of many years of research with primary sources
and participation in the movement. Th e intent was to relay the dilemmas and
uncertainties of its actors and the shift in scenarios in the best way possible. Th e
contrast with the subsequent sections dealing with the events of the 1990s is clear
and visible, as only the main events of the later period are summarized, without
detailing the vicissitudes and subjectivities of the protagonists of the story. Th e
varying depth of analysis should be clear to the reader, and I am both unable and
unwilling to hide or mask this contrast.
I can put forth some explanations for this lack of balance, all of which are
necessarily and unavoidably subjective. Th e easiest option is to say (as clarifi ed
at the end of chapter 2) that the following chapters will deal with more recent
and current events. However, the reality is that the proliferation of events, com-
memorations, vehicles, and places of memory makes an in-depth monitoring of
them almost impossible. To provide an example: at the time of transition there
was only a single trial, that of the former commanders. How many were there in
the 2010s? Plenty of research, publications, studies, and stories in various media
(cinema, television, social networks, books, articles, blogs, and others) exist on
this subject. Th us, it becomes harder to carve out a space and say anything truly
new, which in turn creates a fear of repetition. Lastly, it is possible to argue that
temporal proximity (and a combination of events and circumstances) makes ana-
lytical distance diffi cult and therefore hinders the task of synthesis. Th ese issues
are ubiquitous in the research on social movement: historicity and temporality
reign—there is no end to this story.
***
In the context of the political violence that shook Argentina in the mid-
1970s—violence that culminated in the coup d’état in 1976 and massive,
systematic violations of human rights under the military regime—a new
collective actor started to take shape: the human rights movement.1 Th ough
almost invisible at the outset, mobilization against state repression gradually
attained visibility and a signifi cant political role, occupying a central place in
the public sphere in Argentina.

58 • Th e Struggle for the Past
During the military dictatorship (1976–83), the movement developed
a modus operandi based on open denunciations and protests, support for
victims and their family members, dissemination of information on the
scope of the violations, and eff orts to foster international solidarity. Until
the mid-1980s, the movement was guided by the urgent need to bring to
light and share information about the nature of the massive and systematic
violations—the demand for “truth”—and the insistence on punishment
for the guilty—the demand for “justice.” Since then, new demands have
been added as the principal rallying points of the movement, vindicating
the memory of what happened—now collective and historical, not merely
individual—and fi ghting against oblivion. Th ere were others rallying points
as well. While the quest for justice and punishment remained at the core
of the movement, the agenda of human rights also expanded. Th is agenda
included a forward-looking promotion of human rights in education and
new legal provisions, the struggle against discrimination of diverse sectors of
the population, and the expansion of the notion of rights to include social
and collective rights.
Since its inception, the human rights movement has oscillated between
an institutional and political role, on the one hand, and a role in fostering
memory on the other. Th e movement demanded institutionally sanctioned
justice, and at the same time it actively promoted no olvido (non-oblivion)
and developed symbols and events to keep alive the memories of the experi-
ence under dictatorship, in diff erent ways and spheres. Th is history took
place in shifting political scenarios. Its actors transformed themselves over
time, though these transformations were anchored in a symbolic continu-
ity of the struggle and its slogan, Ni olvido ni perdón (Never forgive, never
forget). Th e struggle against forgiving led to a profound clash with the state,
especially in the 1990s. A rapprochement came later, during the progres-
sive administrations of President Néstor Kirchner (2003–7) and Cristina
Fernández de Kirchner (2007–11, 2011–15), when the state largely adopted
the movement’s agenda. Th e changes that followed the election of right-
leaning President Mauricio Macri (2015–19), however, were a source of great
