reticence and consternation for the human rights movement, yet renewed
expectations were brought about by the change of government in December
2019.
In spite of the movement’s shifting relationship to governments, the fi rst
part of the slogan—never forget—has had considerable continuity: the eff orts
in social and symbolic struggles have remained constant over time. In the
view of the protagonists of the movement, the guiding principle is that the
only way to prevent atrocities from being repeated is by relentlessly remem-
bering what happened— as if persistent recollection of the terror experienced
under dictatorship guaranteed a future of nunca más (never again).

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 59
Th is chapter presents the continuities and transformations in the actions
of the human rights movement, looking at the scenarios where the actions
took place, its adversaries and supporters, and the interpretive frameworks
used to understand both the past and the shifting political scenarios.
Th e Political Scenario of the 1970s: Repression,
Denunciation, and Support and Aid under Dictatorship
Seen from a historical perspective, the “rights of man” was one of modernity’s
tenets in the West, specifi cally as laid out by the bourgeoisie and its philoso-
phers in the face of monarchical power and privilege. Th ough conceptualized
and interpreted in diff erent ways, the French Revolution’s Declaration of the
Rights of the Man and the U.S. Constitution provide the interpretive frame-
work that would later be incorporated by political and social struggles across
the world. At the level of international policy, the issue merited little atten-
tion until the postwar period, when the Universal Declaration of Human
Rights was passed in 1948. Th e passage of the declaration, however, did not
immediately establish an international consensus or a widespread idea of
the human condition anchored in this interpretation. Numerous countries,
in fact, refused to accept or ratify it, invoking cultural traditions or specifi c
religious principles, an appeal to “natural rights,” and other essentializing
notions.
Although the countries of Latin America were actively involved in draft-
ing the declaration, the framework of human rights did not lead to con-
crete policies or actions until the 1970s. During that decade, the human
rights paradigm became a legitimate framework for political action, an ethi-
cal benchmark that diverse actors could refer to when denouncing concrete
examples of state violence (Sikkink 2017). Th ough there had been accusa-
tions of violence and violations in the past, these never drew on the language
of human rights or invoked them as a clear category.
International human rights networks and regional ones in Latin America
also emerged and began to have considerable weight in the 1970s. Since
then, nongovernmental organizations dedicated to human rights multiplied,
some governments began to denounce and issue sanctions for violations in
other countries beyond their borders, and the international system expanded
its presence and endeavors in this fi eld. It is important to consider that until
that time, issues of human rights had been limited to disputes between the
West and the Communist Bloc. Now, in response to the violations in the
Southern Cone, the international concern with human rights issues was rede-
fi ned and reframed: according to the new premise, human rights of popula-
tions living under any political regime can also be violated. Since that initial

60 • Th e Struggle for the Past
moment in the 1970s, the debate over jurisdiction—the sovereignty of the
nation-state or its acquiescence to international legal provisions—has been a
constant, with some especially confl ict-ridden moments, such as the arrest of
former Chilean dictator Augusto Pinochet in London in 1998.
In Argentina, political violence and repressive practices on the part of the
state were nothing new. Illegal detentions, torture of political prisoners and
common inmates, and inhumane conditions in prisons were well-known facts
that diverse political opponents had denounced at various points in history.
Organizations formed to defend and protect the victims had, in fact, a history
all of their own. Th us, the Communist Party had started the Argentine League
for the Rights of Man (Liga Argentina por los Derechos del Hombre) in 1937
to defend and protect persecuted political militants whose rights had been
trampled (Villalba Walsh 1984). Also, since their creation, Jewish community
organizations have fought against demonstrations of antisemitism and worked
to protect potential victims (Veiga 1985; Leis 1989). Th ough more recent and
not as strong as in other places in Latin America, organizations emerged that
were dedicated to protecting the rights of subaltern groups, especially ethnic
minorities and indigenous groups. Th ese include the Service for Peace and
Justice (Servicio de Paz y Justicia, SERPAJ), a secular organization connected
to the Catholic Church and active in Latin America since the 1950s. In addi-
tion, since the beginning of the 1970s, lawyers who defended political prison-
ers, social militants, and labor union activists joined forces in the Attorneys’
Union (Asociación Gremial de Abogados) (Memoria Abierta 2010).
Th e level and intensity of Argentina’s political violence spiked during the
fi rst half of the 1970s. Opposition movements to the governing elites multi-
plied. Th e most radical ones had revolutionary ideas and plans and incorpo-
rated armed struggle as part of their strategy. Th e most important of these
groups were Montoneros and the People’s Revolutionary Army (Ejército
Revolucionario del Pueblo, ERP). At the same time, right-wing paramili-
tary forces—epitomized by the Argentine Anti-Communist Alliance (Alianza
Anticomunista Argentina, or Triple A)—began operating in Argentina. Th e
attacks they carried out, often in coordination with state agencies already
practicing repression, were done increasingly in the open.
In this context of widespread political violence, with a state that began
resorting to illegal repression on a regular basis, the coup d’état took place
on 24 March 1976. At the time of the coup, guerrilla organizations were
already severely debilitated, due to the prior repressive practices of the state
and the paramilitary forces. A state of siege had been declared two years ear-
lier. Secret presidential decrees that ordered the armed forces to “annihilate”
the guerrillas were issued in February 1975; they were executed in missions
like Operation Independence (Operativo Independencia) in the province of
Tucumán (Garaño 2011).

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 61
Two types of repression became emblematic of Argentina: forced disap-
pearances, and the kidnapping of children and newborns who were then
raised under false identities after being taken from their disappeared parents
or born in captivity to a mother held in a clandestine detention center. Th ese
practices accompanied more conventional types of dictatorial repression: the
disenfranchisement of civil society organizations, a ban on political parties
and unions, and limits on public liberties of all sorts. Th ese practices, along
with repression in the cultural fi eld—arrests, censorship, book burning,
etc.—were part of a systematic plan and not an accumulation of arbitrary
violations.
Meanwhile, the opposition, the victims, and progressive social and politi-
cal organizations began to decry human rights violations, making denuncia-
tions and demands out in the open. Existing organizations for the defense
of victims of political and social persecution realigned their objectives, and
new organizations were founded. What is important to note regarding this
process is that state repression was increasingly referred to as “human rights
violations,” an interpretive framework and paradigm that coalesced interna-
tionally in the 1970s (Sikkink 1996; Keck and Sikkink 1998).
Th e fi rst organization to incorporate the expression “human rights” as
part of its name was the Permanent Assembly for Human Rights (Asamblea
Permanente por los Derechos Humanos, or APDH). It was founded in 1975,
a few months before the coup.2 Th e APDH proposed
to promote the eff ective implementation of human rights as established
in the Universal Declaration of the United Nations and in the Argentine
Constitution, and to contribute to putting an end to all forms of terrorism.
A diverse, pluralistic spectrum of social and political actors backed this pro-
posal, which provided a universalistic framework against the surge in violence
on the part of the state, the guerrillas, and the extreme right-wing Triple A.
As one of the most well-known leaders of the APDH noted,
Men with a range of ideological, political, and religious beliefs came together
behind a single idea. With great pride, we saw Catholics, Protestants and Jews
sit down at the same table; and as for the political parties, the Socialist party
was there alongside … the Radical Civic Union (Unión Cívica Radical) … and
the Intransigent Party (Partido Intransigente). … But just to clarify, there were
less than a hundred of us. (Alfredo Bravo,3 Nueva Presencia, 28 September
1984)
Before the 1976 coup, the existing organizations articulated their
demands around political violence and human rights violations before mas-
sive disappearances became the dominant form of repression at the outset of

62 • Th e Struggle for the Past
dictatorship. During that period, activists linked to various religious congre-
gations began to express their concern over human rights violations. Th us,
while other Latin American repressive dictatorships were in place, SERPAJ
launched an international campaign in 1975 denouncing the abuse of human
rights and the need to defend them in the region. Churches associated with
the World Council of Churches also mobilized for the same cause, leading to
the creation of the Ecumenical Movement for Human Rights (Movimiento
Ecuménico por los Derechos Humanos, MEDH) in February 1976. Th e
authorities of the Catholic Church, however, opposed these initiatives. In the
words of Methodist bishop Federico Pagura,4
At a certain point in time, the representatives of our churches associated with
the World Council of Churches initiated a dialogue with the highest authori-
ties of the Catholic Church. We proposed creating a Vicarage of Solidarity
(Vicaría de la Solidaridad), as the Chilean Church had, and we were willing
to overlook the fact that we were Evangelicals and give them all our support if
they accepted the idea. But they informed us that the Argentine Church was
not willing to engage in anything of the sort and that Caritas would handle all
human rights initiatives. … For us, this meant that we had reached a dead end
with them, but it is precisely what led us to start the Ecumenical Movement
for Human Rights (Federico Pagura, Humor, January 1984).
Later on, as repression and horror intensifi ed, the people “directly aff ected”
(afectados directos) formed their own organizations5: Mothers of the Plaza
de Mayo (Madres de Plaza de Mayo), Grandmothers of the Plaza de Mayo
(Abuelas), Relatives of the Disappeared and Imprisoned for Political Reasons
(Familiares de Desaparecidos y Detenidos por Razones Políticas).
During the fi rst few years, some human rights leaders were active in sev-
eral of these organizations at the same time. Th ere was a great deal of solidar-
ity, mutual support, and assistance. Despite the number of organizations,
they all worked to denounce violations and spread the word, not just nation-
ally but also abroad, in order to foster solidarity and support in the struggle
against dictatorship. Second, all provided assistance for the victims and their
families. Yet throughout these years, there were disagreements among the
organizations on both principles and strategies. Th e diff erences between the
organizations and the motivations and goals of the fi rst human rights activists
during these initial years provide insight into the debates, ruptures, and con-
fl icts that surfaced during the transition in 1983, as well as on the alliances
and strategies they later adopted.
At fi rst glance, the group of organizations of the “directly aff ected” appears
to be relatively homogeneous: its members were all relatives of victims, and
the main reason for mobilizing was to recover a family member who had been
disappeared or detained. Th is was their raison d’être. However, there were

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 63
diff erent outlooks even within this group of organizations, which explains
both the existence of diff erent organizations and the dynamics of their rela-
tionships. First, political positions varied from individuals who defi ned
themselves as “apolitical” to others connected to left-leaning political groups.
Th en there were diff erences in the specifi c tasks the organizations carried
out and the demands they expressed; this was especially patent in the case of
Grandmothers, an organization whose purpose was to fi nd and recover the
stolen grandchildren born to disappeared daughters and daughters-in-law.
Finally, within organizations like Mothers, there were distinctive personalities
and leadership styles.
From the time they were founded, the other organizations—those based
on solidarity (often referred to as organizations of the “non-aff ected”)—
brought together a vast array of people. First, there were political activists
with a clear party affi liation, though rarely members of party leadership;
instead, they were often members of minority factions within their parties
or persons interested in the party’s agenda but not actual members. Second
came clergymen of diff erent denominations, often low-ranking members,
and third, professionals and independent intellectuals. Some of these activists,
however, were also “directly aff ected,” i.e., relatives of victims who preferred
to participate in this type of solidarity organization rather than in those of
the “directly aff ected.” Th e public activity of these groups was relatively lim-
ited at the start of the dictatorship as a result of repression and the policy of
silence instilled by the military regime. It was not until the visit of the Inter-
American Commission on Human Rights (IACHR) of the Organization of
American States in September 1979 that public debate began on the modus
operandi of the military regime.
Th e diff erences between human rights organizations were evident in the
strategy each opted for to address various challenges: what degree of pre-
caution was necessary when making accusations and going public against
the regime? What demands or claims should be made? Who could they
speak to? What type of international partnerships or assistance should they
try to obtain? Some of the organizations—fi rst the APDH and later the
Center for Legal and Social Studies (Centro de Estudios Legales y Sociales, or
CELS)—opted for a “legalistic” strategy, their logic anchored in formal law.
Th e thoughts of an APDH leader on the organization’s tenth anniversary are
indicative of its approach:
All of our actions were rigorously legal and we were never tempted to go
underground. … And what made this possible was a precise framework of
what we all agreed on: the thirty articles of the Universal Declaration of
Human Rights. We made no other religious, political, or ideological demands,
not claims of any other sort. … Th e constant search for consensus, and never

64 • Th e Struggle for the Past
working to defeat or reject others, rounds off the approach that has made the
APDH an ethical benchmark in Argentina today. (Enzo Giustozzi,6 Derechos
Humanos, APDH, February 1987)
Th e APDH’s approach is clearly manifested in the tone employed in their
public presentations and demands. For example, a letter sent in August 1977
to General Videla, Argentina’s de facto president, begins:
Th e Permanent Assembly for Human Rights and the citizens who individu-
ally and independently sign this letter are respectfully reaching out to you
under the right to petition established in the Argentine Constitution and laws
to solicit information on disappeared persons, given that there has been no
response to the writs of habeas corpus duly presented to courts of diff erent
jurisdictions. (Cited in Leis 1989: 80)
Other organizations fell in line with what their political parties and reli-
gious institutions dictated. Th us, the Argentine League for the Rights of Man,
linked to the Communist Party, limited its scope of action by the position of
the U.S.S.R., which kept important economic ties with the Argentine dicta-
torship. Th ere were signifi cant debates on what position to take on the visit
by the Inter-American Commission on Human Rights within the APDH,
and between the league and other organizations (Mignone 1991: 111). As
Graciela Fernández Meijide7 has noted, “Th at was a test of strength. …
Th e fact that parents of disappeared children were involved helped us push
onward” (CEDES,8 1989). Th ese positions were better adapted to an insti-
tutional approach, while those of the organizations of the “directly aff ected”
foregrounded other principles and were less concerned with fear or strategic
considerations. In general, the organizations of the aff ected were more com-
mitted to an expressive role, relentlessly denouncing what was occurring,
drawing attention to the violations, and “magnifying the outcry,” often at
considerable personal risks.
Personally, I make a distinction between a human rights organization and a
human rights group—the mothers, the relatives, later on the grandmothers. A
terrible fate, not a common ideology, brought these people together. In con-
trast, in most of the other organizations, the majority of members provided
solidarity to these groups, with all the risks involved. … Th e groups of those
directly aff ected were the ones that stirred things up, while the other organi-
zations had the task of supporting them, while at the same time preparing
court fi lings. Th is was done not out of any certainty that the courts would
take action, but because doing so was a way to stay united and get some press,
a certain recognition of what was happening. (Fernández Meijide, CEDES,
1989)

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 65
During the years of the dictatorship, these diff erences reveal the con-
sensuses and disagreements within and between organizations, despite their
involvement in a common cause. Th e hope that the disappeared would be
released remained a priority until the transition. Th ese diff erences provide
insight into the confl icts over the use of certain words in the documents
that were released and in the choice of slogans, which would unify or divide
the movement. In particular, the term detenido-desaparecido (detained-
disappeared) and the slogans Aparición con vida (Bring them back alive) and
Castigo a todos los culpables (Punishment for all those guilty) were sources of
contention.
With regards to the fi rst expression, detenido-desaparecido, Emilio
Mignone9 recounted that there was a dispute among the human rights activ-
ists over whether to publicly adopt the position that detention-disappearance
(with the hyphen) was the offi cial policy of the dictatorship (Mignone 1991).
In fact, when the APDH was drafting the list of disappearances—a document
that would prove crucial during the visit of the Inter-American Commission
on Human Rights—the question of whether to use detenido-desaparecido or
the term aprehendido (apprehended) was hotly debated, since some believed
the fi rst was too daring an accusation against the military regime. Feeling that
the APDH’s strategy limited the scope of action, especially when it came to
providing support for individual cases, i.e. beyond collective denunciations,
some activists opted to create CELS. Th is organization was led by individuals
“with some political experience who had been directly aff ected by the repres-
sion,” and therefore it allowed them to “unify and coordinate eff orts within
the human rights movement” (Augusto Conte,10 Humor, no. 109, July 1983).
Care and Support for Victims and the Aff ected
During the years in which repression was at its worst, some of the interven-
tions of human rights organizations were intimate and face-to-face. Little
has been written about the care that the human rights movement provided
to those directly aff ected by repression: victims, relatives, and others in their
immediate circles. It is a well-known fact that the state terrorist tactics of
Argentina’s dictatorship included indiscriminately using torture, hiding rel-
evant information, instilling a climate of fear, bypassing the judiciary, creat-
ing uncertainty among relatives of victims, and making deliberate attempts
to confuse the public, as noted in a CELS document from this period (CELS,
n.d.).
Under such circumstances, being a witness or victim of a terrorist raid
sowed confusion and paralysis: what should be done? Where could one go
to report what had happened or request help? Many family members fell

66 • Th e Struggle for the Past
silent, some for many years; others, perhaps, forever, though still others made
an immediate decision to act in the open. As Mignone recounts, when his
daughter was kidnapped and disappeared, he and his wife made three deci-
sions: not to hide the fact, affi rm—and insist—that public forces had taken
her, and be actively involved in solidarity and support for those aff ected
(Mignone 1991: 93–94). Informal channels provided information on how
to present a writ of habeas corpus or a list of institutions that were willing to
accept reports: the U.S. Embassy (following the election of President James
Carter), human rights organizations, and a handful of churches. In all cases,
however, those aff ected had to overcome terror and fear.
Th e APDH centralized the task of receiving testimonies, though other
organizations took them as well. Th e Permanent Assembly, however, did not
handle court fi lings of individual cases—a task that CELS specialized in,
once it was founded in 1980; it only prepared collective suits. In 1977, the
APDH reached the Supreme Court with a suit on the deprivation of liberty
of 425 people. Given the court’s admission that it was powerless to remedy
the situation, the APDH sent the aforementioned letter-petition to the presi-
dent. Later, when preparing for the visit by the Inter-American Commission
on Human Rights in 1979, its archive was used to draft a list of 5,566 cases
of individuals who had been detained-disappeared.
Th e emotional side of the issue—support and care for people’s pain and
desperation—was closely tied to the tasks of guiding and advising family
members. When the individuals aff ected by these human rights violations
had prior experience in the public sphere and political contacts (this was
more common among the middle classes), they would reach out to their con-
tacts through informal links. When such contacts were of no help, despera-
tion and disorientation took hold.11 Th e very decision to start some of the
organizations—especially those of the “directly aff ected”—was in response
to the need to have places where fl ustered, apprehensive family members and
victims with no idea where to turn could mutually support one another. Th is
was the motivation to start Grandmothers of the Plaza de Mayo (Herrera and
Tenembaum 1990). In any case, it is clear that the initial attitude of relatives
at the time of the disappearance was decisive for their subsequent action.
“Th ose who were aff ected and did not get involved during dictatorship never
got involved,” noted Graciela Fernández Meijide years later (CEDES, 1989).
Th ere are other facets to reporting and providing support. Th e work of
human rights organizations was based on accumulating information on indi-
vidual cases, with a strong presence and participation of victims’ relatives.
Th ey took action in the public space: Plaza de Mayo, the seats of national
and international power. Yet others who had also been aff ected did not
reach the main organizations or go public, either internationally or nation-
ally. Local organizations and spaces for community life in popular-sector

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 67
neighborhoods and small towns where human rights violations had occurred
also took action, though in other forms. Neighborhood communities and
social networks—including trade unions and political and religious organiza-
tions—provided support and expressed their solidarity and repudiation in
other ways. Th ese included support and care for victims and relatives, rallies,
local protests on neighborhood streets or at the doors of factories, petitions
with hundreds of signatures, and masses in local churches. Th ese generally
occurred at locations with an existing sense of community, places whose local
groups and networks were ready to respond to the urgent needs of the time.
In relation to a popular-sector neighborhood in La Matanza, on the outskirts
of the city of Buenos Aires, Norberto Liwski12 recounts,
It was very diffi cult to convey the scope of what was taking place locally, in
one’s own area of work, to the formal human rights organizations. Th e human
rights organizations operated under a diff erent dynamic. Th ey were looking at
individual experiences of violations of the right to life, kidnappings, or disap-
pearances, and that made it hard to understand that what we were facing was
massive, immense. In any case, many things were done [in the neighborhood].
We sent telegrams everywhere … (CEDES, 1990)
Th ere are clearly many limitations to protest and repudiation in times of
terror and repression. Th e fact that human rights organizations were scarcely
present in the outlying neighborhoods of Buenos Aires or in towns in the
interior was linked to a question of safety and calculations of danger, to
the pace and timing of the action, and to social class. At the country level,
protests were events with a national or international scope; neighborhood
activists organized at a diff erent pace. Th is type of protest, more informal and
localized in popular-sector neighborhoods or small towns, did not reach the
more visible organizations or places. Th ey remained only in the memories of
those who participated; they are not part of the “offi cial story” of resistance
to dictatorship, a history that focuses on the work of human rights organiza-
tions headquartered in Buenos Aires.13 Th e task of incorporating this type of
action into the records of resistance is still pending, as is the more scholarly
task of exploring the similarities and contrasts between these activists and the
human rights organizations—and the logics that guided each.
Denunciation: “Bring Th em Back Alive”
Persistent and public denunciations of the violations was the second main
task of the human rights movement. It was in this arena of public confronta-
tion where the meaning of the struggle took shape—and at the same time,
a struggle over meaning ensued. What should the demands be? How should

68 • Th e Struggle for the Past
activists recount what they knew? And what they didn’t know? Th e most
emotional, political, and strategic slogan of the human rights movement was
“Bring them back alive.”
Th e year 1980 was particularly critical for the human rights movement.
In the fi rst few months, the military regime launched a diálogo politico
(political dialogue) with leaders of various political parties. Its aim was
to receive civilian backing for the repressive actions the armed forces had
undertaken, and begin negotiating their “exit plan” (Acuña and Smulovitz
1995, González Bombal 1995). In the midst of this outreach, Radical Party
leader Ricardo Balbín made a statement that would have far-reaching politi-
cal repercussions.
I believe there are no disappeared: I believe they are all dead. … Even though
I have not seen a death certifi cate for any of them. … Th ere’s nothing to be
done. Th at’s the way it went. Someday, the chapter will be written on who’s
responsible. … Th ere are no disappeared, only the dead. (Clarín, 24 April
1980)
Th is statement, made to the press while Ricardo Balbín was in Spain, coin-
cided with the international publication of the IACHR report. At around
this time, human rights violations in Argentina began attracting a great deal
of attention abroad. In October, Adolfo Pérez Esquivel received the Nobel
Peace Prize, an event with upshots diffi cult for the military regime to control.
In public statements, the SERPAJ leader extended the Nobel Prize to other
human rights organizations:
It will be a catalyst for other movements. It is not a prize for a single person but
recognizes an organization: it recognizes the Permanent Assembly for Human
Rights, the Ecumenical Movement, the Mothers of Plaza de Mayo, and the
peasants struggling for their lands. (Clarín, 14 October 1980)
Th e leaders of the human rights movement who accompanied Pérez
Esquivel to Sweden to receive the Nobel in December had not agreed on a
strategy for what they were going to say to the press about the disappearances
and deaths. Many leaders had compiled information confi rming the deaths
of some of those disappeared—including that of their own sons or daughters,
or other relatives—and it was impossible for them to hide this information.
Th is is why, as Mignone noted,
When I was approached by the guy from Le Monde, and other European and
Swedish reporters, and they asked me what I believed—whether the disap-
peared were alive or dead—I couldn’t lie: I knew that some of them were dead.
(Mignone, CEDES, 1991)

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 69
Th e scope of this admission, however, was nowhere near Ricardo Balbín’s
statement.
On the other hand, there were cases of people disappeared who returned
alive: starting shortly after the 1976 coup and throughout the period of
the repression, some of the disappeared were freed. Additionally, thanks to
the ceaseless search by the Grandmothers and the support of international
organizations, a handful of the children kidnapped with their parents or
born in captivity were also recovered.14 Although a certain number of those
released remained silent after their liberation, others took it upon themselves
to report on the existence of the clandestine detention centers and denounce
what went on there. It was in this climate of certainties enveloped in a cli-
mate of uncertainty—the certainty that some were alive, and that many
were dead—that the Mothers started demanding “Bring them back alive.”
In literal terms, this slogan—which would become one of the most signifi -
cant banners during the transition—denied that the victims of state terror-
ism were dead. With a slogan that off ered no concessions whatsoever, the
Mothers positioned themselves at one extreme of the spectrum of demands.
Other organizations adopted this slogan to varying degrees.15
Even earlier, in 1979, there had been debates on whether to use this
slogan.16 Th e demand for the disappeared to be returned alive had been a
source of controversy within the human rights movement since early on. In
relation to the meetings held during those years, Mothers president Hebe de
Bonafi ni complained that although the other organizations (the MEDH and
APDH) “said they wanted the disappeared back alive, they didn’t agree we
should ask for that” (Diario de las Madres, December 1987).
After the ceremony of the Nobel Prize, which was an explicit show of
international support for the struggle for human rights defense in Argentina,
Argentines themselves became more open to the topic. International support
and attention played a signifi cant part in the willingness of Argentines to
learn and listen. At that time, the most intense repression (above all, the kid-
nappings) had fallen from the peak in 1976–77, and fear started to fade. Th e
human rights movement became more visible, and many social and political
organizations that had remained silent until then began to incorporate these
demands in their own agendas. In April 1981, on the fourth anniversary of
the fi rst march by the Mothers, two thousand people gathered in Plaza de
Mayo, despite a police announcement forbidding the rally. Th e slogan “Bring
them back alive,” was on the street and in the papers.
Th e fi rst rally jointly organized by all of the human rights organizations
took place on 15 October 1981. Broad sectors across the political spectrum
began to mobilize. In December of that year, the Mothers organized the fi rst
Resistance March (Marcha de la Resistencia) and all the organizations were
involved. While until 1981 the organizations had been divided between the

70 • Th e Struggle for the Past
“moderates” (the Argentine League for the Rights of Man, the MEDH, and
sometimes the APDH) and the more “vocal” ones (Mothers, Grandmothers,
Relatives, SERPAJ), by the end of that year, all had shifted to more open
opposition to the dictatorial government. Th is would become even clearer
when Great Britain defeated Argentina in the Malvinas.
Las Malvinas son Argentinas, los Desaparecidos También
(Th e Malvinas Are Argentine and so Are the Disappeared)
Argentina’s defeat in the Malvinas War (1982) marked a turning point in
politics in Argentina, but also in the public presence of and demands posed
by the country’s human rights movement. Now that the military regime was
fl oundering and an end to dictatorship seemed possible, both those who
held power and those who hoped to succeed them became preoccupied with
what would be done about repression—and most especially, about the disap-
peared. Human rights activists knew it was essential to maintain the momen-
tum. Th e Mothers had responded to Argentina’s takeover of the islands with
the slogan “Th e Malvinas are Argentine and so are the disappeared.” While
many political discussions made no connections between the war in the
Malvinas and the clandestine repression carried out by the military under
dictatorship, the Mothers’ connection of the two was emphatic.17
Th e human rights movement played an important political role in the fol-
lowing stage and until the transition (Sondereguer 1985, among others). In
the second half of 1982, there was a wave of demands and court fi lings linked
to disappeared persons. People who had previously been afraid to report now
worked up the courage to come forward. During this period, a number of
clandestine burial sites were discovered in diff erent cemeteries (on the case
of Grand Bourg, see Gandulfo 2015). Th e time was ripe for a wide variety of
proposals on strategies for political opening, and the tensions and contradic-
tions between diff erent slogans and entreaties became patent.
While the activists had initially demanded information on the fate of the
detained-disappeared, as captured in Vivos o muertos (Dead or alive), the
slogan now changed. “Bring them back alive” was a rallying cry that got right
to the point both during and after the Malvinas War, helping to get people
politically mobilized and building activism and collective identities.
At the same time, diverse sectors of society took the discovery of clandes-
tine burial sites and the images of piles of human bones as evidence that the
demand captured in this slogan was both unrealistic and impossible. In the
following years, the contradiction between demanding that human remains be
identifi ed and clamoring for the disappeared to be returned alive would deepen
and become a source of dispute even within the human rights movement itself.

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 71
Across the country, the population joined the March for Life (Marcha por
la vida) held on 5 October 1982. In Buenos Aires, leaders from the human
rights movement, politics, churches, unions, and intellectual circles led the
march, which attracted over ten thousand people. In addition to the existing
slogans “You took them alive, we want them back alive,” and “Los desapa-
recidos, que digan dónde están” (Tell us where the disappeared are), a new
slogan appeared, this one of an institutional character: “Se va a acabar, se
va a acabar, la dictadura militar” (Th e end is near for the military dictator-
ship). Th e marchers were unable to enter Plaza de Mayo because the military
government had blocked off the square, but newspapers and TV channels
provided ample coverage.
Th e next important rally, the Resistance March, was scheduled for 9–10
December that year. At that event, where Plaza de Mayo was also cordoned
off , political parties carrying banners joined militant political groups from
the 1970s and the members of the human rights movement, each with their
own specifi c demands. Th e media had no choice but to report on these hap-
penings, increasing social awareness of the topic. In fact, the human rights
movement got people out for rallies and collective actions on the street, and
this shaped the public space during the transition. Th ese street initiatives
relied on a “traditional” political expression in Argentina—rallies, especially
at Plaza de Mayo (Sigal 2006)–with new slogans and demands. A new inter-
pretive framework for these slogans and demands combined the call for
democracy (“Th e end is near …”) with other more specifi c ones connected
to repression under dictatorship, i.e., “Bring them back alive,” and later
“Juicio y castigo” (Trial and punishment). Political parties began to support
the public statements of the human rights movement, with political leaders
playing a prominent role.
Here we note the paradox between what was going on, as covered by the
press—the media circus surrounding the collective burials in N.N. tombs
(Feld 2015)—and the insistence on “Bring them back alive,” which turned
a blind eye to the discovery of human remains. As Hebe de Bonafi ni noted,
Th e March for Life was very important because we felt that what the Mothers
were asking for had a great impact on the people. … It was utterly important,
especially at the moment when the people who went to Plaza de Mayo knew
that they were there to say “Bring them back alive.” No one was tricked: every-
one who went knew why, and that is very important. (El Porteño, April 1983)
Th at slogan served to mobilize people outside the human rights move-
ment, which conveyed the image of a solid and unifi ed movement. Internally,
however, the slogan led to a growing rift, which eventually left some of the

72 • Th e Struggle for the Past
organizations at odds with one another. Th ese diff erences would become
more apparent in the years that followed. As Fernández Meijide has noted,
Th e hardest slogans were “Trial and punishment” and “Bring them back alive,”
because in some cases, they were seen as off ering no way out. … Th at was the
argument given publicly, but the truth is that the goal was not to shove them
[the government] up against a wall. (Fernández Meijide, CEDES, 1989)
At that time, new areas of tension began to appear among diverse actors
in the human rights movement: was it appropriate to mention the political
affi liations of the disappeared? While some wanted to do so, others believed
that to some degree, it bolstered a military discourse in which the disap-
peared were labeled “subversives” or “terrorists.” Th e group against mention-
ing political affi liations preferred a more neutral term, “popular militant.”
Th e question on whether to defend the political struggles of the disappeared
sparked intense debate, especially among those who wanted to emphasize
their status as victims of the repression and others who wanted to specify that
these were social and/or political militants. After much confl ict and internal
debate, the Mothers opted to make reference to the struggles of their chil-
dren, but in a very broad and general manner, without invoking any party
organizations or types of struggles. In October 1983, the Mothers put out
their fi rst street sign. It read:
Th e Mothers of Plaza de Mayo call on the people to accompany them every
Th ursday at 3:30 p.m. to ask that the detained-disappeared be returned alive.
With profound love for their people, the detained-disappeared fought for dig-
nity, justice, and freedom. Bring them back alive to defend life, and trial and
punishment for the guilty to defend the nation. (Emphasis my own)
Th e Mothers mentioned “dignity, justice, and freedom” as the guiding
principles of their struggle. Th is was an entirely diff erent approach than
the one that would be taken years later, when they would staunchly defend
the political-revolutionary identities of the detained-disappeared—and even
those of the armed organizations. At the same time, by demanding that the
detained-disappeared be returned alive, the terminology made no reference
to their condition as “victims,” a characterization that would not become
common until the National Commission on the Disappeared (Comisión
Nacional sobre la Desaparición de Personas, or CONADEP) was established
and the trials of 1985 took place.
A few questions are pertinent here. Who identifi ed with the demands of
the movement? What presence did the movement have in diff erent places
across Argentina? What media outlets covered the movement’s rallies and
demand? What was the tone of this coverage? Th ese are not easy questions.

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 73
Th ere were, of course, many demands, and the antidictatorship protesters
harnessed multiple social grievances that were becoming visible in the public
sphere. Th e public presence of the movement was the tip of the iceberg, a
catalyst of the protests that opened up opportunities to also contest other
injustices that had not been voiced for years. Th us, during the last period of
dictatorship, the demands related to disappearances went hand-in-hand with
the political push for democratization; over the next few years, the movement
would also incorporate grievances tied to other violations and demands for
“human rights,” including those of a sexual and social nature. Th ese other
demands, however, were secondary to their raison d’être.
As we have seen, the demand “Bring them back alive” was critical during
this period, as was the formulation of the increasingly specifi c cry for jus-
tice. When information on clandestine burial sites in cemeteries began to
appear in 1982, especially the Grand Bourg case, the relatives of the disap-
peared played a particularly important role. Human rights organizations and
relatives joined rallies at cemeteries and went to court over the exhumations,
both of which received broad press coverage (Gandulfo 2012, 2015).
Th e movement had three priorities: “Bring them back alive,” calls for jus-
tice, and proposals related to the transition to democracy and institutional
change. Its actions focused on political events of the period, decisions made
by the dictatorial regime (the “Final Document”18), and the statements made
by important political leaders. With this agenda in mind, the human rights
movement paid little attention to press headlines or sensationalist coverage of
the exhumations, a coverage some would refer to later as the “horror show”
(Feld 2015). Th is sensationalism was secondary and did not merit a specifi c
response on the part of the movement.19
However, certain important aspects of the movement’s agenda were tied
to what the press revealed (and hid). Unveiling the horror—through accu-
sations, discoveries of clandestine burial sites, testimonies by the Mothers
and relatives, and reports of disappeared persons “reappearing” abroad—had
an eff ect on society: by 1983, as the election campaign gained traction, the
majority of Argentine society no longer had any doubts about the regime’s
brutality. Despite the scarce information about the scope and features of the
repression at that time in comparison to what became known later on, most
of the population would agree that nothing the disappeared could have done
merited the type of repressive actions taken.
Th e Demand for Justice: Trial and Punishment during the Transition
Th e coming together of the human rights struggle with the demand for jus-
tice was neither immediate nor automatic. Every demand for justice involves
restoring a balance previously destroyed. In order to achieve this, it requires

74 • Th e Struggle for the Past
information on the scope of the damage to determine how much eff ort will
be needed to put things back into place. Disappearance is, in fact, a very
peculiar type of damage. One of the most fundamental mechanisms of repres-
sion involved systematically hiding any accurate information. Disappearance,
when conceived as damage, involves both kidnapping a person and confi scat-
ing knowledge. Th is brings in one of the unique aspects of the human rights
movement: in response to an unknown amount of damage, it demands an
indeterminate quantity of justice. At the beginning, all that could be said
was, “something has happened.” One of the movement’s fi rst tasks was to
establish, with some degree of certainty, what had happened and what vio-
lations the military had committed. In other words, the main task was to
demand and actively produce “truth.”
Th e defi nition of the violence in terms of “human rights violations” was
the step that allowed the legal dimension to be introduced to the political
confl ict. Th e relationship between “truth” and “justice” thus became a topic
of discussion; in the years that followed, it would trend internationally as
“truth commissions” proliferated. Human Rights Watch published a 1987
report titled “Truth and Partial Justice in Argentina” that emphasized the
importance of truth for understanding what took place and preventing it
from reoccurring. Th e report also noted that knowledge of what occurred is
“an essential part of what is due to the victims, their families, their friends
and their neighbors” (Americas Watch 1987: 61), adding that publicly expos-
ing the shameful conduct of the perpetrators of massive abuses is also part
of the reparations process. Since then, the demands for justice and for truth
have been connected. As a result, investigating the fate of each victim and
fi guring out who was responsible became part of working for justice.
During the fi rst stage of the dictatorship, it was diffi cult to presume or
conclude who was responsible for the disappearances. Yet the suspicion that
members of the government were directly involved with what was happen-
ing soon took hold. Nonetheless, fi nding an explanation for repression of
this magnitude was a much harder task and would take much longer than
expected. At the beginning, the signs on who was carrying out the violence,
and how, were not entirely clear. Th ere were some indications that unorga-
nized groups were doing the kidnappings, though many aspects of the disap-
pearances revealed that the state and security forces were, at the very least,
aware of them. Sowing confusion, fear, uncertainty, and ambiguity is one
well-known element of terror (Corradi 1985).
Th e movement initially formulated its demands around saving lives
and halting repression, doing so in the midst of widespread confusion, the
challenges in terms of grasping what was happening, and the obstacles to
determining the group, institution, sector, or individual responsible for the
disappearances. Justice fi rst appears as an explicit demand in the IACHR

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 75
report on its 1979 visit, which recommends “prosecuting those responsi-
ble.” Starting in 1980 and 1981, the demand for justice became part of
the discourse, slogans and petitions of the movement, though this was a
topic of some internal controversy. During the fi rst “Week of the Detained-
Disappeared” in September 1981, human rights organizations wrangled over
what slogan should be used for the event. For some (the Argentine League
for the Rights of Man), the demand for peace, democratic opening, and
truth were the priorities. Asking for more, its members felt, would be too
much, and even irresponsible. For others, including SERPAJ, Mothers, and
Relatives, the demand had to link peace with justice and truth. Within the
APDH, members locked horns, unveiling their diverging positions on this
issue. On this occasion, like on others, the lack of a consensus between orga-
nizations resulted in two diff erent paid newspaper ads.
“Bring them back alive” and “Trial and punishment for all those guilty”
became the two battle cries of the movement only after Argentina’s defeat
in the Malvinas War. On the one hand, justice appeared to be a plausible
institutional response. On the other, the most oppositional, confrontational
proposals with the military government prevailed in the political struggle
within the movement.
Who Should Be Prosecuted? Who Should Be Punished?
In 1983, the movement worked to promote some kind of trial and punish-
ment for the “guilty,” though the limits on who should be tried and pun-
ished were unclear. Th e members of the armed forces who carried out the
repression were on the list, as were the members of the military juntas and
those responsible for illegal detentions and torture at the clandestine deten-
tion camps, as attested by survivors. Th is was a period in which the human
rights movements worked closely with the General Workers Confederation
(Confederación General del Trabajo de la República Argentina, or CGT) and
political parties (Palermo 1987). By every means possible, the human rights
movement was attempting to avoid a negotiated exit in which the military
would cede power in exchange for guaranteed immunity for perpetrators.
With this objective, the movement multiplied its presence on the streets,
organizing rallies and marches to demand that the disappeared be returned
alive and to protest the passage of the self-amnesty act and the military gov-
ernment’s “Final Report.”
In 1983, no one in the movement was entirely sure what punishment for
human rights abuses might or should involve. In addition to the fear that
the political parties or unions would negotiate impunity for the military,
there was also concern about how to legally prove the crimes that had been
committed in the case that a democratically elected administration pushed

76 • Th e Struggle for the Past
to prosecute those responsible. Th erefore, a multifold strategy was devel-
oped. First, there was the demand for a political trial of human rights viola-
tions, through the establishment of a special bicameral committee. Besides
its intrinsic value, this congressional trial was thought as an alternative in the
face of uncertainty regarding ordinary justice’s effi ciency and the ability to
gather enough evidence for court convictions. On the other hand, the idea of
legal action was still seen as possible; thus the urgency to support and rein-
force the role of federal courts for the moment when the new constitutional
government took offi ce.
Anxieties swelled as the day of handing over power to an elected admin-
istration neared. During a press conference held on 13 August 1983, the
presidential candidate Raúl Alfonsín expressed his opposition to the self-
amnesty act the military were expected to pass. At the press conference, Nora
Cortiñas,20 a prominent leader of Mothers, asked the future president,
How will ordinary justice work if there is no proof—if the military has system-
atically acted to hide the corpus delicti? What fi rst measure will your adminis-
tration take to make the disappeared reappear? (Clarín, 13 August 1983)
On 23 November, shortly before being sworn in, Alfonsín met with the
Mothers. Th ey demanded that the documentation proving the existence of
the clandestine detention centers not leave the country, and that “a political
trial for all those responsible for the disappearance of thousands of people”
take place (Tiempo Argentino, 24 November 1983).
One of the strategies for achieving a political trial was trying to get a
human rights militant in the House of Representatives, promoting the candi-
dacy of Augusto Conte on the slate of the Christian Democratic (Democracia
Cristiana) Party. From the point of view of Conte’s supporters, his candidacy
would put the demands of human rights organizations into the electoral
campaign and ensure that a member of the movement would work for justice
from inside Congress.
Th e push for a political trial also drew from an alleged representation of
the “popular will” expressed in some statements by the Mothers of the Plaza
de Mayo, and in attempts to position the movement’s demands in terms of
ideological-political confrontations. Th us, for the organization of Relatives
of the Disappeared and Imprisoned for Political Reasons, the repression
unleashed in 1976 had to be understood as a new chapter in the history of
popular struggles. Th e hope was that a political trial of the repression would
also draw into question the economic policies that had motivated it.
None of the positions described was exclusive to any of the human rights
organizations. In addition, although there were profound diff erences in
political strategy within the movement, from the outside these appeared

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 77
complementary, not contradictory. Toward the end of 1983, Argentina’s
political scenario was divided between the adversaries of the military regime,
on the one hand, and the armed forces and their old allies, who were now
beating a quiet retreat, on the other. Among those pushing for democracy, it
was diffi cult to make fi ne distinctions between the diff erent slogans against
dictatorship. Th e political value of all the demands rested on their stance
against the military government, which made all manifestations of the oppo-
sition—not only those of the human rights movement—equivalent.
Beyond their longer-term goals and values, the diff erent organizations
concurred on the ethical and moral need to punish those responsible for
crimes while emphasizing the political drawbacks to impunity. “Trial and
punishment” were of the utmost importance. Th e responses of the organiza-
tions to the fi rst measures introduced by the Radical Party government were
to help specify what kind of trial and punishment they sought.
A Field of Contention: Exhumations
Disputes surrounding the exhumations of N.N. tombs off er a special arena
for analyzing this period. Th ey were the focus of the “horror show” as well
as a source of contention over meanings and interpretations of the past and
about political strategies. Government institutions had to intervene, and dif-
ferent actors clashed, over exhumations; they created a space where family
and personal dilemmas and tensions between public policies and individual-
ized suff ering unfolded in vivid ways.
Th e issues associated with identifying remains are framed by the attempts
of the dictatorial regime—and of certain political leaders like Ricardo
Balbín—to take for granted that the disappeared were dead, an assumption
that contradicted the movement’s proposition captured in the slogan “Bring
them back alive.” Th is confl ict continued throughout the dictatorship and
the transition. Besides reports early in the dictatorship, such as Rodolfo
Walsh’s “Carta abierta a la Junta Militar” (Open letter to the military govern-
ment), the IACHR had made the initial confi rmation of clandestine burial
sites in the cemetery of La Plata in 1979.21 Evidence accumulated over the
years, as seen in the letter that two gravediggers in the San Vicente cemetery
in Córdoba sent to the president (Crenzel 2005). In a similar vein, the
case of the Grand Bourg cemetery attracted more extensive media coverage
(Gandulfo 2015). Th ese discoveries led to the founding of the Argentine
Forensic Anthropology Team (Equipo Argentino de Antropología Forense,
or EAAF) and its continuous involvement in exhumations and identifi ca-
tions at clandestine burial sites.22
Each case is, in fact, a labyrinth, where legal actions move things forward
a few steps only to be pushed back again; frequently, the red tape made it

78 • Th e Struggle for the Past
diffi cult to follow what was happening, drawing into question the supposed
rationality of the legal process. Beyond this labyrinth, each exhumation is a
moment in which stances are taken, interpretations are disputed, and politi-
cal confl icts unfold.
In this regard, the confl ict within the organizations was profound and
extensive. On the one hand, there were those who strongly opposed exhuma-
tions of N.N. remains, including the Mothers, even when a judge had ordered
them. Recovering the remains of a disappeared person was not a search for
justice, in their minds, but an individualistic act that weakened the collective
political struggle. In legal terms, a corpse meant that the clock began ticking
on the statute of limitations (twenty years from the time the crime was com-
mitted), creating a possibility that those responsible for the crime would not
be prosecuted and thus perpetuating impunity. Th e Mothers thus believed
that remains should not be identifi ed until it became certain that those
responsible would be prosecuted. Other organizations—and some of the
Mothers—saw things diff erently. Opposing a court order meant a confron-
tation with the relatives who had requested the order, almost always in the
hope that it was a step toward serving justice. Recovering a body also meant
that a loved one was, in fact, dead, allowing relatives to pay their respects and
mourn.
Th e case of Mar del Plata is emblematic of the controversies surrounding
the exhumations within the human rights movement, provoking animosi-
ties and even ruptures. In November 1982, a Mar del Plata court issued an
exhumation order for a local cemetery. Due to the usual delays associated
with court cases, the order was not carried out until the beginning of 1985.
Th e court case had been brought by some mothers and other family mem-
bers because they suspected that their disappeared relatives could be buried
in that cemetery. Th e exhumation was scheduled for 10 March 1985.23 Th e
president of the Mothers, Hebe de Bonafi ni, traveled to the city to lead a
protest against the exhumation. Protesters were convinced that exhumations
hindered the larger cause, while the relatives who had brought the case saw
them as a possible road to justice and the recovery of a body that would
facilitate mourning and family memory. When Hebe de Bonafi ni and several
other Mothers reached the cemetery, they discovered a police line preventing
them from getting to the tombs. Given the tension sparked by the protest,
the remains were not exhumed. Th e next day, all of the newspapers reported
on the story and on the Mothers’ position. Bonafi ni was categorical in her
statements to the press: “We don’t want any death certifi cates: we will not
stand by and watch as anthropologists or scientists, no matter where they are
from, come in and tell us that a bag of bones is [what’s left of] our children”
(cited in Gorini 2008: 271).

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 79
Diff erent institutions criticized the position of the Mothers and the way
the court handled the case, including the executive branch, the government
of the province of Buenos Aires, and the Association of Attorneys (Colegio de
Abogados) of Mar del Plata. Within the human rights organizations, CELS
and Relatives criticized the stance of the Mothers at the cemetery, noting in a
press release “the right of family members of the victims to take all measures
necessary to identify the victims and, when appropriate, to decide what to do
with their remains.” Grandmothers issued a similar statement. Th ese organi-
zations stood behind “Bring them back alive” yet also acknowledged that, in
terms of knowing what had happened to each disappeared individual, “We
cannot rule out any possibility to receiving the answer we have sought from
the authorities time and again” (cited in Gorini 2008: 274). A few days later,
and with no previous announcement, the judge in charge of the case ordered
the exhumation to move forward.
What is critical about this and other cases is that they reveal the tensions
within the human rights movement at the time of transition. Relatively
removed from the sensationalist media coverage, the various actors of the
movement were searching for the best strategy for settling accounts with the
past. Th e tension between the “truth” about the fate of some of the disap-
peared—which could be obtained in exhumations and subsequent identifi ca-
tion processes—and the search for identifying the perpetrators, for trials, and
for punishment was undoubtedly a stumbling block. Th e two were conceived
as incompatible alternatives, not as steps within the same process, as would
be the case later. Each position had specifi c repercussions in terms of the
importance attributed to the courts, to family emotions and private suff ering,
and to “truth,” i.e. any information that could provide insight into what had
happened to each and every victim. Th ese tensions contributed to the divi-
sion of the Mothers into two separate organizations.24
Memory, Truth, and Justice:
Human Rights during the Transition (1983–90)
Th e transition to democracy entailed an important challenge for the human
rights movement. Th e “Somos la vida” (We are life) slogan of the Partido
Radical during the electoral campaign of 1983 had a familiar ring to the
human rights movement. Th e election of Raúl Alfonsín, vice president of the
APDH, meant that the new government would accept and adopt the move-
ment’s demands and values, taking them as the ethical guidelines for state
action. Although human rights also played a role in other transitions in Latin
America, they were the founding block of Argentina’s emergent democracy.

80 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Th e multifaceted tasks to be handled during the transition took place at
diff erent levels, with diff erent meanings and temporal scopes. Th ere were
demands tied to the past and others tied to the future, as well as legal, politi-
cal, cultural, and social concerns.
First, there were the courts and the judicial system. Although human
rights activists never reached a consensus, one thing they did agree on can
be summarized in a slogan—a categorical one, though with an implicit
ambiguity: “Trial and punishment for (all) the guilty.” What kind of “trial”?
What type of “punishment”? “Guilty” of what (González Bombal 1995)?
Although the demands for justice in relation to the responsibilities for past
violations were a central focus of the movement’s actions, demanding justice
and the protection of rights had much broader implications. It was neces-
sary to act on behalf of the victims of human rights violations; their rights
had to be restored, and the damage had to be repaired. Th is included help-
ing those directly aff ected and their relatives, especially but not exclusively
the children of the disappeared. On the other hand, the transition involved
building new institutions geared to the protection of human rights. How
could rights be protected in the future? What institutional transformations
were required? And there was yet another type of question, about the con-
tent of the rights that had to be guaranteed. On this point, the proposals
and demands of the human rights movement aligned with the historical
struggle for expanding citizenship—and all the social confl ict associated
with this struggle.
A second level was strictly political. Besides establishing the parameters
for civilian-military relations, the struggle at that time included other urgent
topics of an economic, institutional, and political nature. In this context, the
policy toward perpetrators was just one item on a packed agenda.
At the level of ethics, values, and culture, human rights were becoming
a key component in the proposal for building a democratic, humanistic,
tolerant, and pluralistic culture. While action on the levels described above
depended necessarily, and almost exclusively, on the state, the cultural tasks
at hand implicated society much more directly. It was diffi cult, however, to
imagine how such tasks would be completed without the support and initia-
tive of the state apparatus. In this societal arena, the human rights movement
began to have a multiplying eff ect during transition, especially in the area
of cultural production, public opinion, and in a range of trade unions, pro-
fessional associations, and student and neighborhood organizations where
human rights committees were formed (González Bombal and Sondereguer
1987; Leis 1989).
When Raúl Alfonsín was sworn in as Argentina’s president, the govern-
ment adopted some, but not all, of the movement’s principles and demands,
and some only partially. Alfonsín wanted to make the issue of justice a

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 81
cornerstone of his presidency. Although prosecuting members of the armed
forces in civil courts was a costly political move—one that could even jeopar-
dize the incipient democracy—not holding any trials was not a viable option.
Alfonsín’s human rights policy thus drew from his commitment to prosecute
the military, but also the need to limit the scope of these trials as part of
his negotiating strategy (Acuña and Smulovitz 1995). Gradually, what was
initially defi ned as “clarifying human rights violations” came to be known as
“the military issue.” More than a semantic change, the new term expressed
a shift in the government’s priorities: the need to resolve an ethical issue
became a need to maintain good relations with the military and its allies. To
a certain extent, this can be attributed to the pressure the military exerted,
although actors and analysts concur that there was political room for bolder
actions during the transition—from the end of 1983 through 1984—given
the weakness of the armed forces.25
In fact, what Alfonsín had proposed during the electoral campaign had
clear limitations: his idea of justice fell within the realm of military justice.
Prosecution, in the president’s view, would be based on the logic of “superior
orders,” i.e. a hierarchy of responsibilities that makes a distinction between
those who gave the orders, those who carried them out, and those responsible
for excesses (Mignone 1991: 150–51; Acuña and Smulovitz 1995). Most
politicians, including Peronist party presidential candidate Ítalo Luder, had
even more moderate proposals for dealing with the “military issue” than
Alfonsín did. Some of these arrangements, in fact, even favored the military
(Palermo 1987).
Right from the outset, some of the human rights organizations (Mothers,
Relatives, SERPAJ) concluded that the government was being too acquies-
cent; they noted that the government was proposing only gradual transfor-
mations in order to avoid unrest among the armed forces. A good portion
of the human rights movement thus distanced itself from the government’s
positions and began to demand more, in terms of serving justice to military
personnel responsible for the violations, clarifying the disappearances, and
freeing the remaining political prisoners. Other organizations that shared the
same objectives and demands (APDH, MEDH) decided instead to embrace
the new democratic institutions and accept the political rules of the game,
convinced that the slogans and demands had to change:
In my opinion, any political slogan that was valid during the dictatorship
could no longer be used under democracy, because we were now directing our
demands to a diff erent entity. (Fernández Meijide, CEDES interview, 1989)
For others like Mothers president Hebe de Bonafi ni, the claims and
demands had to remain the same:

82 • Th e Struggle for the Past
[To continue to use the slogan “Bring them back alive”] is a political issue.
Some human rights organizations have stopped using it precisely because they
have politicians among their members. But we are never going to abandon
that slogan. “Bring them back alive” means everything. No Mother is going
to kill off a son or daughter by abandoning that slogan. (Diario de las Madres,
December 1984)
In this context, on 12 December 1983, the president issued a decree instruct-
ing that the members of the fi rst three military juntas26 be tried in military
court, and a bill was sent to Congress to amend the Military Justice Code
with a specifi c request for legislators to give the bill priority. Congress, in turn,
repealed the Self-Amnesty Act that the military had passed months earlier.
Human rights organizations were quick to react. Th e Argentine People’s
Forum Against State Terrorism (Foro del Pueblo Argentino contra el
Terrorismo de Estado), which had representatives from all the organizations,
expressed its unanimous opposition to the trials of those responsible for
repression being held in military tribunals. Th e organizations did not believe
the military offi cers had the moral integrity to serve as judges of their peers;
they were fearful that such judges might sacrifi ce a few comrades to pro-
tect the institution’s good name. Even if the military court handed down a
resounding sentence, in their opinion, it would inevitably be less severe than
that of a civil court. On the other hand, there was some concern that the
alternative Alfonsín had proposed could prevent other trials. Increasingly,
people clamored for a political trial. In response to the signing of the presi-
dential decree ordering the trial of the military government leaders, Hebe de
Bonafi ni remarked that “a political trial is essential.” Although she considered
the presidential decision positive, she objected to the fact that parts of its
contents “are not very clear, like the need for evidence in order to prove the
crimes” (Clarín, 15 December 1983).
All of the movement’s organizations demanded that a special bicameral
committee be formed to investigate events associated with the repression.
Several legislators echoed this demand, including some from Alfonsín’s own
party, who promised to work on this initiative. Th e argument as to the
usefulness of this strategy was that if legislators began an investigation, the
military would be more likely to hand over documentation on what had hap-
pened, given that Congress was invested with authority as the people’s repre-
sentatives. In a context of democratic euphoria, this argument was strongly
persuasive, as it included the possibility of representing—in this case, “repre-
senting” was often understood as a synonym of “embodying”—the people’s
will. At the same time, it drew on the republican ideal of the division of
powers, a division that was very signifi cant for the public opinion, given the
unprecedented emphasis on the Constitution at this time.

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 83
Th is bicameral committee was carefully planned in conversations between
all of the organizations, members of the executive, and legislators from diff er-
ent parties. While the military justice option made a conviction doubtful, the
fi ndings of a congressional investigation could lead to punishment. Th is was
one of the outcomes of the political trial the human rights movement sought:
a form of punishment that, though outside the scope of the courts, remained
within the scope of the law. Th e goal was a decision that was legal—because
it fell within the realm of the powers of Congress—and legitimate, that is,
drawing on ethical values such as justice, popular sovereignty, or human
rights, which were prominent at the time.
One of the fi rst acts of the government was to ask Congress to amend the
Military Code. Approved immediately, this amendment granted the military
tribunal of the armed forces jurisdiction to prosecute military personnel. Yet
during the congressional vote, legislators were able to include in the law an
automatic civil courts appeal for the decisions made in military courts, and
to establish the fi gure of “atrocious, aberrant crimes” that could not be pro-
tected by the principle of authority or due obedience. A few months later, in
response to inaction by the military courts, the Buenos Aires federal court
took over the case against the members of the three military juntas. Th e his-
torical trial of the former commanders took up most of 1985.
In terms of policies to clarify what had happened, President Alfonsín’s
fi rst measure was to form the National Commission on the Disappeared
(CONADEP). Th e creation of this commission instead of a bicameral
committee sparked tensions and problems. Th e president had summoned
important fi gures to form this offi cial committee, which was tasked with
investigating the crimes committed under dictatorship. Some of the most
renowned members of the human rights organizations were asked to partici-
pate. Alfonsín’s decision initially provoked bewilderment, which then devel-
oped into disparate stances: reluctant acceptance, a desire to collaborate, and
outright rejection. Th is is how Fernández Meijide remembered the debate on
the topic at the APDH:
At some point, we held a meeting at the assembly and the discussion began [with
the APDH members who had been asked to join CONADEP]. Th e response
we got from people we respected and loved … was basically this: if what they
wanted was to mop the fl oor with us, they had sure hired a poor cleaning staff . I
remember asking Marshall Meyer,27 Jaime de Nevares,28 and [Carlos] Gattinoni29
if they had considering resigning, and they said no. So we said, “All right, gentle-
men, it’s over.” Th at’s it. Now we have to make a decision. Th is committee has to
give the most we can get from it. (Fernández Meijide, CEDES, 1989)
Other organizations reacted diff erently. When Adolfo Pérez Esquivel,
the SERPAJ representative, was asked to be president of CONADEP, he

84 • Th e Struggle for the Past
consulted with the other organizations. Since he placed conditions on his
participation, his name was ultimately struck from the list (CEDES, 1990).
SERPAJ’s position was clear: it was against leaving justice in the hands of the
military, but it was not against handing over the documentation it had gath-
ered to CONADEP.
Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo was the group most radically opposed
to the government’s strategy for addressing the issue of the disappeared.
Disappointed because the government had not heard its initial demands, the
group took a strong stance against the process, although most of its members
ultimately gave testimony and collaborated in the investigations, sharing
photographs and other materials that served as evidence of the disappear-
ances. Th e rallies organized by the Mothers around this time stressed the slow
pace of justice and the lack of the people’s participation in the investigations
and work to prepare for upcoming trials. In September 1984, Mothers of
the Plaza de Mayo did not join the march held to escort the delivery of the
CONADEP report, announcing that they did not know what the report
contained. Th e Mothers continued to demand that a joint Senate-House
committee be formed.
As CONADEP moved forward with its tasks, a confl ict arose between the
members representing the position of the executive, who thus intended to
send the results of their investigations to the military courts, and those rep-
resenting the human rights movement, who insisted that civil courts handle
the case. In a close vote, the commission’s members resolved to hand over
the report to the executive (Crenzel 2011). Th is apparently minor incident
reveals some of the unresolved issues of the process of reexamining the past
during the fi rst years of the transition. On the one hand, it demonstrates that
the government’s policy on the past needed the approval—or, at the very
least, had to take into consideration—the position of human rights organi-
zations. It is also indicative of the strength of the movement: a commission
formed by the executive was able to pass a resolution that went against the
president’s own strategy.
In fact, the position each organization adopted with regards to how to
unveil the truth did not line up with its stance on the pathways to justice.
In other words, despite the fact that some of the APDH members and the
MEDH had joined CONADEP, and despite the fact that almost all of the
members of human rights organizations collaborated in one way or another
with the commission’s activities, the movement throughout 1984 continued
to protest the amendment to the Military Justice Code.
Social movements are characterized by internal diversity and a wide range
of strategies, tactics, action plans, and organizational practices. A social
movement is never synonymous with a single institution or organization. On
the contrary, social actors expressing demands and protests in diverse ways

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 85
conform a social movement when they confront a common opponent and
take a critical—though often implicit—stance vis-à-vis the dominant socio-
political system. From this perspective, a structural reading of the human
rights movement indicates that when the movement confronted a unifi ed,
visible opponent—such as the dictatorial government—its actions were more
unifi ed and coherent. At the time of the transition, its diverse internal goals
and strategies allowed it to make headway on several fronts simultaneously.
And although these did not respond to strategic, rational decisions, the dif-
ferences proved eff ective in terms of collective action. In this regard, dissent
and internal confl ict are not necessarily negative. Organizations like Mothers
of the Plaza de Mayo were harshly critical of the government, especially
the executive, and opted to let their disagreements play out in the public
sphere. Other organizations with similar objectives (APDH, MEDH, CELS)
seized on the opportunity to participate in decision-making and challenge
the executive’s strategies from within, resorting to public mobilization only
as a secondary resource. In short, what was initially designed as a summit of
public personalities tasked with guaranteeing a set of reliable data became a
sui generis prosecutor that ended up substantiating the evidence that would
later be presented in civil courts. In that task, they resorted to the most strin-
gent legal criteria constructed over a long experience of gathering data, hear-
ing testimonies, and fi ling lawsuits (Crenzel 2011).
A massive rally gathered to stand by as the CONADEP report was deliv-
ered to President Alfonsín. Political parties and a wide range of social orga-
nizations mobilized for the event, as did human rights organizations (but
not, as described above, the Mothers). As Leis notes (1989: 42), most of
the people who came to the rally did so on their own, out of a belief in the
importance of human rights, not because they belonged to a given organiza-
tion. According to the newspapers, there were some fi fty thousand people
present.
Th e investigations entered a new phase after the CONADEP report was
delivered. For the human rights cause, the commission had established the
burden of proof, gathering and systematically organizing a massive set of
documents that would play a decisive role in the civil courts phase of the trial
of the members of the military juntas. Th e publication of the book summa-
rizing the report, Nunca más, and the airing of a television program with the
same name had an enormous impact on public opinion. Th e impact on the
high ranks of the armed forces was immediate, as they became increasingly
concerned about their fate. Against the wishes of the human rights move-
ment, the government decided to strike the list of the military personnel
