referred to by name from the CONADEP report. It also created a new offi ce,
the Undersecretariat for Human Rights, in charge of continuing the investi-
gations, sending the results to the courts, and acting as the liaison between

86 • Th e Struggle for the Past
the president and the human rights organizations. Th e new offi ce met with
immediate and harsh criticism from almost every organization.30 Th is put the
executive and the human rights movement at odds with each other, and their
relations would be further strained with the passage of the Final Stop (Ley de
Punto Final) and Due Obedience (Ley de Obediencia Debida) Acts.
Th e movement organizations all supported the trial of the high command-
ers in federal court that began in April 1985.31 Th is consensus rested on the
symbolic potential of seeing the nine off enders sitting before members of the
judiciary. However, representatives of all organizations concurred that this
trial should be considered only the fi rst stage of a process that should reach
“the very last torturer,” to cite a slogan Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo used on
several occasions.
All organizations agreed on framing the human rights issue within a con-
text of a broader social confl ict. Nonetheless, not even the most extreme
demands by Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo called for punishing anyone
other than those who had planned, overseen, or directly participated in acts
of repression, e.g. military personnel, members of law enforcement agencies,
para-police forces, and other personnel such as military chaplains and doc-
tors. In other words, criminal punishment was reserved for those directly
responsible; those indirectly responsible,32 in the movement’s view, should be
dealt with at the political level and in the inevitable “judgment of history.”
At organizations like the APDH and CELS, the idea of justice was closely
associated with the rigorous application of existing law. Th e political conno-
tations of the demand for settling accounts with the past had to be handled
separately. Th is distinction between the legal and the political realms did not
exist in groups like Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo, where ideological and
political connotations and the demand for justice tended to blur together.
Why would that be the case? Th e depth of the demand for justice for-
mulated by Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo had to match the personal loss
that motivated them to act. Th e justice and punishment they called for were
rooted in the material act of the disappearance. Th e label “guilty” applied to
those involved, to those who could have stopped it from occurring but did
not, to those who could have helped but did not. Th e Mothers did, however,
attribute a “historical” guilt and responsibility to politicians, union members,
priests, journalists, and similar fi gures who had the power to infl uence deci-
sions made at the highest levels. Th ese individuals, indirectly responsible for
what had happened, would endure the “judgment of history.” Th e justice of
the courts—earthly justice—was reserved for those who had committed spe-
cifi c crimes. Th e circle of the guilty thus encompassed anyone who had had
physical contact with the disappeared: those who had kidnapped, tortured,
and murdered them, and those who had given orders to this eff ect, who were
directly responsible and guilty. Th e scope of who was considered guilty would

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 87
change in the twenty-fi rst century when civilians who had collaborated with
the regime on many diff erent levels began to be indicted and prosecuted.
Th is is when the term used to refer to the regime was modifi ed to dictadura
cívico-militar (civilian-military dictatorship) in order to emphasize the sup-
port that civilians provided to the dictatorship.
Th e Trial and Beyond
Held in 1985, the trial of the former commanders of the juntas shifted the
focus of attention—and the scene of the confl ict—from the executive branch to
the judicial. Undoubtedly, the struggle against impunity for the human rights
violations committed under dictatorship reached a peak during those months
of 1985. With all the formalities and rituals surrounding the legal process, the
judicial branch took center stage at the institutional level: victims became “wit-
nesses”; those who committed acts of repression, “defendants”; and the political
actors, “observers” of the work of the judges. Th e judges, in turn, represented
“neutral” authorities who made decisions based on legitimate, pre-established
rules. From a legal perspective, the case for the prosecution was a daunting one,
since criminal legislation on homicide had to be used to judge people who may
not have personally killed anyone or given an order to kill specifi c individuals
by name, but had organized and ordered the kidnapping, torture, death, and
disappearance of thousands of people. Th e prosecutor’s strategy was to present
evidence that pointed to a systematic plan involving the same method of illegal
detention, torture, and disappearance all across the country.
Building the evidence was no easy task. Th e victims’ testimony here
proved critical, since no military records or fi les were available. Th is meant
acknowledging the victims’ rights to speak. Th e testimony, however, had to
be presented in keeping with what constituted legal evidence. What could
not be shown—the act of aggression—had to be recounted, but in pre-
cise, controlled terms so that what the victim was relaying could be veri-
fi ed. Physical wounds, for example, constitute evidence, but the same does
not apply to feelings or suff ering, which cannot be measured or included.
Similarly, the beliefs or ideologies of witnesses were excluded, thus depoliti-
cizing the confl ict and polarization that led to the coup. When a testifying
witness was overcome by emotion, the judges would call for a recess until
he or she had calmed down. Th is intermittent pattern had a very specifi c
eff ect: the implicit message was that the experience, in its totality, could not
be narrated, and certainly could not be heard. Th e trial was video recorded,
and television broadcasted three minutes of each day’s proceedings, without
sound (Feld 2002).
After fi ve months of testimonies by more than eight hundred witnesses—
people who overcame the enormous fear and diffi culty of recounting humil-

88 • Th e Struggle for the Past
iating experiences—the federal court sentenced General Jorge Rafael Videla
and Admiral Emilio Massera to life in prison; General Roberto Viola to sev-
enteen years in prison; Admiral Armando Lambruschini to eight years; and
Brigadier Orlando R. Agosti to three years, nine months. For lack of con-
clusive evidence, the court absolved the members of the third military junta
(1979–83): General Leopoldo Galtieri, Admiral Jorge Anaya, and Brigadiers
Basilio Lami Dozo and Omar Graffi gna.
Th e verdicts dealt a blow to the human rights movement. On the day of
the sentencing, some of the Mothers participated in an improvised march to
denounce the injustice of the sentences and to emphasize the need to avoid
future amnesties and legal guarantees of impunity. In the midst of the demo-
cratic enthusiasm during transition, many imagined stronger punishment for
the military, despite the fact that there was no precedent for trials of this sort
in Latin American history. Th e disappointment was thus enormous.33
Once the defendants had been sentenced, the rift between the human rights
organizations and the executive grew even larger. Many political protagonists
involved in the case believed that, following this trial, the military would con-
sider any additional punishment unreasonable. However, the federal court’s
controversial ruling opened the doorway for new court cases, a ruling that
contradicted the executive’s strategy, which expected to bring closure and
settle accounts with the past through the trial of the military commanders.
Th e possibility of further trials led to unrest in the armed forces, with a
string of military uprisings in 1986 and 1987, forcing the administration to
take steps to limit them. As part of the Final Stop Act passed in December
1986, a deadline was set for fi ling court cases related to crimes under dic-
tatorship. In response, the human rights movement focused its action on
the judiciary, pushing to ensure that it act strongly and decisively given the
narrow freedom of action the act permitted. Th e passage of the act, para-
doxically, thus led to a wave of prosecutions across the country in the early
months of 1987, as victims and family members raced to meet the deadline
established by the law. Popular pressure, and the eff orts by provincial delega-
tions of human rights organizations, were important factors in encouraging
victims and family members to move forward with their cases. Th e image of
gravitas and effi ciency conveyed during the Buenos Aires federal court’s trial
of the former commanders had set an example that was hard to overlook.
Th ough the human rights movement had blamed many of the members of
the judiciary—often not without reason—of antidemocratic ideologies, these
same judges now worked speedily to issue summons for as many suspected
members of the military and security forces as possible.
In 1987, in an additional attempt to assuage the military, the Due
Obedience Act was passed. Th is limited those accountable to the highest

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 89
ranks of the armed forces, thus exonerating or granting amnesty to the major-
ity of military personnel. Th ere were two additional military insurrections in
1988 and a fi nal one in December 1990, after Carlos Menem had already
been sworn in as Argentina’s new president. Th ese fi nal uprisings revealed a
change in course: if military dissatisfaction had been previously tied to gov-
ernment policy on human rights violations under dictatorship, it was now
owed to internal confl icts within the military.
President Menem adopted a strategy aimed at separating these two dimen-
sions of the “military issue.” First, in 1989, he extended presidential pardons
to free part of the military personnel convicted of human rights violations
under dictatorship, misconduct during the Malvinas War, and their involve-
ment in the 1986 and 1987 uprisings. A year later, the president took this
strategy a step further, pardoning all those who were still in prison, including
the military commanders convicted in 1985 and some of the former guer-
rilla leaders. Th e pardons, however, did not extend to the carapintadas,34 i.e.
the military personnel who had participated in the most recent uprising.
Menem’s strategy was clear: to pardon past crimes, and to concentrate pun-
ishment of the military in any disobedience or insurrection in the present or
future (Acuña and Smulovitz 1995).
Although the rallies organized against the passage of the Due Obedience
Act under President Alfonsín and the subsequent pardons by President
Menem attracted massive numbers of supporters, the human rights move-
ment experienced these as defeats. Th ese steps backward, however, did not
erase the social impact of the Nunca más report and the trials, two mile-
stones that connected the transition with the ethical and social recognition
of human rights as basic rights for a good number of Argentines. Similarly,
the trial of the military leadership continued to serve as a case that showed
what a state with an active rule of law could accomplish. Some human rights
activists refl ected:
Th e report [Nunca más] and the dramatic television documentary it inspired
have been critical to creating awareness about this topic. No one within the
organizations was satisfi ed with the sentences in the former commanders’
trial, yet most of us understand that this was a transcendental, historical event.
(Mignone 1991: 159–61)
I lived the trial as a witness, as an activist, and as an actor. And from all of these
perspectives … I think that the trial was a decisive moment in the life of the
country. In terms of consolidating ethical values, I believe it was indispensable,
as much or more so than recovering the electoral process. In my view, it was
even more successful than CONADEP at getting the majority of society to
climb into the time tunnel. (Norberto Liwski, CEDES, 1990)

90 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Interlude, Memories, Oblivions, Returns, New Horizons
In the early 1990s, the human rights movement suff ered undeniable setbacks.
First, the failed attempt to take over the military barracks at La Tablada,35 in
which several militants of the human rights movement were involved, placed
organizations in a highly vulnerable position. A few months later, despite massive
street demonstrations against his decision, President Carlos Menem issued presi-
dential pardons to several high-profi le perpetrators. Th e pardons drained activists
and militant of their capacity and energy, an energy that had previously seemed to
be inexhaustible. I was no stranger to that climate of defeat and disappointment.
Periods like these, however, can be productive from an intellectual and emotional
point of view, insofar as they encourage us to refl ect on and review ideas, convic-
tions, and feelings that were obstinately held.
At that time, a small group of colleagues and I developed a collective research
project to study the political and social impact of the transition process, especially
the meaning of the trial of the former commanders. Th e result was a book pub-
lished shortly after (Acuña et al. 1995). I did not choose to study memories but
bumped into them in the context of that project. I was studying the human rights
movement, and during the transition the movement began to talk about memory,
with a slogan that was central at the time, “remember not to repeat.” I wanted
to understand what that strong reference to memory involved, and this was the
starting point of my exploration of the subject.
Th e theoretical and comparative search was for clues and frameworks that
could contribute to understanding what was going on in Argentina. No simple
idea or slogan could serve to disentangle the complexities of people remembering
and forgetting, silencing and expressing, of amnesias and amnesties. Th e next
section of this chapter, which presents a more general refl ection on the subject of
memory, was written at that time (the early 1990s) and shows the questions and
issues that were on my mind then. I resume the narrative tone to refer to the ava-
tars of the human rights movement afterward.
A Refl ection Anchored in Time and Memory36
Th e tensions and pressures that crisscross the human rights movement—the
call for political pragmatism, the urgent demands of non-oblivion and con-
stant remembering—are, in fact, impossible to resolve or overcome. Is it pos-
sible that the mandate of “non-oblivion” is, deep down, incompatible with
the rationale of politics, where something must always be forgotten?
In a text analyzing amnesty in ancient Greek politics and the calls for non-
oblivion implicit in the reiterated use of pain in tragedy and myths, Loraux
(1989) notes the contradiction between the logics of politics and the logics of

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 91
mourning. Presenting this contrast here, magnifi ed and nuanced, allows for
a refl ection on the contradictions inherent to the human rights movement in
Argentina.
Politics is to act as if everything were fi ne. As if nothing had happened. Neither
confl ict nor murder nor ill-feelings (nor resentment). Of politics, then, which
would start where vengeance stops. (Loraux 1998: 91)
In Greece, and perhaps everywhere, the clamor for politics involves an inter-
diction on remembering misfortunes and the promise (or oath) not to. Th e
call for oblivion, however, is not easy to implement. On the other hand, non-
oblivion is rooted in a victim’s wrath and mourning, leading them to demand
revenge. Wrath and pain emerge in the tragedy.
It is left to the citizen-spectators gathered in the theater to guess what, for the
city, is the ultimate danger in this anger that does not forget, because it is the
worst enemy of politics: anger as mourning makes the ills it cultivates “grow”
assiduously, and it is a bond that tightens itself until it resists all untying.
(Loraux 1998: 98)
Th e introduction to fi ctional and symbolic mediations in the mechanisms of
remembering—diaper/scarves, cut-out silhouettes of missing bodies, holding
hands, masks, signs—responds to the need to bring some type of “closure” to
the traumatic experience. In Greece, the tragedy-fi ction was a response to the
fact that “the Athenian people made it known that they could not bear to see
anything that aff ected them painfully” (Loraux 1998: 86). Perhaps it could
be argued that Argentine society is still immersed in the “traumatic experi-
ence” of disappearances, without having achieved a meaningful elaboration,
and with a symbolization process that is still very limited and tied directly to
the pain. It has not yet been possible to drive out the terror or the sense of
doom, so as to begin to surmount the realism of experience.
Th e process by which victims both individual and collective recover from
social catastrophes requires acknowledging and naming its gaps and voids:
Th e trauma suff ered in social catastrophes obliterates trust and, what is worse,
turns victims into strangers within a history they cannot make their own. …
External memory, the collective memorial, the history that must fi nd meaning,
can protect against the resurgence of the horror, against repetition and … off er
some supports to utter, with borrowed words, something of their own truth.
(Kaes 1991: 162–63)
Since it is inspired by wrath, non-oblivion contradicts the political principles
that establish the basis for institutional memories: “We have here a female

92 • Th e Struggle for the Past
model of memory, which the cities try to confi ne within the anti- (or ante-)
political sphere” (Loraux 1998: 98)
A meaningful topic comes into view here: gender diff erences in the human
rights movement. Th e symbols of pain—Mothers and Grandmothers of the
Plaza de Mayo—are female; the political organizations are male. Th e inter-
pretation of male and female roles in the human rights movement does not,
however, coincide with the divisions between organizations. In contrast, and
in keeping with the description provided by Gilligan (1982), gender diff er-
ences take on a complex dynamic of moral conceptions anchored in notions
of rights (among males) and notions of responsibility (among women),
including caring for others.
After many years, I continue to refl ect on the past from a gender perspective. Th e
disparities between men and women in the human rights movement are per-
fectly clear. Th e majority of the activists and leaders of the groups of the “directly
aff ected” were women, while men held more formal and institutional roles, even
if they themselves were also directly aff ected (including Emilio Mignone, Augusto
Conte, and others). Mothers, Grandmothers, and Relatives were in the hands of
women; men ran the APDH, MEDH, and CELS. Furthermore, when the state
took charge of human rights, the disparities became even starker: there was only
one woman among the thirteen members of CONADEP; only one female secre-
tary among the fi ve in charge of day-to-day tasks (Graciela Fernandez Meijide,
who took the depositions while the male secretaries dealt with documentation,
procedures, and legal and administrative matters). And when the time came for
the trial of the former, all-male commanders, the six judges were men, as were
the lawyers for the prosecution and defense. No one questioned this status quo; no
feminist movement came out to demand parity or even some degree of representa-
tion. I am sure the situation would be very diff erent now.
Th e implications of this topic go beyond the immediacy of the lived experi-
ence and the pain of still-bleeding wounds. Th e ethical reconstruction of
society is anchored in the wound of state terrorism, yet offi cial state policy
refuses to acknowledge this experience at the institutional level. As a result,
the respect for those aff ected and their memory can, paradoxically, terrify
and paralyze the younger generations, i.e. those who did not live the expe-
rience in the fl esh. Bearing the pain and the memory as a victim or direct
witness grants symbolic power and authority based on the monopoly of the
meanings and contents of truth and memory. At the same time, this power
can thwart intergenerational memory transmission by not giving the new
generations permission to reinterpret the meaning of the transmitted experi-
ences in their own terms and historical circumstances. In the long run, this
becomes dangerous: only as part of an active and dynamic process of ongoing

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 93
reinterpretation can the recollection and memories of historical events be
meaningfully incorporated and contribute to building a new culture and col-
lective identity.
In this regard, there are two historical dangers: the oblivion and emptiness
that politics proposes and its counterpart, insistent and ritualized repetition
of the sinister and traumatic past/tragedy, without symbolic transformation.
It constantly reappears, hindering the construction of new subjects and new
meanings.
I return to the fi rst person: the last two paragraphs were and are central to my
thinking, twenty-fi ve years ago and now. In the early 1990s, the text I wrote
about the history of the human rights movement included this fi nal refl ection. It
was ready to be published, and I decided not to publish it. Th ese were the early
years of the Carlos Menem administration, when the press and a good part of
the political spectrum attacked the human rights movement. In part, this was
a response to the failed takeover of La Tablada regiment that revived the specter
of the armed struggle. In part, it was an attempt to present an image of a calm
and normal society that had already gotten over the “crazy” vociferous Mothers
and their allies. And exactly at that moment I was going to talk about attempts
to “monopolize” the story and the truth! It was not a good political moment to
say this in Argentina. Yet once I had come to such a conclusion and felt the need
to express my caveats, I could not omit those from my texts. Th e risk was that my
argument could be twisted by the reactionaries. Ultimately, I decided to keep the
text unpublished for a few years, until the political climate changed (Jelin 1995).
Clearly, this was an exercise in self-censorship.
From the Pardon to the Repeal
of the Due Obedience Act (1990–2001)
President Menem’s pardons came as a blow to the human rights movement.
Rallies to protest the president’s actions initially attracted many, but later
the social activity surrounding human rights claims fell into the shadows.
During the fi rst half of the 1990s, the movement’s public presence was mini-
mal; there were few rallies, scarce press coverage, and a negligible presence
in the public sphere. After the hyperinfl ation of 1989, economic policies to
control infl ation and the pegging of the Argentine peso to the U.S. dollar
were at the top of Argentina’s public agenda. Issues associated with human
rights attracted little attention, though this was only temporary. It could be
argued that the human rights movement was “hibernating” during the fi rst
few years of the 1990s, while new types of social expression—and new state
responses—were brewing.

94 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Under the human rights policy introduced by President Menem’s adminis-
tration, the victims of violations during dictatorship received fi nancial repara-
tions. In strategic terms, the goal was to minimize the political costs of the
highly unpopular pardons.37 Th e fi rst presidential decree on fi nancial repara-
tions in 1991 benefi ted anyone who had been illegally detained or detained
“by executive order.” In 1994, this was extended to parents, children, or heirs
of those disappeared or killed as a consequence of state repression. Several bills
to extend benefi ts to those forced into exile also circulated in Congress, though
none became law. Th ese measures refl ected international guidelines and, more
specifi cally, the recommendations of the Inter-American Commission on
Human Rights (Guembe 2006). At the same time, the implementation of an
individualized fi nancial reparations program was ideologically consistent—or
manifested an “elective affi nity”—with the dominant (neoliberal) visions of
the Menem administration. With its focus on economic costs and benefi ts,
this vision splintered social ties to the benefi t of individualism.
At the beginning of the implementation of the reparations program, there
was little public debate surrounding these governmental measures. Each indi-
vidual or family aff ected made its own decision on fi nancial reparations and
acted accordingly. In some cases, the money received in reparations was used
to fi nance commemorative projects (memorials, literary and artistic competi-
tions, etc.) or publish books on the topic, but these were individual projects
rather than part of organized collective action.
In the human rights movement, not everyone was in favor of fi nancial rep-
arations. Some of the “directly aff ected” feared that economic compensation
would tacitly mean renouncing their demand for justice. International back-
ing for this type of measure, however, led fi nancial reparations to be seen as
a legitimate demand meriting the state’s response. Nonetheless, the Mothers
of the Plaza de Mayo Association systematically opposed fi nancial reparations
because it considered them “an act of prostitution.”38
Another area of constant activity, though sluggish and unnoticed during
the fi rst years of the 1990s, was legal action both within Argentina and
abroad. Th us, in March 1990, a court in France sentenced former military
captain Alfredo Astiz to life imprisonment (in absentia) for the disappear-
ance of two French nuns. In Argentina, Grandmothers of the Plaza de Mayo
were responsible for the most notable legal victories of this period, recover-
ing children who had been kidnapped or born in captivity. Th e search for
these children involved relentless work by individuals and networks, who
followed up on clues and reports. Highly committed activists and groups
were engaged in this sensitive and silent task. Since the time of their creation,
the National Commission for the Right to Identity (Comisión Nacional por
el Derecho a la Identidad, established in 1992) and the National Genetic
Databank (Banco Nacional de Datos Genéticos) have been involved in each

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 95
case (Sikkink 2008). Proof of parentage and the restitution of one’s identity
are legal acts that almost always attract a good deal of media coverage, bring-
ing to light that work done in silence.
As mentioned in chapter 2, Argentina was shaken at the political and
cultural levels by a former navy offi cer’s confession on the tenth anniversary
of the trial of the military leaders in 1995. Th e offi cer described a particular
method of disappearance: drugging prisoners, loading them onto military
planes, and then dropping them, unconscious, into the waters of Río de la
Plata during fl ight. Th ough many were aware this method existed—several
bodies had washed up on the coasts of both Argentina and Uruguay—it was
the fi rst time someone directly involved in the repression had confessed to
it and described the modus operandi (Verbitsky 1995). Th e offi cer simply
acknowledged the truth of what had occurred but expressed no regret in his
statements. Th e mass media, especially television, picked up on the confes-
sion, and these clips of information became part of the daily news. Th e media
storm resulted in an offi cial apology by General Martín Balza, then head of
the army, who admitted that the army had committed crimes and asked for
the population’s forgiveness.
Th at year, a new human rights group made its public appearance: Children
for Identity and Justice and Against Oblivion and Silence (Hijos por la Identidad
y la Justicia contra el Olvido y el Silencio, or HIJOS). It was made up of the sons and
daughters of the disappeared, most of whom were just over the age of twenty.
Th e presence of young people clamoring for human rights would change the
way protests and demands unfolded (Bonaldi 2006).
Th e year 1996 marked the twentieth anniversary of the coup. Over the
course of the year, particularly during the month of March, multiple com-
memorations took place. Th ere were emotionally charged stories; a chance
for victims to express what they never said before; the surprise on the part
of listeners to hear things they had not known; and the acknowledgment
of what many had been unable to see or recognize when it was happening.
Human rights organizations led the commemoration initiatives with the
support of a wide range of other social organizations, yet there was scarce
participation on the part of state institutions.
From that moment on, human rights violations under dictatorship
again captured public attention. Internationally, courts were taking action:
in April 1996, Spanish judge Baltasar Garzón began proceedings to pros-
ecute Argentine military personnel accused of crimes under the dictatorship
(Anguita 2001). Th e Spanish courts later took on the case of Chile, leading to
the arrest of dictator Augusto Pinochet in London in 1998. Th e work of the
Spanish courts continued over several years, creating confl icts between Spain
and Argentina over issues related to jurisdiction when Argentina refused to
extradite the accused.39

96 • Th e Struggle for the Past
In the Argentine judicial system, Grandmothers of the Plaza de Mayo
initiated proceedings for the crime of child abduction during the dictator-
ship in December 1996. Th is has had important repercussions. Th e crimes
of child kidnapping and forged identities have no statutory limitations, given
that it is a crime perpetuated over the course of the victim’s whole life (that is,
until s/he discovers his/her true identity), thus allowing the highest-ranking
offi cials of the military dictatorship to be indicted. In 1998, former general
Jorge R. Videla, former admiral Emilio Massera, and other top offi cers were
arrested and prosecuted for their involvement in these crimes.
At the same time, in response to various petitions fi led during previ-
ous years, hearings in the truth trials (juicios por la verdad) began in La
Plata in 1998. In response to several cases presented to the Inter-American
Commission on Human Rights, the commission established the right to
knowing the truth about the fate of disappeared family members and the
identifi cation of their remains, even in cases in which it was not possi-
ble to prosecute or convict those responsible due to pardons and amnesties
(Andriotti Romanin 2013).
Human rights organizations were undeniably important in their role as
plaintiff s in cases against the state. In addition to fi ling lawsuits, activists
from human rights organizations systematically organized other initiatives,
from site-specifi c practices and commemorative markers such as monuments,
parks, and museums to the recovery of clandestine detention centers that had
been demolished or put to another use, etc., as well as the discovery, recovery,
and digitalization of archives.
In each and every initiative, however, disagreements over strategy of the
various social groups working for human rights continued and even intensi-
fi ed. In the 1990s, there were diff erences and confl icts over what type of rela-
tionship to establish or agree to vis-à-vis the state. Th e stances varied between
groups like the Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo Association, which refused any
type of negotiations or ties to the state, to those who took on one of many
roles as state offi cials. Diff erences and confl icts would persist in the follow-
ing decade, though there was an important change in both the government’s
attitude toward the movement and, accordingly, in the groups willing to col-
laborate with the administration.
Another source of diff erences among human rights groups over the years
is the interpretation and meaning attributed to the political confl icts and
violence of the 1970s. At the beginning of the transition, the dominant
framework was that of a terrorist state that committed human rights viola-
tions and crimes against humanity. While there was little debate on this, the
meanings associated with the “before” and the visions of the “after” were
open to interpretation. How could the meaning of the transformational proj-
ects that inspired the social and political struggles of the early seventies be

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 97
integrated to the narrative? How could continuities and diff erences be estab-
lished between the political repression of the dictatorship and the policies
of exclusion and economic marginalization of the 1990s? Th ese were open-
ended questions with no easy answer, given the short temporal and historical
distance from the processes analyzed.
Th e End of an Era?
On 6 March 2001, federal judge Gabriel Cavallo declared the Final Stop
Act (1986) and the Due Obedience Act (1987) “null and unconstitutional.”
Together, these laws had made it impossible to prosecute military personnel
responsible for repression during the military dictatorship, with the exception
of those accused of child abduction. Th ree years earlier, in 1998, Congress
had repealed the laws, but the repeal was not retroactive. Judge Cavallo’s
sentence, which was upheld by the federal court in November 2001, applied
to a specifi c case; however, once the Supreme Court stood by the lower court
rulings in 2005, it allowed hundreds (if not thousands) of cases of human
rights violations to be reopened. Besides representing a milestone, the text of
Judge Cavallo’s court ruling established a new legal precedent and a model
even beyond Argentina.
Th e courts’ recognition of the crimes committed had an immediate and
lasting political impact. A few days later, the strongly symbolic twenty-fi fth
anniversary of the coup d’état, on 24 March 2001, was commemorated. Two
areas of action converged on this anniversary: the labors of memory and
institutional justice. Th ough at times they appeared to take diff erent paths,
they had now come full circle. Justice and memory converged, and the dis-
tance between symbolic construction and institutional processes was erased.
Judge Cavallo’s ruling anticipated a change in the relationship between the
state and the human rights movement. When President Néstor Kirchner took
offi ce in 2003, the state began speaking about the topic and taking initiative.
First, starting in 2005, a new wave of trials began. Cases were fi led by rela-
tives of victims, with human rights organizations providing legal counsel and
support. Th e state also supported these actions, and often became a plaintiff .
Th e justice cascade that Sikkink reported internationally (Sikkink 2011)—a
cascade that had begun in Buenos Aires in the 1980s—had now multiplied at
the local level, as trials began in diff erent places across the country.40
Th e national government under President Néstor Kirchner and then under
President Cristina Fernández de Kirchner increasingly identifi ed with the his-
torical demands of the human rights movement, introducing a wide range of
memory initiatives and policies. Th e recovery of the former premises of the
Naval Mechanics School (Escuela de Mecánica de la Armada, or ESMA), the
site of the country’s largest and most notorious clandestine detention camp,

98 • Th e Struggle for the Past
was a visible action with strong symbolic weight, as was the policy of placing
markers at sites that had operated as clandestine detention centers, and the
declaration of 24 March as a national holiday.41 At the same time, leaders
of the human rights movement like Mothers president Hebe de Bonafi ni
and Grandmothers president Estela de Carlotto—both of whom had been
distanced and critical of state action before—now increasingly appeared at
government events and consistently made statements supporting state poli-
cies. Other fi gures such as Adolfo Pérez Esquivel, Nora Cortiñas, and the
Plaza de Mayo Founding Line, among others, maintained a critical distance
and greater independence from the state.
Th e number of government initiatives and the administration’s commit-
ment to the visibility of issues related to the country’s past under dictator-
ship—combined with the fi nancial support it provided for organizations and
the incorporation of movement activists to relevant state institutions—bring
up an almost classic quandary in the study of social movements. When a
movement’s actions result in the incorporation of its demands and agenda by
other institutions (in this case, the state), the movement often loses auton-
omy and strength. Th is is because social movements stand united when they
have a strong, unifi ed adversary. In this regard, the changing position of the
diff erent administrations has led the movement to oscillate, its drive dimin-
ishing when it has many political allies and increasing in the face of tough
new opponents.
I prepared this overview of the history of the human rights movement during
2016–17, shortly after Mauricio Macri became the president of Argentina, for
the original Spanish edition of this book (Jelin 2017a). It was clear then that
the incumbent government did not identify with the human rights movement’s
demand for “memory, truth, and justice.” Some government offi cials started speak-
ing of a “paradigm shift,” and some even referred to human rights work as dirty
business. Controversies about the meaning of the past intensifi ed once again in the
public sphere in Argentina. One side questioned whether there were in fact thirty
thousand disappeared. On the other side, accusations and court cases indicting
civilians for their collaboration in dictatorship led to a rethinking of how to refer
to the military regime: was it a military dictatorship or a civilian-military one?
Who vindicates the armed struggle, then and today? More silences were starting
to break: what does it mean to talk about four hundred missing LGBTIQ persons
who are not visible in existing records? So, a new number appeared in the public
sphere: thirty thousand plus four hundred. Can attempts to grant benefi ts—such
as house arrest—to judicially convicted repressors be justifi ed?
As I revise the text for the English publication, in January 2020, a new admin-
istration has just taken offi ce. Th is government states it is committed yet again to
settling accounts with the dictatorial past. In the midst of an unprecedented

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 99
economic recession and crisis, and with the unforeseen burden of the pandemic,
will it be able to handle the tasks this involves, in all its layers and levels?
Notes

that stage public events and present demands to the state related to the recognition of
violations occurred during the military dictatorship (1976–83). Th e positions of these
organizations on a broad and universal notion of “human rights” have varied over time,
and we will return to them in the last chapter of this book.
2. In fact, 1975 was the year in which violence, political instability, kidnappings, and mur-
ders rose astronomically. According to Graham-Yooll (1989), there were between 850
and 1,000 deaths from political violence in 1975 alone.

A founding member of the APDH and a human rights activist, he was kidnapped by the

dent of the World Council of Churches.

refers to relatives of victims of dictatorial repression. Usually the distinction is made

such as mothers and fathers, siblings, children, and, less often, marital partners.
6. A theologian and priest, Enzo Giustozzi was one of the founders of the APDH.

period. Th ese interviews are available in the Archivo de Historia Oral de la Argentina
Contemporánea, IIGG, Facultad de Ciencias Sociales, UBA. Th e verbatim quotes of
these interviews are referred to as CEDES, identifying the year in which each interview
was conducted.
9. Emilio Mignone was a lawyer and professor serving as rector of the Universidad Nacional

tion until his death in 1998.
10. A lawyer, Augusto Conte became a human rights activist after the disappearance of his
son. He was one of the founders of CELS. He was elected to Congress as a human rights

11. Th ere are many published testimonies on how relatives reacted to a disappearance
(Bonafi ni 1985; Melibovsky 1991; Mignone 1991; Ulla and Echave 1986; Herrera and
Tenembaum 1990; Giorgi 1990; among others). Since the founding of Memoria Abierta
in 1999, its archive of oral history interviews has incorporated a great number of testimo-
nies (www.memoriaabierta.org.ar). One example of in-depth research into the actions of
relatives over time can be seen in da Silva Catela (2001).

100 • Th e Struggle for the Past
12. A pediatrician and popular activist, Norberto Liwski was working with the poor at the
time of the coup. After being kidnapped and detained by the military, he became a
human rights activist upon his release, working at institutions dealing with the rights of
children.
13. On one district within La Matanza, see Dubois (2005).
14. Th e fi rst cases of recovered children, associated with Operation Condor, occurred in

adults) continue to make the news.
15. Some years later, in 1985, Mother Nora Cortiñas off ered a new interpretation of the
slogan: “‘Give them back alive’ is more than a slogan: it is both a desire and an accusa-
tion. Th ere is nothing crazy about it. Th ough it is painful to say, we Mothers know per-
fectly well that the majority of those disappeared had been murdered. Yet we believe that
for the Argentine people … saying ‘Give them back alive’ is the fairest thing we can do.
Because if they aren’t alive, then many people are responsible, and that is where the courts
must take action” (cited in Sondereguer 1985: 33).
16. Th e slogan “Con vida se los llevaron, con vida los queremos” (You took them alive, we
want them back alive) appeared in 1978, at one of the Mothers’ weekly rallies in Plaza
de Mayo. Th at particular week—as Argentina was hosting the soccer World Cup—the
Mothers gathered on the corner of Buenos Aires City Hall, because the square was over-
run by police. Several foreign physicians who were attending the 12th International
Conference on Cancer stood by the Mothers. “Th e doctors stood out front, facing off
with police, and from behind the doctors, a woman’s voice cried out, ‘You took them
alive, we want them back alive.’ Th en she yelled it again and all of us began to repeat it. …
It was nothing and yet, it was everything: it was an accusation that captured exactly what
we were feeling” (Bonafi ni 1985: 160).
17. Gorini (2006) provides a detailed overview of the juncture of the war and the stance of
the Mothers.
18. Published in April 1983, the “Final Document of the Military Junta on the War against

dictatorship. It claimed that the military had found itself “at war” and that, as in all wars,
there had been “mistakes and excesses.” Importantly, the report stated that “those named
on the lists of the disappeared … can be considered dead.” Th e document represented
the military’s attempt to declare its own self-amnesty with an eye to discouraging future
administrations from holding its personnel responsible for the crimes committed once
democracy was reinstated.

that the information should be provided by the offi cials so as to avoid “sensationalist
reporting by the press” (La Nación, 4 January 1984). I thank Claudia Feld for calling my
attention to this fact and providing me with this information.
20. After her son disappeared, Nora Cortiñas joined the Mothers. After the group split, she
became the president of Mothers Founding Line. She is a renowned human rights activ-
ist.

Explorations, Certainties, and Uncertainties • 101
information and testimony … persons belonging to the Armed Forces entered La Plata
cemetery, and perhaps others, at night, and personally buried the bodies without permit-
ting the intervention of the cemetery authorities’” (Cohen Salama 1992: 91).
22. Cohen Salama recounts and analyzes successive cases of identifying the remains of the

Forensic Anthropology Team (Cohen Salama 1992). He also presents a list of the some
fi fty cases covered by the press between December 1983 and May 1984 (Cohen Salama
1992: 75–85).
23. Th is report draws on Gorini (2008) and news reports of that time, including documents
reprinted in the magazine Puentes (2007). I thank Enrique Andriotti Romanin for shar-
ing his Mar del Plata press clippings from this period.
24. In 1986, the most emblematic organization within the human rights movement splin-
tered into two smaller groups: the Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo Association (Asociación

Plaza de Mayo—Línea Fundadora). Since then, the two groups continue to exist—and
march weekly at Plaza de Mayo—separately.
25. In a revealing anecdote, Fernández Meijide remembers visiting military barracks with the

guys there were spooked. Th ey let us see every last cell and even the pits … and we went
through every place and took pictures, and the victims and witnesses would come along
on these visits and say, ‘Th is. Here.’ Looking back, it’s like a dream. Offi cers would send
their subordinates to ask whether they were on the list, if they were accused of anything.
All were worried and afraid. I can’t believe we didn’t seize on that opportunity” (CEDES,
1989).

(Admiral Emilio Eduardo Massera), and air force (Orlando Ramón Agosti). General
Videla was appointed the de facto president. Th ere would be three additional tripartite
committees, all with this structure, between 1973 and 1983, though only three would
be indicted in the trial, since the fourth was in power during the fi nal months before the
democratic elections and was not deemed responsible for human rights violations.
27. A North American conservative rabbi and human rights activist, Marshall Meyer served
as a member of CONADEP.

29. A Methodist bishop and human rights activist, Enzo Gattinoni was one of the APDH

31. Th e trial in federal court took place as a response to the inaction of the military courts

manders.

the ranks of the disappeared—to the authorities.

102 • Th e Struggle for the Past
33. Fernández Meijide later recalled that on the day of the verdict, “I was attending a meet-

went into the conference the next day, some of our colleagues began to applaud. And
we stopped in our tracks and said, ‘Why are you applauding? Are you drunk?’ And they
replied: ‘You don’t appreciate what you have. Nothing satisfi es you—that’s the way it goes
with Argentines’” (CEDES, 1989).

Led by Lieutenant Colonel Aldo Rico, the insurgents seized control of the military bar-
racks at Campo de Mayo. After days of public protests and a face-to-face meeting with
President Alfonsín, the uprising ended peacefully.

part of an insurgent group, the Movement All for the Fatherland (Movimiento Todos por
la Patria, or MTP) (Hilb 2013).

memory while analyzing the history of the human rights movement up to that date.
37. According to public opinion surveys, during the 1980s nearly two-thirds of those inter-
viewed believed that the former military commanders should remain in jail. Following
the pardons issued in December 1990, also according to public opinion surveys, the
popularity of President Menem dropped signifi cantly.
38. According to the association, “whoever receives fi nancial reparations is prostituting him/
herself,” and receiving money was akin “to selling the blood of our children” (Guembe

39. In Italy, where military personnel from Southern Cone countries were also prosecuted,
seven Argentines were convicted (in absentia); four received life sentences, and three

40. According to the 2019 CELS annual report on human rights, 226 sentences had been
handed down since 2006; 915 of the defendants were convicted, and 144 were absolved

Chapter 4
MARKERS OF MEMORY
Dates, Places, Archives
8
As I have already mentioned, there were voices in the 1980s that started clamor-
ing for “memory.” At the time of the political transition, it was relatively easy
to recognize the role of the subjective and personal dimension of victims, family
members, activists, witnesses, and neighbors. Committed psychoanalysts provided
the clues to interpret the trauma and understand the silences. Yet beyond the per-
sonal feelings, the human rights movement had a collective expression in its public
activities. How do private emotions and feelings become public? What is the link
between subjective and intimate processes on the one hand and collective forms of
action on the other? My attention was focused on fi nding some clues to these and
similar questions.
I was closely following the practices of the human rights movement, which
involved reinforcing symbols, performing rituals of remembrance of the dictato-
rial past, and presenting a series of demands to the state. Th ere were attempts to
put up territorial markers, erect memorials, commemorate events and dates, and
recover documents and archives. Such actions, however, were loaded with the
feelings and emotions of the people involved. In retrospect, it is possible to see how
much the urban landscape of cities and towns in the region has changed: offi cial
territorial markers overlap with and complement societal marks on tiles, graffi ti,
murals, and other reminders of the recent past. We know that their existence is the
product of the will and the subjective energy of those who fought for them.
In 1998 we started the “Memories of Repression” program. Attracted and
galvanized by these public manifestations, where subjectivities turned into public
symbols and demands for institutional policies, we decided that the fi rst group of

104 • Th e Struggle for the Past
fellows would investigate the struggles and debates about commemoration dates
and emblematic places. Research on these issues in the countries of the Southern
Cone by the program’s fellows led to the fi rst three collective books of the series
“Memories of Repression,” on commemorations (Jelin 2002a), archives (da Silva
Catela and Jelin 2002), and territorial markers (Jelin and Langland 2003).
Gradually, I came to see these three topics as part of a general theme, that of
“memorialization policies.” A few years later (2007), the International Journal
of Transitional Justice was launched. I was invited to prepare a paper on memory
for the fi rst issue and decided to focus on memorialization policies in the Southern
Cone. Th e journal is published in South Africa and has a large international
and global scope. Th e invitation was an opportunity to disseminate the results of
refl ections and research on public memories developed in South America (Jelin
2007b). A couple of years later, at the request of Spanish colleagues, I prepared a
new version of that text (Jelin 2009).
In 2008, I spent several months in Berlin. I arrived with my baggage of per-
sonal experiences anchored in my family’s stories of the Shoah and the experience
of the Argentine dictatorship. I also carried my intellectual concerns and years of
work on memory. Berlin is saturated with markers of its past. One can wander
through the city with a geological mindset, as each new stratum of history pushes
and transforms the previous ones: the historical present of a “unifi ed” Germany
(a term not everyone agrees on), the vestiges and especially silences of the GDR,
the marks of the Nazi past, and the long history of the previous multiple pasts. I
walked the city during those months and wrote short chronicles of what I saw and
felt in my journeys. Th ere was no respite. Th e marks of memorialization policies
were in full swing everywhere. I was overcome by new questions, asking about the
opposition between literality and symbolization, the contrast between the person-
alization of each victim and the massive nature of violence and repression, whose
fi gures (in thousands, in millions) prevent any possibility of seeing or feeling each
case as unique. In my tours and visits, I paid special attention to the representa-
tions of these contrasts between personalization and massiveness, and between
literal and symbolic representations, in inscriptions, photographs, monuments.
I had the opportunity to put together a text about territorial memory markers
for a seminar in Quito, Ecuador. Th e year 2013 marked the thirty-fi fth anni-
versary of the designation of the historic center of that city as “World Heritage,”
a decision taken at the fi rst meeting of the UNESCO heritage committee. Th e
temporality of memory marks was again evident, contrasting the shorter-term
memories of the “recent” past I was studying and the centuries-old colonial past of
Quito, the one defi ned as heritage and patrimony. Parts of that presentation and
the ensuing publication (Jelin 2014b) are recovered in this chapter.
While preparing this book, I wonder what I would have said if someone had
asked me in the mid-1990s how memorialization policies and the corresponding
research fi eld would look in the future. In retrospect, I can hardly imagine what I

Markers of Memory • 105
Figure 4.1. Stolperstein (stumbling block), memorial blocks in Berlin.
Photograph by Elizabeth Jelin.
Figure 4.2. Memorial tile in Buenos Aires. Photograph by Elizabeth Jelin.

106 • Th e Struggle for the Past
would have answered. At that time, the topic was relatively new. Memories
linked to the Shoah were in full swing and would soon become a universal
trope. We had nothing similar in the region, yet the diverse scenarios of political
struggle expressed multiple demands for memorializing dictatorships, repression,
and violence. Social demands and state responses have not ceased since. Perhaps
these actions opened the fl oor for the expression of longer-lasting memories, those
of populations silenced and made invisible by the powerful, as was the case with
indigenous groups and their demands regarding the policies of bicentennial com-
memorations of independence in each country of the region.1
My concern with memorialization policies has an enduring core: the centrality
of social actors, especially victims; the subjectivity that permeates the initiatives;
the energy of those who push for markers. It is not so much about the material
objects or the repetitive rituals involved. It is more about the subjectivities that
end up deposited in materialities (or fail to do so). Each mark, each place, each
commemoration is the product of the human will. And it is that will and that
energy that continue to challenge me, as a person and as a researcher.
Th ere is something else, something very personal, in this refl ection. When I
pass by a memorial tile in Buenos Aires, or a Stolperstein in Berlin, I can’t step
on them. I almost always stop to look and read what they say. Also, though I go
by that place almost daily, I always stop at a tile in Buenos Aires in memory of
“Pechito” Ferreira, not a victim of dictatorship but a structural victim of gov-
ernment neglect. A very special type of homeless person, “Pechito” lived in front
of a bank branch with his two dogs, “Alberto Cortés” and “Nino Bravo,” on a
busy corner of the city. He was part of the neighborhood and chatted with local
residents, who attended to his needs and celebrated birthdays with him. On 30
August 2013 he was picked up by a city government van. He vanished, and every-
body in the neighborhood started inquiring about him. Finally, we learned that
he had died a few days later, with no explanation on the part of the authorities.
On the sidewalk that used to be his home, there is a memorial tile (similar to the
ones that are embedded for the disappeared) and a mural, where people leave mes-
sages so that he will not be forgotten.
***
In postdictatorial periods, social demands multiply for state institutions to
act vis-à-vis the past. While some demands are specifi cally institutional—
such as investigative commissions, trials of those responsible for the repres-
sion, guidelines for vetting and reinstating dismissed workers, and reparation
policies—there are also demands for memorialization policies. Anchored in
symbolic recognition and in forward-looking transmission initiatives, these
policies comprehend commemoration dates, territorial markers, museums,
and archives. Th e demands for memorialization are part of a broader fi eld of

Markers of Memory • 107
Figure 4.3. Neighborhood memorial for “Pechito” Ferreira, a homeless person, on the
block where he lived in Buenos Aires. Photograph by Elizabeth Jelin.
public policies and practices linked to settling accounts with the past; they
cannot be seen as autonomous or independent of the others. Th e develop-
ment of these processes at the institutional, symbolic, and subjective levels
reveal that the demands and policies of memorialization are an integral part
of the entreaties for “truth” and “justice.” In fact, the three words—truth, jus-
tice, and memory—are often used together, in slogans and in public events,
to refer to the full policy on dictatorial pasts.
Memorialization policies are the response that the state gives to those
social actors—generally led by victims and their family members—who
demand symbolic recognition through the materialities and materializations
of memories. Perhaps we could speak of a material culture of memories,

108 • Th e Struggle for the Past
one that unfolds in buildings, places, the itineraries of marches and rallies,
archives, documents, and virtual mobilizations on social media.
On Dates and Commemorations
A country’s public holiday schedule is a privileged space that allows the
past to be brought into the present. Public holidays contribute to building
and reinforcing the symbols of community and nation, and participating in
public commemoration rituals is a way of expressing feelings of belonging
to a political community and of reaffi rming collective identifi cations. As
they are part of school calendars, these dates are also vehicles for transmit-
ting these feelings to children and youth, fostering public actions and prac-
tices that, over time, become rituals with symbolic inscriptions. Th e annual
rhythm of commemorations—a repetitive one, though with changes from
one year to the next—reasserts the events, dates, and anniversaries that must
be remembered.
However, calendar markers do not automatically crystallize or have the
same meanings for everyone. Diff erent social actors give specifi c meanings to
these markers according to the circumstances and the political scenarios in
which they develop their strategies and projects.
How are certain dates incorporated to this calendar? Who fi ghts to achieve
this? What is the point? Th e social and political trajectory between the occur-
rence of the event and its offi cial commemoration is never harmonious or
calm. Insofar as there are diff ering social interpretations of the past, public
commemorations are a source of confl ict and debate. What dates should be
commemorated? Or perhaps a better question, who wants to commemo-
rate something? And what is it they want to commemorate? Furthermore,
the meaning of dates changes over time. Even if one vision or idea of the
past becomes crystallized and institutionalized, with its corresponding set of
meanings and rituals, new generations may well challenge that vision later
on, as new actors attempt to imbue it with new meaning.
How do these processes operate in the Southern Cone? As seen in chapter
2, when the military seized power in the 1970s, they emphasized their mis-
sion as the “saviors” of the nation, observing the continuities of their role
with the glories of the founding fathers in the early nineteenth century.2 Th eir
intervention was urgent, necessary to save the nation from external threats,
those of “international subversion” and “communist infi ltration.” In fact, at
the time of the coup, the military regimes revealed an active determination
for future commemoration. Th e military commanders projected themselves
into the future, and were very clear about the meanings of their actions and
of the commemorations they sought to impose. In Chile, just one year after

Markers of Memory • 109
the coup d’état, 11 September was commemorated with offi cial public cel-
ebrations; later, in 1981, the date was added to the country’s holiday calen-
dar as “National Liberation Day” (Candina Palomer 2002; Joignant 2007).
In Argentina, there were no public commemoration ceremonies during the
dictatorship. Th e military held their own commemorations in the barracks,
without civilians. Th e sole contact point between the military and the rest of
society was the “Message to the Argentine people,” in which the armed forces
exulted in the public service they were performing and gave their own version
of events. Th e message highlighted the role of the military as the savior of a
nation threatened by chaos and as the enemy of “subversion” (Lorenz 2002).
While the victors worked to instill their narrative, controversy simmered.
Alternative visions and interpretations were banned, censored, repressed,
silenced, or hidden in the private or family sphere. Little by little, diverse
channels for expression became available. During the moments of dictato-
rial regimes where repression was at its peak, performative acts were crucial.
Th ese signs—almost all of which were associated with women, like dressing
in black, visiting the cemetery, or walking the city streets in silence—revealed
an incipient yet timid process of rebuilding communities of belonging that
had been destroyed or threatened. It was diffi cult to repress signs of this sort.
Th e protagonists were social actors organized in the human rights movement
or scattered among a wide variety of popular protests aimed at producing
memories that would stand in for the military narrative. In their interpre-
tation of past and present, the emphasis was on repression, absences, and
suff ering. In Chile, these two contrasting meanings attributed to the date of
the coup led to violent street confrontations between Pinochet supporters
and the social forces against dictatorship. During the 1980s, Chile suff ered
septiembres rojos (red Septembers), months of blood and death, especially in
popular-sector neighborhoods. Th e pattern of street confrontations contin-
ued even after transition (1990), while controversies and battles over com-
memoration lasted for decades (Joignant 2007). In addition, since marches
and commemorations of the date were identifi ed with demands for human
rights, they became “a place or a ‘domain’ to protest against the political
system as a whole, and also a place to defend the legitimacy of multiple alter-
native projects” (Candina Palomer 2002: 46). Years after the transition, in
1998, the date was struck from the offi cial calendar,3 though the commemo-
rations, marches, and rituals continue on 11 September each year. In 2013,
the acts of commemoration for the fortieth anniversary of the coup d’état
attracted massive numbers of participants.
In Argentina, “the twenty-fourth” is a particularly important date of
observance. In response to the savior narrative of the armed forces, human
rights organizations developed a rival narrative of what took place on 24
March 1976. Since the transition (1984), these organizations have occupied

110 • Th e Struggle for the Past
the public space for commemoration, while the national government has
stayed silent. Commemorative events included a broad array of expressions,
all of which were tied to memory of dictatorship and its consequences: body
outlines representing the disappeared, murals, plays, rallies, marches, and the
diaper/scarves of the Mothers. Myriad books, documentary fi lms, television
specials, and other expressions presented and represented the voices of the
violence and suff ering of victims and their families (Lorenz 2002). As had
occurred in Chile, commemorative rallies in Argentina evolved into spaces
for numerous groups with diverse slogans and demands on the state, all of
which were set in the language of rights. Absent as an explicit actor from the
marches and mobilizations, the state was present as the symbolic interlocu-
tor through the public sites chosen for the gatherings. In Buenos Aires, the
Congress building and Casa Rosada, the seat of the executive branch, were
two such sites; in other cities, the meeting point for gatherings was also in
front of government buildings.
Th is is not to suggest that the public space was free from confl ict during
the commemorations of 24 March, yet these were not—as they were in
Chile—confrontations between those who defended dictatorship and demo-
cratic forces. In Argentina, the friction was both within the human rights
movement and between the movement and other social and political orga-
nizations. For many years, there were two separate demonstrations, since
the Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo Association (Asociación Madres de Plaza
de Mayo) did not join the central march in Buenos Aires. Furthermore,
since 1996, the massive gatherings of the twenty-fourth have been jointly
organized by human rights organizations and a large number of other social
organizations. Given the pluralistic nature of the actors involved, there are
disputes over the main messages to be conveyed and over the position of each
participating group. Th ese generally involve minor confrontations, which at
times have led some groups to withdraw. In short, the date and commemora-
tion may have dissimilar meanings even for those who are “on the same side,”
that is, diff erent groups whose identities and social visibility play out in this
space.
Th e state’s absence in these commemorations changed years later, in 2004,
when the government actively participated in the ceremony to mark the
repossession of the Naval Mechanics School (ESMA), a topic we will return
to in chapter 5. In 2006, the year marking the thirtieth anniversary of the
military coup, 24 March was declared a national holiday, the “National
Memory Day for Truth and Justice.”
Ironies of the political struggle! Th e parallels in the histories of Chile and
Argentina show how meanings of the past can unfold in completely opposite
directions. Although the administrations in both countries had a similar
interpretation of dictatorship as an instance of state terrorism and repression,

Markers of Memory • 111
the date was erased from the country’s calendar on one side of the Andes and
marked as a national holiday on the other. Without a doubt, the reason for
this type of decision depends on its location in the trajectory of the political
struggle. In both countries, the intent was to alter or disrupt the preexist-
ing offi cial calendar. In Chile, the disruption involved eliminating the date
of the offi cial celebration established during the Pinochet dictatorship and
maintained throughout the transition. After the dictatorship in Argentina,
the state and the fi rst democratic administrations remained silent about the
date, leaving the commemoration to social actors. Here a break with the past
involved incorporating the date in question, granting it offi cial recognition.
In 2017, 24 March became a topic of controversy in Argentina yet again
when the Macri administration proposed making it a fl oating holiday. Just a
few days later, in response to widespread protest, the administration reversed
its proposal, and it remained a fi xed date.4
In short, for the protagonists of the struggles surrounding the past and
for others committed to the memory of this past, dates of commemoration
have a special meaning, and the public nature of the rituals is a vehicle for
transmitting these meanings. On these dates, the public sphere opens for the
display and emotional impact of the testimonies and personalized narratives.
It off ers the opportunity to express what has been silenced and forgotten, to
hear stories previously untold, and to recognize narratives totally or partially
denied or erased from consciousness. People confront and rework their fears
and preoccupations, and even face the question of how such a horrendous
repression could have been going on when daily life had been so seemingly
“normal” and peaceful. And as the past is redrawn, these spaces enable new
meanings, new groups and demands, new forms of expression.
It is clear, however, that not everyone shares the same memories. Th ere are
diff erent and even contradictory interpretations of the same events, not only
between the “winners” and “losers” of past confrontations, or between perpe-
trators and victims on each side, but within each side of the confl ict as well.
Furthermore, there is also a diff erence between those who personally expe-
rienced the event that is being remembered and the wider societal collective
body that shares its feelings and cultural knowledge. Here complex processes
of identifi cation, belonging, and transmission are at work. Since the pioneer-
ing works of Maurice Halbwachs, we know that some institutions (the family,
the educational system) off er the fi rst “frameworks” for social memories.
How do diverse institutions (family, school, church, political parties, media
outlets, etc.), social and cultural products (the arts and other forms), and
public commemoration practices operate and interact in transmitting mean-
ings of the past? How do the generations that did not experience the com-
memorated events incorporate the past? What meanings do they attribute to
it? Th ere are occasions in which young people seem entirely uninterested in

112 • Th e Struggle for the Past
certain episodes of the past. Yet in some cases, young people do commit to
the cause and make it “their own,” expressing specifi c demands or adopting
militant stances. Information, knowledge, silences, feelings, ideas, and ideol-
ogies: these are the symbolic goods being conveyed or transmitted. However,
nobody can be certain as to how these will be received at either the individual
or the group level. Here, intergenerational relations and dialogues are crucial:
the dynamics of such interaction yield new meanings and interpretations of
the past (Jelin and Kaufman 2006a; Jelin and Sempol 2006).
Territorial Markers as the Nexus between Past and Present
Just as some dates become meaningful, there are spaces where meanings of
the past are concentrated. Th is explains the eff orts to establish territorial
markers at these sites. Th e struggles to build monuments, museums, memori-
als, and commemorative plaques are straightforward and free of pretense in
the global political scenario. For that reason, they also represent an attempt
to reassert feelings of collective belonging and identities rooted in a violent
history, though not always explicitly so. Th ese gestures and affi rmations, a
materiality with a political, public, and collective meaning, play out in at least
two ways: putting up a marker is always the result of political confl icts and
struggles, and the existence of the marker serves as a reminder of a political
past ridden with hostilities. Th is reminder can, in turn, set off new waves of
dissent with regards to the meaning of the past for each generation or histori-
cal period. Initially supported by individuals or groups, these initiatives must
be collective and public in order to become a reality. Government support
and resources must also be involved. Once established, the sites can serve as
vehicles for the intergenerational transmission of historical continuities and
ruptures, though this transmission and its meanings are by no means assured.
Constructing monuments, marking spaces, respecting and preserving
ruins or sites where the events that people wish to remember have occurred,
or proposing and constructing museums are processes that take time and
involve social struggles. If successful, the installation of these sites semanti-
cizes the material spaces. Th ese processes also imply struggles surrounding
the aesthetic criteria that will be applied when building or choosing what
to conserve on the site in question. Representing horror is not a simple or
linear process. How can that which is no longer there—the empty spaces—
be represented? How should the disappeared be represented? If talking and
speaking is diffi cult, the attempts to mark the physical space can be easier
in some respect: in the minds of many, the remains, ruins, and traces are a
materiality that “speaks for itself.” However, these attempts also prove more
diffi cult, because markers do not belong to a person or group. Neither private

Markers of Memory • 113
nor intimate, they come about in physical public spaces and thus need to be
recognized by the state and by the legitimate authority. Additionally, they are
subject to the transformations of meaning and the changing uses given to
public space.
Granting and transforming meaning is never an automatic or random
process but the outcome of human will and actions. Memory entrepreneurs,5
subjects acting in a specifi c political scenario with the aim of connecting the
present with the past (paying tribute to the victims) and the future (trans-
mitting messages to the “new generations”) are inevitably involved in these
processes. However, even when the promoters and entrepreneurs use every
means available to instill their interpretations of the past, these will never be
entirely set in the stone of a monument or crystallized in the engraving on
a commemorative plaque. As a vehicle of memory, the territorial marker is
just a support—one riddled with ambiguities—for the subjective work and
the collective, political, and symbolic action of specifi c actors at any given
juncture.
Not all places are the same or equal. First, there are physical places where
the events and repressive practices of the recent past took place: clandestine
detention centers, places where people were slaughtered, sites where sociopo-
litical actors in the past suff ered repression, sidewalks and homes where they
were abducted and, in many cases, seen alive for the last time. Th ese spaces
become scenarios for struggle between those who attempt to transform the
way this space is used, and thus erase the identifying marks of this past, and
those who promote initiatives to establish inscriptions or markers, and thus
fi ll such sites with meaning. Villa Grimaldi, now Parque de la Paz (Lazzara
2003), and the Londres 38 building in Santiago are two emblematic cases of
how initiatives of this sort played out at two clandestine detention centers in
Chile. In Argentina, important initiatives of this kind include the recovery
of the premises of ESMA, where the country’s most notorious clandestine
detention center operated, and that of numerous other detention centers,
including the D2 and La Perla in Córdoba.6 At times, initiatives of this sort
encounter opposition, and there are attempts to erase any remnants of the
past, as if the memory of what occurred at a given site could be obliterated
by changing its form and function; the most emblematic example is the
Montevideo jail, which once housed political prisoners, that has been con-
verted into an upscale shopping mall (Achugar 2003).
In relation to the clandestine, hidden “sites of horror,” there are memory
initiatives—almost always led by survivors or family members of victims—
that attempt to retrace every corner of such sites, to mark and reconstruct
every detail of their materiality. Th e goal is to recreate its horrifi c workings,
as occurred at the ESMA Offi cers’ Hall in Buenos Aires (now the ESMA
Memory Site Museum), at La Perla in Córdoba (Argentina), and at Villa

114 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Grimaldi in Santiago, Chile. When a human group succeeds in publicly
marking a place, it encourages others to attempt to commemorate, recover,
or establish markers at other sites, creating a snowball eff ect. Often, the
objective of the group seems to be a literal reconstruction of the past. Yet
because these are places with prior histories, such reconstructions can never
be complete or literal, and they always require historical and contextual
interpretations.7
Second, some of the initiatives do not aim to build or recover a site with
a single meaning; instead, the uses and activities to be introduced there add
a new layer of meaning to a place teeming with history, memories, public
meanings, and private feelings. In fact, some initiatives may not even explic-
itly formulate memory projects, but through the unfolding of the human
actions they entail, new rituals and new meanings are incorporated.
Plaza de Mayo, the emblematic square in downtown Buenos Aires, is one
example of how these layers of history overlap. Its main monument, the May
Pyramid (Pirámide de Mayo), was constructed in 1860 to commemorate
freedom and independence. Th e spot was chosen because a group of citizens
had gathered there in 1810 to say that “the people want to know what is
afoot” in front of the Cabildo building, the seat of the Spanish government
in colonial times. Th is gathering marked the beginning of the movement
to sever ties with Spain and form an independent Argentina. Since that
foundational moment, the plaza has been the scenario and symbol of cer-
emonies associated with power. Especially after the events on 17 October
1945 when a crowd gathered to demand the release of the then–secretary of
labor Juan D. Perón (who had been imprisoned a few days earlier), the square
became—and remains—the physical and symbolic space where the relation-
ship between the national authorities and popular movements is staged.
It has hosted Perón speaking to the crowds from the balcony of the Casa
Rosada, worker protests, celebrations of election victories, and commemora-
tions of all sorts: the country’s entire political history can be read on its paved
sidewalks and potted plants.
In April 1977, a small group of women who had left no stone unturned
in the search for sons and daughters presumably kidnapped by the military
regime congregated at this place. Th ey began meeting there weekly and made
their “marches”8 in silence. Th e Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo have been
marching at the square ever since. While initially their demands focused on the
return of their children alive, as the reality set in that no return was possible,
they continued marching to keep the memory alive and also, more generally,
as a show of support for a broad set of human rights. Th eir rounds are very dif-
ferent from those of the “Peronist” plaza, with all the drumming and chanting
of political slogans, or the government-organized square, where leaders speak
to the people below from the balcony. Th ey are also diff erent from those of the

Markers of Memory • 115
Figure 4.4. Th e diaper/scarf of the Mothers inscribed on the fl oor of Plaza de Mayo.
Photograph by Elizabeth Jelin.
twenty-fi rst-century Plaza de Mayo, where social protests have unfolded and
the unemployed and other groups with specifi c demands have “camped out.”
Every Th ursday, every commemoration day associated with the military
dictatorship (24 March), every twenty-four-hour vigil held at the beginning
of December—the Resistance March, which commemorates International
Human Rights Day—Plaza de Mayo is overrun with memories and repudia-
tion of dictatorship, and its meanings are updated to incorporate the social
demands of the moment. Th is presence is inscribed in stone and cement,
since someone, at some point, painted white diaper/scarves—the symbols of
