the Mothers—on the sidewalk tiles surrounding the pyramid.9 Th us, layers
and levels of history and past meanings coexist in the plaza, a signifi er that
has gradually accumulated multiple meanings (Sigal 2006).
Th ird, initiatives have been proposed to honor and commemorate events
and actors of the past by establishing monuments, naming or renaming
streets and squares, and building memorials and museums. Th e places chosen
for these markers of memory do not necessarily coincide with the places
where the events to be honored occurred. Th e most well-known cases inter-
nationally include Berlin’s Memorial to the Murdered Jews of Europe and

116 • Th e Struggle for the Past
the Vietnam Veterans Memorial in Washington, DC. It was social activ-
ists who proposed and pushed for the erection of these memorials, and the
state implemented them as governmental policy. In both cases, there was
considerable controversy over who should (or should not) be included and
remembered, given the tacit existence of a “recognition policy” anchored
in the diversity of identities of the victims. Th e Berlin monument honors
“murdered Jews,” and thus excludes non-Jews. Th is resulted in the con-
struction of two additional memorials nearby, one to pay tribute to the
homosexual victims of National Socialism and the other for the Sinti-Roma
victims (Jelin 2014b). In Washington, groups that felt that the Vietnam
Veterans Memorial made no explicit reference to ethnic or gender categories
set off the debate. Th is led to the addition of two fi gurative statues that are
part of the memorial and evoke the ethnic identities of the soldiers: “Th e
Th ree Soldiers,” showing an African American, a Latino American and a
Caucasian; and the Vietnam Women’s Memorial, where women are repre-
sented as nurses caring for wounded (male) soldiers, not as women at the
front or carrying weapons.
In Latin America, the monuments and memorials connected to state vio-
lence in the recent past have multiplied. Among many others, there is the
Detained-Disappeared and Executed Political Prisoners Memorial at the
Santiago cemetery, inaugurated in 1994. Buenos Aires has a park dedicated
to memory, Parque de la Memoria; Montevideo has a Detained-Disappeared
Remembrance Memorial; Lima, Peru, has the Eye that Cries memorial; and
Recife, Brazil, has the Torture Never Again Monument. Th e pattern repeats
in cities and towns, where social groups, with the support and involvement of
family members and survivors, demand that the state pay tribute to victims
in public space.
Th ere are cases in which a group successfully establishes a marker whose
meaning endures over time; in others, the battle can be lost, either because
competing groups are pushing for diff erent ways of distinguishing the spot or
because the state does not recognize the demand for a marker as a legitimate
one. Th is language of “success” and “failure” highlights the intent behind
the eff orts to establish memory markers at given sites. Given the historic-
ity of these processes, what could be experienced as “success” or “failure”
at one point in time may change in the future. Th is depends on how the
coming generations interpret what is commemorated; on the policies that
each administration develops, supports, or rebuff s; and on the meaning that
the site acquires for other projects, including the potential for indiff erence
or oblivion. Returning to one of this book’s key arguments, both the events
and actors that are to be remembered—as well as the specifi c places—are
inscribed on a historical-temporal path, and their meaning depends on the
political and social contexts.

Markers of Memory • 117
Figure 4.5. El oj o que llora (Th e Eye that Cries), Lima, Peru. Photographs by
Elizabeth Jelin.

118 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Figure 4.6. Memorial del Detenido Desaparecido y del Ejecutado Político
(Memorial to the Political Detained Disappeared and Executed) at the General
Cemetery in Santiago, Chile. Photograph by Elizabeth Jelin.
Figure 4.7. Monumento a las Víctimas del Terrorismo de Estado (Monument to
the Victims of State Terrorism), Memory Park in Buenos Aires. Photograph by
Elizabeth Jelin.

Markers of Memory • 119
Projects for site markers in Latin America have been ongoing since the
political transition. Th e human rights movement has been responsible for
most of these initiatives, with support from other organizations like trade
unions, professional organizations, student centers, some churches, political
party groups, and state agencies. Th ose who propose the marker have a clear
message they wish to convey. Th ere are areas of confl ict and ambiguities, and
above all multiple voices: those of survivors and protagonists of past confl icts,
victims of repression, human rights organizations, and artists and profession-
als entrusted with documentation and conservation. Additionally, there are
new generations who look to the past for the roots of their current projects,
and other social and political actors who attempt to “use” the past as part of
their future plans, desires, and ideals.
Initiatives of this sort are not always successful. One case that deserves
mention is the former clandestine detention center in the center of Buenos
Aires known as “Club Atlético.” In the 1990s, plaques and commemorative
structures put up with the aim of transforming it into a memory site met
with opposition and even acts of vandalism on several occasions. However,
far from thwarting the initiatives, these acts served only to strengthen the
resolve of the groups involved. Finally, in 2002, the Buenos Aires city gov-
ernment agreed to take responsibility for preserving the site. As the origi-
nal building—a police station whose basement operated as this clandestine
detention center—had been demolished to make way for an overpass, the
preservation task involved archaeological excavations. Excavations turned up
a series of objects, including chains, a police hat with a swastika and the
word “Nazi” sketched on its band, and a ping-pong ball (survivors remem-
bered their captors playing in between torture sessions). As the evidence was
unearthed, a plan was drafted to preserve the site and the objects found there
(www.centrosclandestinos.com.ar).
Cases like this raise a disturbing question: what happens when the pro-
posal for a spatial location of memory fails, that is, when memory cannot
be materialized at a specifi c site? Administrative or governmental decisions
do not erase the personalized memories or the public projects of memory
entrepreneurs. When initiatives are blocked by opposing social forces or by
state actions, the subjectivity, desire, and will of the men and women who
fi ght to materialize their memories grow stronger, and they go out seeking
new channels and new ways to commemorate. In a certain sense, it is pos-
sible to say that activists are unable to rest until memory has been “deposited”
somewhere; it remains in the minds and feelings of the people. Th e process of
transforming personal feelings—unique and diffi cult to convey—into public
collective meanings requires open, ongoing eff orts. Is it possible to obliterate
that which groups of people are trying to remember or perpetuate? Could
inhibiting commemorations and markers in order to impose silence and

120 • Th e Struggle for the Past
oblivion have the opposite eff ect? Could they paradoxically serve to multi-
ply memories by keeping the issues and public debate on the past open and
active?
At the same time, aesthetic debates are a foundational part of projects for
markers, monuments, and memorials. Is one aesthetic more appropriate than
another for depicting the horror? Who should decide how it is done? Here
the debate between representational and performative (van Alphen 1997)
becomes relevant, along with expectations regarding society’s participation
in this public space (Young 2000). Although the monumental realism of
national heroes in the nineteenth century gave way to more diverse forms
of representation, the question of literalness is very present in this fi eld. In
fact, there are frequently power struggles over aesthetics between those who
promote the project (generally the victims, survivors, and members of the
human rights movement), the experts (curators, artists, museologists), and
government offi cials. Th e messages and objectives may be clear, explicit, and
anchored in a fi gurative, realist, descriptive, or literal aesthetic. Th ey can also
be formulated more ambiguously, with the intention of leaving the path
open to the inevitable subjectivity of those who will receive and interpret the
message or visit the site. Without a doubt, the passage of time, the presence
of new subjects, and the redefi nition of sites and interpretive frameworks will
contribute new meanings, many of which may contradict the original ones.
At other times, despite the great eff ort that goes into establishing a marker, it
may be met with indiff erence.
An aesthetic question today revolves around how to incorporate ambigu-
ity in the design of the territorial marker in order to actively engage the sen-
sitivity of site visitors/viewers in the work of memory. It is, as Young notes in
reference to the “countermonument” trend in Germany10:
As a result, even as monuments continue to be commissioned and designed
by governments and public agencies eager to assign singular meaning to com-
plicated events and people, artists increasingly plant in them the seeds of self-
doubt and impermanence. Th e state’s need for monuments is acknowledged,
even as the traditional forms and functions of monuments are increasingly
challenged. Monuments at the end of the twentieth century are thus born
resisting the very premises of their birth. As a result, the monument has
increasingly become the site of contested and competing meanings, more
likely the site of cultural confl ict than of shared national values and ideals.
(Young 2003: 77)
When a place becomes emblematic, the interplay of memories upon memo-
ries becomes central. Plaza de Mayo serves as a reminder of the repression
that occurred in diff erent locations across Argentina, but also symbolizes the
memory of Plaza de Mayo itself: both the violence waged there as well as the

Markers of Memory • 121
succession of protests and marches experienced by those who gather there or
conveyed from “old” participants to “new” ones. At the same time, the prac-
tices developed in one place can be transferred to other spaces and even other
cities and countries. Mothers march in numerous squares in cities and towns
across Argentina, and also in other cities of the world. Th ere are processes
whereby meanings connected to painful and repressive pasts comparable the
world over are generalized—metaphorical processes, Huyssen would argue—
and emblematized.
By their very nature—the fact that they are in a specifi c place—territorial
markers are local and localized. However, their meanings unfold in diff er-
ent scales and scopes for both the people who initiate and struggle for them
as well as for others, e.g. their contemporaries or future generations. Th us,
the Torture Never Again Monument in Recife, Brazil, was thought of with
a universal message in its very conception and design, as an exhibit against
torture in all of its forms and wherever it may occur (Brito 2003).11 In other
cases, what began locally, aff ecting and involving specifi c groups in com-
munity spaces, can become meaningful for others far away, through complex
processes of identifi cation and recognition. Media, fi ction fi lms, cultural
tourism, and other channels play an important role in this process, increasing
the number of protagonists engaged in memory endeavors.
Yet a marker does not have to be anchored in a specifi c physical place; it
may be, instead, a trajectory, an itinerary, a way of speaking and denounc-
ing in a territory of memory. One example of this kind is a march that
has taken place since 1980 through the streets of the city of Neuquén, to
demand information about the disappeared. Since that fi rst march, when
Bishop Jaime de Nevares—founder of the Permanent Assembly for Human
Rights (Asamblea Permanente por los Derechos Humanos)—led a group of
Mothers, relatives, and activists, the event has been repeated year after year,
evoking the Catholic practice of the pilgrimage with the corresponding “sta-
tions,” marking signifi cant locations for the human rights cause. It is thus a
constantly renewed demonstration of a doubly ritualized memory: that of the
events to be remembered and that of the path followed by the marchers, a
practice anchored in an itinerary and a collective action that recreates com-
munity and collective identity (Mombello 2003).
Physical space is not the only territory where actions for memory unfold;
they can also play out in virtual spaces. Facebook, for example, can be a
scenario where meanings of the past can be inscribed.12 In 2010, a group of
users proposed the idea of temporarily removing their profi le picture on the
social network, leaving it “empty.” Atop this anonymous silhouette, some
users added the phrase Nunca más, in the same font used on the cover of the
CONADEP book. Th e message urging other users to join this gesture of soli-
darity read, “From now until 24 March, let’s all take down our profi le picture

122 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Figure 4.8. Facebook profi le. Reproduction courtesy Agustina Triquell.
and remember those who are no longer among us … 30,000 disappeared
here, now and forever.”
Deleting one’s profi le picture staged the presence-absence dichotomy: the
erasure of the photograph, as a symbolic identifi cation of an empty marker,
served as a metaphor for the physical disappearance of people under state ter-
rorism. Th is was not the fi rst time activists drew on the rhetoric of absence
using a blank space to represent the disappeared. Th e most important initia-
tive of this kind was the September 1983 Siluetazo (Body outline protest) by
a group of artists. Th e idea was to make thousands of full-body outlines, so
as to represent the massive nature of disappearances, an empty reproduction
on a “real” scale of what it would be like if those absent were physically pres-
ent. Th e outlines, like empty white masks on other occasions, were ways of
representing disappearance in a massive, far-reaching way, though without
personalizing it (Longoni and Bruzzone 2008).
Th is way of marking real and virtual spaces—the body outlines on Plaza
de Mayo or the blank profi les on Facebook—contrasts with the more per-
sonalized use of photographs by relatives of the disappeared, or the number
tattooed on the arm of Shoah survivors, both of which serve as a persistent
reminder of the horror. Impossible to erase, the corporeal mark is proof,
symbol, and personal recollection. In the case of the detained-disappeared,
the photograph was present from the very beginning of the public protests,
pinned onto the clothing of those who demanded answers most insistently:
relatives, and especially, though not exclusively, mothers. Th ere are norms for
these rituals: those who are not relatives can carry signs with photographs
of the disappeared, but never have the picture pinned to their clothing. Th e
photograph is personalized; it has a name and a place in the family body and
family story (da Silva Catela 2009).
Both forms—the photograph and the outlines—seem to respond to two
diff erent logics: the personalized and specifi c on the one hand, and the

Markers of Memory • 123
Figure 4.9. Anonymous silhouettes, Buenos Aires. Photograph by Elizabeth Jelin.
Figure 4 .10. Mothers with photos of
their disappeared children. Photographs
by Gerardo Dell’Oro. Published with
permission.

124 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Figure 4.11. Banners with photographs of the disappeared at a rally, Calilegua, Jujuy.
Photograph by Elizabeth Jelin.
Figure 4.12. Pasaje Santa Catarina, Córdoba. Courtesy Archivo Fotográfi co, Archivo
Provincial de la Memoria.

Markers of Memory • 125
Figure 4.13. Personalized silhouettes of pregnant women with the inscription of their
names. Photograph by Elizabeth Jelin.
massive and general on the other. However, these trajectories that started
from distant or even opposite points soon crossed. With the photographs,
a process of “standardization” took place in order to put them next to one
another on panels and cloths: they are the same size, generally taken from the
shoulders up, the kind of square head shots used for identity cards.
In the case of the body outlines, some activists began giving them identi-
ties at the moment in which they were drawn: “special” outlines of pregnant
women and children were made and inscriptions with specifi c, personal iden-
tity markers added—names, belongings, places.
In the Facebook case analyzed by Agustina Triquell, someone uses a profi le
image without a face but adds dreadlocks, sparking a lengthy debate over the
idea of diversity. Th is addition of Rastafari culture shows the way in which
these body outlines acquire their own features, not of the person disappeared
but of the subject who is speaking (or in this case, posting). On Facebook,
the addition of something unique to the standardized representation of the

126 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Figure 4.14. Facebook profi le and post. Reproduction courtesy Agustina Triquell.
Transcription (left): Nunca más (Never again). Join us. Put this profi le photograph
until March 24. NEVER AGAIN for the 30,000 disappeared during the military dicta-
torship in Argentina.
Transcription (right): Memory should serve not only to ask for justice I do not forget
or forgive that many of the 30000 smiles that were turned off had long hair and wore
fl owery shirts Not to discriminate is the basis for unity and the warranty for NEVER
AGAIN!!!!!! Continue wearing rastas since this does not prevent thinking and feeling I
congratulate you as an ARGENTINE, damn it!!!!!!
silhouette was considered “disrespectful,” and the discussion turned from fos-
tering memory to arguments surrounding “being Argentine”: it had become
necessary to decide whether Rastafari culture could be included in an image
of the nation.
In this case, in a similar manner to the Siluetazos, the anonymous, stan-
dardized images began to be individualized. Th e absence of the disappeared
can be evoked through an empty space, yet also through his/her presence in
a photograph, or through “putting oneself in the place of” the disappeared
person, as when lying down on a piece of paper to be a model for an outline
or changing one’s Facebook profi le.
Generally speaking, the “duty to remember” is the guiding principle for
both the social and state initiatives. Th is moral obligation entails the trans-
mission of a specifi c story legitimized by suff ering, pain, and the fi gure of the
victims. Th e results are reparation policies and tributes, with a push to iden-
tify and mark each and every place where the events occurred. Th e questions
come up time and again: does it matter whether the place chosen is the site
where the events occurred? Are literalness and ruin—understood as a non-
transferable testimony—necessary, or are symbolic spaces also valid?

Markers of Memory • 127
Committed social and political actors struggle “against oblivion” and
clamor for the recovery of memory. Th e past was ridden with confl ict, and so
are present-day attempts at establishing memory markers. Now, however, the
struggle is about diff erent interpretations of the past. Markers, like narratives,
contain silences and concealments. Th ey are the product of political negotia-
tions, such as the controversies over what date in the past should mark the
start of what has to be commemorated, a source of contention in Argentina,
Chile, and Uruguay.13
Do the objects we are dealing with—memorials, signs, maps, itineraries,
museums, photographs or silhouettes—qualify as memory? Commemorative
names are displayed in every city, written on its walls, streets, and statues.
Yet daily life continues as always, and people generally pay little attention
to the events that took place in the past and the meanings attached to them.
In a city like Buenos Aires, where a great number of streets are named after
people, how much do the locals know about the person for whom their
street is named? Th e main act of commemoration takes place at the time of
the installation, as part of a political action in response to the debates about
the past and also about current demands, marginalities, discriminations, and
prejudices.
Materialities—and now virtual images and messages as well—are vehicles,
instruments for awareness and refl ection. But in and of themselves, they are
not memory. Even if the goal is to crystallize them in stone or in a preserved
ruin, even if the materiality of the marker retains its form over time, there are
no guarantees that the meaning of the place will remain unaltered or be iden-
tical for diff erent actors. Th is meaning always remains open, subject to new
interpretations. It can be appropriated in diff erent ways, relegated to oblivion
and silence, treated as a routine or even with indiff erence in the space of
everyday life. Its future is uncertain, subject to new enunciations and new
senses. Th is open-ended character of the message and meaning of territorial
markers of public space is not random insofar as memories are at the core of
ideological struggles, political projects, and disputes over hegemony.
Th e Archives of Repression
Th e incorporation of archives in a discussion about memory politics merits
special mention. Th e notion of the archive is closely tied to the idea of main-
taining or preserving traces of the past. Adding a paper or an object to the
archive means that it is tied to history, to the past, that it is no longer relevant
for the “living” present, though it is attributed with meanings today. In the
object’s trajectory from the records of daily institutional and social practices
to its inclusion in the archive, there are entities, people, and authorities with

128 • Th e Struggle for the Past
the power of appraisal. Th ey decide what is to be kept and what should be
destroyed or dismissed, a decision based on the distinction between what is
important and valuable versus that which is trivial and, in some cases, dan-
gerous. In Western tradition, besides serving as a storehouse for documents
with information about the past, national archives are spaces that affi rm the
nation-state, places that defi ne national heritage and identity (Pomian 1997),
and evidence the historical continuity of a community or nation.
What should be stored in the national archive versus the archive of a
province or a town? What documents should remain in central governmental
archives and which should be conserved in specifi c institutions, i.e. govern-
ment entities (like military or education institutions) or nongovernmental
institutions (like the Catholic Church)? What papers can be discarded? Who
establishes the appraisal criteria and has the fi nal word over what stays?14 Th e
controversy over which papers are offi cial and which belong to an offi cial (for
example, the president) is never-ending.15
Appraisal and ownership criteria, and access and use norms, are subject to
public and political debate. Confl icts of interest over archives can be personal
(in relation to protecting one’s “honor,” respecting privacy, or conserving
potential intellectual property rights of memoirs or testimonies that may
be published in the future), collective or corporate (the push to destroy cer-
tain documents due to their potential as evidence), or public considerations
(often justifi ed as necessary for “national security”). Such confl icts demon-
strate that archives are not just a warehouse of documents, papers, and “dead”
traces of the past but a scenario for social and political dispute that is active
and ongoing.16
When the documents make reference to totalitarian or dictatorial regimes,
the question of protecting evidence and organizing archives merits special
attention. Dictatorial regimes have always made arbitrary decisions in power
and carried out illegal, covert operations. It is thus to be expected that in
many cases, these regimes leave no written records or documents. However,
there is a chain of command and an administrative-military organization
within such regimes, with police and intelligence bureaus, and the bureaucra-
cies inherent to them. Maintaining, accumulating, organizing, and updating
fi les, records, reports, and transcripts are common tasks for authoritarian and
dictatorial regimes. Intelligence reports, along with “confessions” of tortured
detainees and documents confi scated at the time of detention, add up to
thick individual records and fi les on opposition groups.
If the dictatorial regime had been truly convinced that it would last well
into the future, that its actions were legitimate, and that it would thus not be
held accountable for them, it would have been in no hurry to destroy all of its
documentation. It is only when the regime falls into disfavor and its collapse
becomes imminent that the leaders of the dictatorship order the destruction

Markers of Memory • 129
of the documentation so as not to leave traces that could incriminate it in the
future.17
While the bureaucratic administration of Southern Cone dictatorships
upheld chains of command and the diff erent areas and responsibilities of
the diff erent institutions (police and armed forces, and within the armed
forces, the army, navy, and air force), they also carried out covert operations.
Bureaucratic culture involves compliance with orders as seen in the phrase
“Este es mi informe” (Th is is my report) included before an offi cer’s signature
on police reports found in the Paraguayan “Terror Archive” (Boccia Paz et al.
1992). Th is type of recorded documentation and the archives it is kept in are
organized in accordance with “normal” police work. Many such records were
destroyed as the sun began setting on these dictatorships, though some did
survive. Discovering them, however, was no easy task. Some of these archives
belong to the institutions responsible for repression under dictatorship, many
of which were closed before or after the transition, with a slew of records
destroyed in the process.
Th e archives that were most commonly saved and preserved are those
of police and intelligence agencies. Th is is the case of the archive of the
International State Defense Police (Polícia Internacional e de Defesa do Estado,
or PIDE) in Portugal (Lacerda 2010) and that of the Stasi, the intelligence
agency of the German Democratic Republic, where a timely intervention
prevented its destruction. Th is is also the case with archives that were discov-
ered accidentally, hidden or abandoned in some state offi ce. Th e customary
criminal records, along with police and intelligence reports, continued during
dictatorship. Th e armed forces, on the contrary, did not share this bureaucratic
record-keeping culture, either during armed confl icts or at wartime.18
Transitions generate a social demand to make every eff ort to fi nd these
military and police fi les before they can be destroyed. Th is is because one
of the fi rst tasks of a new administration involves shedding light on human
rights violations, a response to the demand for “truth” that may or may not
be accompanied by a social demand for “justice,” to use the very important
distinction made by America’s Watch (1991). Information that might exist or
could be found is important for many people. It is important for the aff ected,
who seek documents that can serve as court evidence; for those who have
the right to reparations and need documents that attest to their suff ering; for
those seeking information on what happened to their family members, part-
ners, or friends; and for those who want recognition that what they have said
is true, even when there are no immediate economic or instrumental gains
associated with this recognition. For many voices that have been shushed or
gone unheard, demonstrating that what they had reported or said is true—
and having papers to prove it—is suffi cient incentive to go on searching. A
social obsession with proof and papers thus ensues.

130 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Th ere is no single roadmap on how to carry out such a search. Th e fi les that
have been found were hidden, stored, or forgotten, and found by chance—as
occurred with the Police Intelligence Bureau archive in the province of Buenos
Aires and the “Terror Archive” in Asunción, Paraguay.19 Th ere are also system-
atic eff orts to recover documentation dispersed at numerous police, court, or
administrative bodies, like the Provincial Memory Archive (Archivo Provincial
de la Memoria, APM) in Córdoba, Argentina, created by provincial law 9286
in 2006. Th is unique law ordered the creation of an archive without any exist-
ing documents, laying the legal groundwork for the future tasks of searching,
requesting, and storing what documents that could be found.
Th e systematic search methodology introduced at the APM relied on dif-
ferent strategies: on-site searches, requests fi led with diff erent institutions,
joint work with the courts, and visual records. For the on-site visits and recov-
ery, an agreement was reached with the Córdoba police department allowing
APM personnel to visit all of the police stations in the province and recover
documentation. Th e documentation was dispersed in storehouses fi lled with
rubbish, on forgotten shelves, and inside cubicles that had been locked for
years. Th e methodology for recovering the documents came together gradu-
ally as the search moved forward. Th rough this methodology of searching
for papers at all police stations in both the capital city of Córdoba and small
towns within the province, new information on police workings (like fl ow
charts outlining structure, roles, and document output) could be linked
to the actual records of detentions and repression. Th ese search operations
resulted in the recovery of police personnel fi les, administrative briefs, shift
logs, detainee reports, daily agendas, newsletters, records, and photo albums.
A second strategy involved requesting documents from institutions such
as the university, hospitals and maternity wards, the morgue, the archdiocese,
the gendarmerie, and unions and factories. Such fi les were important because
they could provide information on detained-disappeared persons and others
who suff ered repression. A third type of document search consisted of work-
ing with the courts to digitize and store fi les obtained through court-ordered
raids and confi scations. Finally, the APM generated an enormous visual
archive by recording the trials for crimes against humanity in the province of
Córdoba. Th ese trials, and the audiovisual record of the proceedings, provide
insight into how state terrorism worked in the province. Th e APM is the only
institution in the province to store such images.20
Th e investigative commissions established during the initial stage of
transition, however, had no archives to refer to as a source of documenta-
tion to prove what happened, as these were hidden or had been destroyed.
Th erefore, these commissions had to resort to another type of documenta-
tion, namely the papers stored in the archives of human rights organizations.
Th is documentation consisted mainly of reports by people aff ected, personal

Markers of Memory • 131
Figure 4.15. Façade of the Provincial Memory Archive (APM), Córdoba, Argentina. Th e names
of the disappeared from the province are inscribed in the images of fi ngerprints on the wall.
Photograph courtesy Archivo Fotográfi co, Archivo Provincial de la Memoria.

132 • Th e Struggle for the Past
testimonies, press clippings, and the oral testimonies of the victims and wit-
nesses at hearings held by the commissions and in courtrooms.
Th e fact is that during the harsh times of dictatorship, activists started
building these alternative archives, not in the open but in underground
networks popularly known as las catacumbas (the catacombs). Th e solidar-
ity organization and its newsletter Clamor in Sao Paulo,21 the Vicarage of
Solidarity (Vicaría de la Solidaridad) in Santiago, Chile (Cruz 2002), and the
APDH (Permanent Assembly for Human Rights) and other organizations in
Buenos Aires (Crenzel 2011) came up with another type of archive: not that
of the institutions responsible for the repression but that of testimonies and
reports supplemented by data (photographs, documents) that could serve
to locate the victims and prosecute the perpetrators. Th e archives of the
human rights organizations laid the groundwork for investigative commis-
sions during the transition (in Argentina and Chile) or for reports drafted by
nongovernmental organizations (like the report on repression in Uruguay).
Th ere were also archives that numerous individuals and families had stored
over time (da Silva Catela 2001).
At the time of the transition, the objective was to gather information for
immediate use: the evidence of the “truth” and, eventually, justice. Th ere
was no historical mission behind these eff orts, no aim to build an archive for
history or for posterity. Th at concern with the archive for memory and for
history would only appear several years later. Th e next steps, those of estab-
lishing preservation and access criteria, sparked intense, complex debates at
both the professional and political level, and these criteria continue to be a
source of contention even today (da Silva Catela 2002a; González 2002b;
González Quintana 2010). At the time, there was also a pressing social
need to supplement documentary archives with testimonies and oral history
archives.22
Th ree types of archival collections resulted from this long process: those
of the institutions in charge of repression during dictatorship, those gathered
by human rights organizations and investigative commissions, and other
more dispersed collections, often family archives that included documents
and relics of dictatorships, as well as testimonial and oral history archives.
Each archive has its particularities and its organization, its way of dealing
with documents and its own access guidelines. Among professional archivists
and international institutions, this is a reason for concern and a challenge.
Internationally, there was an attempt to systematize and work on the archives
of the organizations in charge of repression to later expand the focus to a
broader set of documents—the archives of the truth commissions, collections
housed in social organizations, etc.23
In short, the building of archives—like other memorialization processes—
is a history of social and political struggles. Th ese struggles—which play out
in congressional debates, the circles of human rights activists, state offi ces,

Markers of Memory • 133
professional spheres, and the mass media—focus on what content should be
sought out and stored, on ownership norms,24 on access, and on the respect
of privacy. Th e debates they promote can have important repercussions like
legislative bills on the protection of privacy and intimacy, a protection that
did not exist previously in most of the countries and whose eff ects far exceed
the specifi c realm of dictatorial archives.25
Th e “Owners” of Memory
Let us return for a moment to human action and memories. Th ere are sev-
eral implicit questions, with diff erent temporalities and actors. When people
want to install a marker, commemorate a certain date, store a document, or
present a testimony, the group that has undertaken the project puts all of its
energy to this end. Along the way, it will face confrontations in diff erent insti-
tutional spheres and at diff erent symbolic and subjective levels. Th e question,
then, is about the scenarios in which these voices achieve legitimacy, allowing
them to obtain what they are after, even in the face of opposition. Th is legiti-
macy will come from the public sphere of the social debate and from the state
apparatus that, through legislative measures or executive decisions, can turn
the social demands into public policies—or rebuff them.
Th is is the fi rst part of the story: establish a date, build a memorial or
museum, develop an archive collection. What follows is a trajectory over
time, once the memorialization project has come into being. If the sought-
after project is successful and all the energy that went into it is placed and
crystallized in a museum, an archive, a memorial, or a plaque, what will
happen to it in the future? Th ere is a clear paradox here because we are speak-
ing of a historical process, and nothing about the struggle or the meaning
attributed at the time guarantees it will remain the same in the future. In fact,
in many of the urban initiatives of the past, crystallized in the current-day
urban scenario, the opposite is the case. Events that represent a break with
everyday life and are thus considered “memorable” at a given point in time
lead a group of memory entrepreneurs to propose and direct eff orts at install-
ing a marker. Th e goal is to inscribe that unique event in stone and transform
a space into a place with meaning—conquering a territory both real and sym-
bolic. Once that is achieved, the passage of time turns these inscriptions—the
monument, the name of a street, the bronze plaque, the commemoration
date, the papers in the archive—into part of daily life, their origin and refer-
ences lost in the past. How many Buenos Aires residents know something
about the person their street is named for? Or the name of the hero depicted
in the statue they pass every day, hardly giving it a thought? Memories can be
constructed only when there is a human will to do so—because, at the risk of
repeating, memory is a human, subjective construction.

134 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Notes
1. Most South American countries gained their independence from the Spanish crown
during the 1810s. Th us, the 2010s were full of bicentennial commemorations in the
region.
2. For an analysis of the history of commemoration dates related to the recent past of
violence and repression in Argentina, Chile, Uruguay, Paraguay, and Brazil, see Jelin
(2002a).
3. In 1998, National Liberation Day was replaced by National Unity Day, to be commemo-
rated on the fi rst Monday of September. Th is holiday was eliminated from the calendar
in 2002.
4. In Brazil, the offi cial date of the coup was 31 March 1964, though the military actu-
ally seized power at dawn on 1 April. To avoid its association with April Fool’s Day, the
military regime “pushed up” its observance date, which remained on the offi cial holiday
calendar until 1998 (Carvalho and da Silva Catela 2002). On the vicissitudes of com-
memoration in Uruguay, where the names of key dates suff ered numerous changes, see
Marchesi (2002).
5. Th e notion of “memory entrepreneur” refers to individuals who work to make memory
issues a public issue. Th e use of the term “entrepreneur” should not be limited to its asso-
ciation with private, for-profi t enterprise. Instead, it refers here to entrepreneurial actions
of a “social” or collective nature. Th e memory entrepreneur becomes personally involved
in the project and gets others involved, fostering participation and organizing collective
eff orts. Th e notion, which draws on Becker’s (1963) characterization of the “moral entre-
preneur,” is further developed in Jelin (2003).
6. Starting in 2007, the Argentine government introduced a policy to put up markers at all
clandestine detention centers that once operated in the country, and to recover several of
these sites as “Spaces for Memory and the Promotion of Human Rights.” Police and the
military were forced to relinquish control of such places. Th e markers at these sites all
bear the same sign with the same information. Enacted in July 2011, Law 26,691 estab-
lishes as a “state terrorism memory site” any location that operated as a clandestine center
for detention, torture, and murder, or any other place where crimes associated with the
illegal repression before 10 December 1983 occurred.
7. Th e layers of historicity of social activism can be seen in the demands to recover the site
where the National Student Union operated in Rio de Janeiro until the building was
set ablaze on the day of the 1964 military coup. Starting at the end of the 1970s, the
Brazilian student movement began to clamor for the recovery of the site. Th e demand
persisted in the following decades as a way of showing and marking the historical and
symbolic continuity between the student movement that had been the victim of repres-
sion under dictatorship and the same movement’s plans for the future (Langland 2003;
Langland 2013).
8. Th e Mothers say that they began circling the pyramid in the center of the square in
response to a police order—“Keep it moving!”—when they gathered there. However,
they prefer the term “march” as opposed to “rounds” in reference to their circle around
the pyramid because “rounds suggest retracing one’s steps, but when you march, you
move towards something” (Memoria Abierta 2002).
9. It is diffi cult to pinpoint the moment when the diaper/scarves were drawn on the pave-
ment. Starting in the transition (1983), it was common to paint slogans like “Aparición
con vida” (Bring them back alive) and “Cárcel a los genocidas” (Jail for the murderers)
on streets and sidewalks. Around that time, people also began painting the scarves. Th e
careful engraving of the scarves on the pavement around the pyramid—and their renova-

Markers of Memory • 135
tion and upkeep—is systematically handled by young people close to the Mothers. Over
the years, there have been attempts to erase them, not only in Buenos Aires but also in
other cities across the country. In April 2016, for example, the Bariloche City Council
considered a proposal to erase the scarves painted at the civic center.
10. According to the proponents of this movement, traditional monuments actually serve to
promote public oblivion by laying rest to the concerns of active memory and replacing it
with an object that will later be met with indiff erence.
11. Is the Hiroshima memorial a tribute to the residents of the city who were victims of
the atomic bomb? Or is it a memorial with a universal message of “never again”? Or an
entreaty for peace (Yoneyama 1999)?
12. I thank Agustina Triquell for helping me interpret the world of social media and for shar-
ing with me her work and the images presented in this part of the text (Triquell 2013).
13. Choosing a specifi c date is a way of distributing historical responsibilities and blame,
and of defi ning victims. Th is choice, always a matter of dispute, is at its core a political
statement. Who should be included and named on the victims’ memorial at Parque de la
Memoria in Buenos Aires? What dates should defi ne the temporal limits of the repressive
events to commemorate? In this case, a decision was made to start the victims’ timeline in
1969, the year of the worker-student protest movement and ensuing repression known as
Cordobazo (Vecchioli 2001). Similarly, why does the story told at the Santiago Memory
Museum start on 11 September 1973? Is there nothing to be said of the projects and
disputes leading up to this date?
14. Almost every country has legislation establishing what documentation must be conserved
indefi nitely and the period of conservation of other documents, as well as access condi-
tions and restrictions. For the selection of documents, committees composed of histo-
rians are often formed to determine the historical value of the documents. Th is means
that political and ideological perspectives—in addition to technical ones—are brought
to bear on the documentation. In some cases, this can lead to the “disappearance” of an
entire set of documents. Referred to as expungement or fi nal disposal (archival concepts
that are certainly telling!), these documents can be erased under the pretext of spatial
constraints, a lack of interest in the topic, etc.
15. Papers and material objects have an aura of authenticity, despite the growing use of
electronic documentation and archives. Th e questions associated with the selection and
the people assigned to handle the selection of electronic archives are, in a certain sense,
diff erent and novel.
16. To off er one example, Hernández (2010) analyzes the controversies surrounding the
physical location of the documents on the Franco dictatorship in Spain and the condi-
tions for returning documents to individuals under the Historical Memory Act passed in
2007.
17. Recent developments on crimes against humanity and new international courts dealing
with this type of crime aff ect the decisions of dictators and repressive regimes (present
and future) across the world. In the past, those responsible for repression rested assured
that they would not be prosecuted or questioned about what they had done, leading
them to act with impunity. Yet if they suspect there is a possibility they could be taken to
court—or that there will be a search for the “truth”—the proof and documents of their
illegal actions and repression must be destroyed.
18. Some examples of these types of archives are the fi les of the Nunca Mais report in Brazil,
composed entirely of secret copies made of court records (Weschler 1990); the archives
of the Department for Political and Social Order (Departamento de Ordem Política
e Social, or DOPS) in several capital cities of Brazilian states (da Silva Catela 2002a),
Paraguay’s “Terror Archive” (Boccia Paz et al. 1992; González 2002b), and the archives

136 • Th e Struggle for the Past
of the Police Intelligence Bureau in the province of Buenos Aires (www.comisionporla
memoria.org/archivo; Kahan 2008).
19. For example, when preparing for a move to another building, workers at a national bank
in Buenos Aires stumbled on boxes in a basement. Inside was an enormous set of state
documents, including censorship orders and a list of banned books and authors, thus
providing the empirical evidence of the existence of a systematic plan of cultural repres-
sion (Invernizzi and Gociol 2002).
20. Ludmila da Silva Catela, director of the APM Provincial Memory Archive from 2007 to
2015, and archivist Diego Carro provided detailed information on how the archive oper-
ates. See also the interview with Diego Carro published in Red de Archiveros Graduados
de Córdoba (2016).
21. In March 1978, a small group of human rights activists founded the Human Rights
Defense Committee for the Southern Cone Countries (Comité de defesa dos directos
humanos para os paises do Cone Sul). Th e objective of this committee was to help those
who were escaping from repression in other countries in the region and had arrived in
Sao Paulo. Supported by Cardinal Paulo Evaristo Arns, the organization operated until
democracy was reinstated in all of the countries. Clamor was the name of their newsletter,
which reported on the atrocities. A description and analysis of this group’s transnational
solidarity and rich archives can be found in Lima (2002, 2003).
22. An important pioneering institution in this regard is the Research and Documentation
Center for Brazilian Contemporary History (Centro de Pesquisa e Documentação de
História Contemporânea, or CPDOC) in Rio de Janeiro. Th rough oral history inter-
views, the center has built a record of the visions of the Brazilian military personnel
who participated in the 1964 coup and in the subsequent military governments (Soares,
D’Araujo, and Castro 1995, among others). In Argentina, Memoria Abierta took on the
systematic task of organizing an oral history archive (www.memoriaabierta.org.ar), fol-
lowed by other governmental and nongovernmental initiatives.
23. In 1993, the International Council on Archives created a task force to study the archives
of the security forces of repressive regimes from the past (including many in Eastern
Europe). In an agreement with UNESCO, the task force’s report was published in 1998,
along with practical recommendations and ethical considerations for the preservation
and use of these archives (González Quintana 1998). An updated version of the report
published by the International Council on Archives in 2008 broadened the sphere of
research and recommendation for archival work to other archives produced by repressive
bodies (special tribunals, prison systems, etc.) by civil society organizations, commis-
sions and ad hoc organizations that operated during transition (truth commissions, for
example), and public archives in general (González Quintana 2010).
24. During police raids and abductions under dictatorship, a wide range of personal objects
was stolen, including books, photographs, letters, and other household items. Should
these objects be returned to their “owners” or are they state property (da Silva Catela
2002a; da Silva Catela 2002b)?
25. Th e challenges associated with establishing criteria for opening archives are evident in
many places. Many dictatorial regimes—in Europe, Latin America, Africa or elsewhere—
relied on “confessions” obtained under torture, and forged documents with the names of
alleged “informants.” Such practices clearly indicate that not any document or piece of
paper is synonymous with a factual truth. Th e ethical issues associated with bringing into
the public view that which took place while at the same time protecting the privacy of
the victims come up time and again. (On the debates surrounding this question in South
Africa, see Simpson n.d.)

Chapter 5
VICTIMS, RELATIVES, OR CITIZENS?
Whose Voices Are Legitimate Enough?
8
Th e theme of this chapter has been with me for decades, from my early entrance
into this fi eld. Since the late 1970s, the Mothers have been the emblematic
fi gure in the Argentine human rights movement, to the point that internation-
ally—in Europe, North America, and elsewhere—they have often been seen as
the exclusive protagonists of the resistance to dictatorship. I was startled by this,
since I was aware of the multiple protagonists of resistance. Th is made me wonder
about the place of familism and maternalism in expressions of suff ering and in
claims toward the state. In this, my concerns about the memories of dictatorships
converged with my involvement and research on the family and the social role
of women. On the other hand, and more directly linked to the argument of this
chapter, I began paying attention to claims to “ownership” of memories and of
suff ering. Who has the right to speak? Th e idea that the voice of some actors is
more legitimate than others started to gain saliency in my thinking. As mentioned
in chapter 3, there were actors (especially women) who claimed they were the
legitimate “owners” of truth and memory, a claim anchored in their deep personal
suff ering.
From that initial preoccupation—perhaps more of a hunch than a fi nding—
the issue reappeared time and again, guided by my academic, ethical, and politi-
cal concerns. At the beginning of the 1990s, while I was researching and writing
about the human rights movement in Argentina, preparations were underway for
the World Conference on Human Rights, to be held in Vienna in 1993. For the
feminist movement, it was an important opportunity to voice claims regarding the
rights of women and also denounce rape as a war crime—given what was going

138 • Th e Struggle for the Past
on at that time in the Balkans. In that context, friends from an international
network of women (Entre Mujeres Sur-Norte) asked me to write about the link
between women and human rights issues. Th e resulting text (Jelin 1993) connects
women’s human rights with the role women play in human rights movements.
It is not an obvious or simple link, either theoretically or in terms of observing
social practices. In Argentina, and in other countries of the region, a peaceful
convergence of objectives and struggles seemed unlikely; disagreements and a lack
of coordination between women’s movements and human rights movements were
more the rule than the exception.
As I studied the human rights movement and the role of kinship ties, the family
and familism became part of the vocabulary of my work. I felt it was important
to deal with the family when analyzing the human rights movement. It was also
signifi cant to introduce ideological and political dimensions when the focus was
on families. Th e human rights framework and the actual place of families in resis-
tance and redress were signifi cant enough to merit a place in studies on the family.
I included a reference to the subject in my book on the transformation of families
(Jelin 1998), and expanded my thoughts on the subject in a full chapter of the
revised edition of that book (Jelin 2010a). Also, I researched the topic of genetics
and identity recovery of children kidnapped or born in captivity for a dossier on
the family in a Brazilian journal (Jelin 2007c). A section of this chapter is based
on that paper.
In the arenas of public debate in Argentina, it was not easy for me to argue
that there are struggles for the “ownership” of memory and truth. At times, I faced
passionate opposition. Stating as I sometimes did that Argentine society consid-
ers some voices more legitimate than others—or that there are people who assert
their truth, grounding it in their position as a victim, someone “aff ected,” or more
recently, an activist—was taken as a betrayal of the movement. Th e argument
was that I was not recognizing the prominence and primacy of pain. Over the
years, I have repeatedly included in my texts a caveat, a phrase that reiterates,
with minor changes, the following (it is also in this text, on p. 152): “I do not
doubt the pain of the victims or their right (and the right of society as a whole) to
discover the truth about what happened during the repressive regime. Nor is there
any doubt about the leadership role played by direct victims and their relatives (in
Argentina and elsewhere) as voices denouncing repression, or their central place
in the demands of truth and justice.” My aim in adding this caveat is to avoid
hostilities, often with no success.
***
Th e Argentine experience can be taken as an extreme case of the power of
those “directly aff ected,” highlighting the role of personal narratives of suf-
fering in the disputes about who has the right to speak about the dictatorial

Victims, Relatives, or Citizens? • 139
past. In the period after dictatorship, the “truth” gradually became identifi ed
with the position of the “directly aff ected.” Th e fi rst voices to be heard were
those of relatives of the victims of state repression, the most emblematic
being the Mothers and Grandmothers, whose voices were later joined by
those of HIJOS (Children of the Disappeared) and Herman@s (Siblings
of the Disappeared). In the public sphere, little attention was paid to the
voices of the survivors of clandestine detention centers or those of militants
and activists of the early seventies until many years later. In fact, these voices
would not take center stage until nearly thirty years after the 1976 military
coup.
Th e public presence of the voices of relatives at the beginning and of sur-
vivors later on carried considerable power in setting the agenda of demands
associated with the country’s dictatorial past. Th e notion of “truth” and
the question of what voices were legitimate—or, to take this further, who
“owned” the topic—came to be embedded in personal experience and in
family ties, especially genetic ones. Within the progressive political forces
identifi ed with the condemnation of state terrorism, the presence and result-
ing political power of these voices in the public sphere is very strong and
carries signifi cant legitimacy. Th is symbolic and political dominance of
familism, and more recently, the identifi cation with the political activism and
militancy of the 1970s, leave little if any room for other voices in society. Th is
means that arguments based on citizenship or a universal perspective on the
human condition were excluded from public discussion about the meaning
of violence in the recent past and from the discussion about policies regard-
ing that past. Th e historical and political challenge for democratic actors, in
Argentina and elsewhere, is to move away from these exclusionary tendencies
and open up the political debate to wider citizen participation.
Family and Familism in Memory Politics
Th e idea of family and family ties has occupied a very particular place in
Argentina since dictatorship and state terrorism. As noted in chapter 3,
after seizing power in 1976, the military used (and abused) references to the
family. First, the government defi ned society as an organism composed of
cells (families). By doing so, it established a direct connection between the
social structure and its biological root, naturalizing family roles and values.
Th ere was only one way, the “natural” way, to organize Argentine society. To
the extent to which the family metaphor applied to the nation as a whole, the
father-state acquired inalienable rights over the moral and the physical fate of
its children-citizens. Th e image of the nation as the “grand Argentine family”
tacitly implied that only “good” children-citizens were truly Argentine.

140 • Th e Struggle for the Past
In this discourse, paternal authority was fundamental. Sons and daughters
had a moral obligation to obey; there was no room for citizens with rights or
for human beings with personal autonomy. In a world such as this—a world
more “natural” than it was social or cultural—evil or illness was seen as an
external danger, a foreign body that invaded and spread. In order to restore
the natural balance, then, a surgical intervention was necessary to extract and
destroy the infected social tissue. Th e military regime thus became a protec-
tive father who took on the arduous responsibility of cleaning house and safe-
guarding his family, with the assistance of actual family fathers, in charge of
controlling and disciplining rebellious teens. State-sponsored advertisements
on TV would ask, “Do you know where your child is now?” urging parents to
replicate ad infi nitum the military’s policing and oversight.
Th e image of the family as the cell of the nation meant that parents had to
protect it from foreign penetration, given that a virus or bacteria that invaded
a single cell could infect others. Children and adolescents were the weakest,
most fragile parts of the family-nation body, and if they were in contact with
these external elements, they could spread illness within the social body.
Th erefore, the way to defend the nation was to confront the enemy at its
entry point: the ties between youth and their families. In this regard, while
father-state was there to protect the nation, he couldn’t lose sight of what
happened within families. Th e result was an encroachment on family life that
blurred the distinction between public and private life.
Th e defense of the traditional patriarchal family was a clear and explicit
touchstone of the de facto regime (Filc 1997). At the same time, the covert
repression introduced by the military directly aff ected thousands of fami-
lies through mass kidnappings of persons—often from their own homes—
who were later tortured or disappeared (Calveiro 1998; CONADEP 1984).
Children were kidnapped with their parents, and pregnant women who
were kidnapped were kept alive until they gave birth. Military personnel and
others with ties to the regime took the newborns and gave them new identi-
ties, forging adoption papers or birth certifi cates to make it appear these chil-
dren were their own. Estimates of the number of disappeared persons vary
and are an ongoing source of dispute, the highest being thirty thousand; the
number of children kidnapped or born in captivity with forged identities is
estimated at fi ve hundred.1
In 1976, relatives of detained and disappeared persons came together to
form the organization Relatives of the Disappeared and Imprisoned for Political
Reasons (Familiares de Desaparecidos y Detenidos por Razones Políticas).
April 1977 marked the fi rst meetings that would become the emblem of
Argentina’s human rights movement: Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo (Madres
de Plaza de Mayo). In November of that same year, the Grandmothers of the
Plaza de Mayo (Abuelas de Plaza de Mayo) association was founded.

Victims, Relatives, or Citizens? • 141
Why should the demands and denunciations of the human rights move-
ment be couched in kinship terms? In the political context of dictatorship,
repression, and censorship, political organizations and labor unions were
suspended, and there was no room for public expression of dissent, as fear
and terror were the basic tools of power. Under such conditions, part of the
human rights movement also embraced the familistic language used by the
dictatorial regime: the denunciation and protest of relatives was, in fact,
the only one that could be voiced. After all, these were mothers searching for
their children and trying to fi nd out what had happened to them.
Th e dictatorship gave parents the ultimate responsibility of keeping their
children from becoming “subversives.” When parents of disappeared persons
entered a government offi ce asking about the fate of their children, they were
met with accusations instead of answers: such parents didn’t know what their
children were doing because they had not suffi ciently exercised their parental
authority. If children became subversives, it was owed to poor child rearing.
Th us, the paradox of the Argentine military regime of 1976–83—one
that bore similarities to other military regimes in the Southern Cone during
that period—was that both the military government and the human rights
movement utilized the language and metaphor of family in their respective
discourses and practices. Th e paradigmatic image of this period is the mother
symbolized by the Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo: donning a diaper/scarf, this
mother emerges from the “natural” private sphere of family life and invades
the public sphere in search of her kidnapped-disappeared son or daughter.2
Relatives, Mothers, and Grandmothers are kinship-based organizations that
have maintained active demands of justice, truth, and memory since the
1970s. Twenty years later, they were joined by HIJOS and, later, in the
twenty-fi rst century, by Herman@s. What is signifi cant here is the fact that
these organizations enter the public sphere drawing on bonds of kinship that
are not metaphors or symbolic images of family ties but are grounded in lit-
eral (and biological), kinship.
Despite their contrasting stances, both sides talked in terms of family. For
the military, the family represented patriarchal control and authority, pro-
tection against alleged threats and evil. For the human rights organizations,
the personalized, private family bond justifi ed and motivated public action,
with two aims: fi rst, to revert the image of the “bad family” that the military
wanted to convey when depicting the families of its victims. To counter this
image, relatives-victims were presented as exemplary children, good students,
and members of loving families: in short, ideal or “normal” youth. Second,
family loss made private bonds and feelings public, breaking down the divide
between private life and the public realm.
In the image that the human rights movement projected to society, the
bond between the family and the victim was the basic justifi cation for action.

142 • Th e Struggle for the Past
For the justice system, only surviving victims and members of their immedi-
ate family are considered “aff ected” in their demands for information, justice,
and reparations—demands that have to be personalized and individualized.
However, this public and political familism poses challenges in terms of its
cultural and political impact. Th e Mothers could have shared or even univer-
salized their motherhood with a slogan such as, “All the disappeared are chil-
dren of all the Mothers.” Yet the interpretation of motherhood was a literal
one: only actual mothers of people disappeared could participate as Mothers.
Th ere was no symbolic extension of motherhood or kinship to others. At the
same time, due to this particular interpretation of the notion of family and
motherhood, an insurmountable distance is created in public mobilization
between those who bear the “truth” of personal, private suff ering and those
who mobilize politically for the same cause but for other reasons (political,
humanitarian, citizenship-based)—motivations not seen as equally transpar-
ent or legitimate. In the public sphere of debate and denunciation, not every-
one participates on equal footing, as if participation were stratifi ed according
to the public exposure of family bonds with victims. Ideological, political,
and ethical motivations do not seem to hold the same justifi catory power for
acting in the public sphere. Others can only “accompany” the demands of the
“directly aff ected.”
From Victims to Subjects of Rights:
Truth and Justice during the Transition
Th e emphasis on familism only conveys part of the story. Th e end of the dic-
tatorship and the establishment of a constitutional government in December
1983 meant searching for institutional responses to the human rights viola-
tions perpetrated by the dictatorial regime. For the new government, set-
tling accounts with the past would be key to establishing rule of law. It thus
worked to shift the focus from the suff ering of both victims and their rela-
tives to the recognition of the crimes committed by the state and to the trial
and punishment of perpetrators. In this process, the victims—who had been
stripped of their rights and reduced to something less than human—were to
become recognized, legitimate citizens.
As seen in chapter 3, the demands of the human rights movement led
to intense confrontations with the new administration. Th e movement was
after a legitimate form of punishment that could also serve as a reaffi rmation
of the basic ethical values of democracy. Instead of creating a congressional
committee, the government decided to form an independent commission of
noteworthy fi gures to handle the investigation. Th e National Commission on
the Disappeared (Comisión Nacional sobre la Desaparición de Personas, or

Victims, Relatives, or Citizens? • 143
CONADEP) produced strategic information on the methods and techniques
of repression, and Argentine society began to hear the details about what had
occurred. Th e inquiry launched by CONADEP off ered an opportunity to
learn about what had occurred and recognize the truth. Once the truth had
been established, it would be time for justice. As noted earlier, with all the
formalities and rituals surrounding the legal process, the judicial branch took
center stage at the institutional level: victims became “witnesses”; those who
committed acts of repression, “defendants”; and the political actors, “observ-
ers” of the work of the judges. Th e judges, in turn, were presented as “neu-
tral” authorities who made decisions based on legitimate, preexisting rules.
During the trial, the pendulum shifted from concrete, personal, histori-
cally situated narratives to more impersonal universal claims linked to human
rights. In the words of one witness who had been disappeared and jailed for a
prolonged period, “Th e trial erased those phantom testimonies from society.
It made the victims into human beings, put them on par with every other
human being” (Norberto Liwski, CEDES, 1990). Th e trial was a historic
moment: it demonstrated the triumph of the rule of law, transforming the
victim into a subject of rights. By making the subject of rights the embodi-
ment of the new democratic regime, equal citizenship rights were thus
reaffi rmed. At the same time, however, the trial did not abolish either the
suff ering or the need for redress, and the specifi city of the personal and the
family level would reemerge in various ways later on, perhaps even stronger.
In the testimonies taken by CONADEP and, even more dramati-
cally, during the trial hearings, something important was happening.
Disappearance, torture, and covert detention entail a suspension of the social
and political bond.3 Victim and victimizer have a direct relationship; there
is no governing social or political framework. Th e notion of victimhood in
this case does not specifi cally refer to the level of damage or suff ering one
has borne but rather to the radical condition of having been stripped of
one’s voice and of the means to prove that which the subject has experienced
(Lyotard 1988). A victim’s voice does not belong to the real world as we know
it; therefore, there are no means to verify anything that has occurred in the
context of arbitrary terror and total power. In other words, it is as if nothing
had taken place. Victims are thus forced into silence; if they do work up the
courage to speak, no one believes them. In contrast, the position of a subject
of rights is one in which adversaries have access to an authority, a tribunal
that can gauge the truth of accusations according to procedures and rules
for presenting evidence. Legal recourse implies a radical shift in the position
of opponents; once it is instated, both sides are recognized as parties to a
confl ict.
Th e events associated with political repression, which many on both
sides had interpreted based on a “war” paradigm—often accompanied by

144 • Th e Struggle for the Past
the adjective “dirty”—were now to be judged according to the paradigm of
human rights violations. Yet this growing awareness about the rule of law
and its legal embodiment in the human rights paradigm presents a paradox:
believing in an individual subject of rights is akin to seeing a human being as
abstract. Th e law reinstates the victim’s humanity, but in doing so, it renders
his or her historically and politically situated condition—a concrete condi-
tion—abstract. In this way, the rule of law can inhibit or erase political and
moral perspectives. One consequence of the legal paradigm that resulted
from the trial of the former military commanders was the concealment and
silencing of substantive political identities and of the ideological and political
confrontations involved.
Th e outcome of the trial and the verdict went beyond the conviction
of the former commanders. Far from settling accounts with the past in an
almost defi nitive way, as President Alfonsín had hoped, the verdict opened
the door to more indictments and trials.4 However, as analyzed in chapter 3,
the scope of action of the judicial branch was reduced in the following years.
Th e story does not end here, however. When the state abandoned institu-
tion building, social actors, especially victims and their relatives, took over
the initiatives associated with the recent past. Mothers of the Plaza de Mayo
did not cease their activities, nor did Grandmothers, who continued to inves-
tigate the children kidnapped and illegally adopted under dictatorship. Th e
human rights movement continued to denounce what had occurred and to
demand justice, though its public profi le and capability to mobilize experi-
enced ups and downs during these years.
Th e Search of the Grandmothers,
DNA Evidence, and Recovered Identities
Th e military kidnapped thousands of people and made them disappear.
In many cases, children were kidnapped with their parents; in others, the
women abducted were expecting, and their children were born in captivity.
In some cases, these children were returned to family members—generally,
their grandparents—but not always. Th is meant that the task had doubled
for relatives, who were now searching for the disappeared young adults and,
at the same time, their children. Grandmothers of the Plaza de Mayo began
to organize and put together a strategy when, toward the end of 1977, several
women—who kept running into each other in their constant visits to police
stations, government offi ces, churches, and embassies—realized that there
were others like them, searching for their children and, at the same time, for
grandchildren kidnapped or born in captivity. Th ere were also rumors that
the kidnappers would keep pregnant women alive in clandestine detention

Victims, Relatives, or Citizens? • 145
centers until they gave birth, at which point they would take the babies, and
the women would join the ranks of the disappeared.
What followed was the realization that these children had become war tro-
phies, illegally taken by the kidnappers themselves or given away to others—
mostly people related to the repressive apparatus. When it became clear
that the kidnapped children were most likely alive—and that many were
living under forged identities—the Grandmothers moved on two fronts.
Th e fi rst was to fi nd any scrap of information about where these children
might be, and the second was to seek international support to prepare for
the hypothetical situation of identifying children living under a false name.
In response to this need, the global scientifi c community made headway on
genetic testing techniques. Th e Grandmothers needed blood and genetic
tests that could confi rm second- and third-degree kinship, given that the
children’s biological parents had disappeared and only grandparents, uncles,
or aunts remained to provide blood or DNA samples. Immediately after the
constitutional government took power in 1983, work began to establish a
National Genetic Data Bank. Founded four years later, in 1987, the bank
invited the relatives of children kidnapped or born in captivity to provide
blood and DNA samples for future testing. In 1992, the National Right to
Identity Commission (Comisión Nacional por el Derecho a la Identidad, or
CONADI) was established.
Identity restitution is a complex process that requires legal, psychological,
scientifi c, and social interventions. Technically, the justice system is the fi nal
instance for resolving confl icts. Of course, it cannot resolve the subjective
experience of traumatic situations and their marks, which take other paths.
Th ere are at least two parts to each case: the crimes of kidnapping and alter-
ing someone’s identity (committed by the military and others), and the issue
of the personal identity of the child/young adult. Th e claims of the family of
the disappeared and their right to truth are also at stake, as are the eff orts to
seek truth and justice in society as a whole. Th e desires and needs of these dif-
ferent actors rarely coincide: the state that sanctions the crime of kidnapping
and forging identities; the child and his/her identity rights, but also privacy
rights; families and their right to truth; and a society that demands historical
truth. What is more, they can confl ict with one another and hinder progress.
Th e judicial branch is entrusted with legal resolution. Th e other issues follow
other paths—political disputes and proposals, group activities, and cultural
expressions. For those aff ected, the subjective level is also critical.
Th e social and cultural impact of identity restitution is quite signifi cant,
though diffi cult to gauge. Th ere is clearly widespread societal support and
admiration for the Grandmothers and for the processes of helping children
kidnapped and born in captivity learn their true identity. Th e genetic data
bank and DNA tests are undoubtedly fundamental tools for this task and

146 • Th e Struggle for the Past
reinforce the belief that the ultimate evidence of the truth rests in DNA test-
ing—that is, in genetics, biology, and blood (Penchaszadeh 2012).
However, there is a paradox to this form of truth, with social repercus-
sions diffi cult to anticipate. DNA testing as a fundamental resource is being
developed at a historical moment in which genetics is gaining a central role
in family issues. Yet kinship and family are essentially social and cultural
ties. How can societies and legal systems conciliate or confront tensions
between these two normative domains? Undoubtedly, the need has arisen
for Argentine society—or rather, global society—to address and establish
rules and guidelines in several related areas: the ethical dilemmas associated
with reproductive technologies, norms regarding adoption and the right of
children to know their lineage or fi liation (introduced in the Convention on
the Rights of the Child), and advances in medicine on genetic predisposi-
tions, among other issues.5 Given the cultural and political signifi cance of
the processes of identity restitution during recent decades and the sense of
“truth” provided by DNA testing, Argentina may off er a crucial pilot case for
exploring changes in social interpretations of the links between biology and
culture at the family level.
Survivors and Relatives in Public Commemoration
Struggles for meanings of past suff ering and repression express themselves
in rituals and commemorations. Who are the protagonists of such events?
Which voices are to be heard? What messages or interpretations do they seek
to convey? Th e 1976 military coup occurred on 24 March 1976. Since then,
that important day in March has evoked diff erent meanings for various social
and political actors. One particularly special commemoration was that of 24
March 2004. For the sake of the arguments herein, two elements are funda-
mental: the protagonism of the survivors, with a strong presence in the media
and favorable coverage, and that of President Néstor Kirchner. Strikingly, the
president’s key role was less about his past—which would have already been
a novelty, given that no previous president had ever commented on previous
commemorations—but in his identifying as an activist and compañero6 (com-
rade) in the social struggles of the 1970s.
Th e recently appointed president was on the verge of signing an agreement
with the Buenos Aires city mayor to make ESMA (the Naval Mechanics
School) a memory site. At this infamous clandestine detention center, some
fi ve thousand people had been detained—and the vast majority disappeared.
During the days leading up to the agreement, survivors were the focus of
attention. Th eir voices could be heard constantly on radio and television;
newspapers ran interviews and testimonies; and they could be seen leading
public fi gures—including the president and his wife, then-senator Cristina

Victims, Relatives, or Citizens? • 147
Fernández—through the hallways and up and down the staircases of the
former detention center, a building located behind ESMA’s monumental
gates and columns, surrounded by landscaped gardens, in one of the most
exclusive neighborhoods of Buenos Aires.
Although these voices had been heard before—survivors had provided
fundamental testimonies during the trial of the military juntas in 1985, and
their accounts had appeared in books and multiple interviews—they had
not had an easy time of it in the public sphere. Th e fact that they had made
it through the horror often produced suspicion as to how and why they had
survived. From the fi rst testimonies provided by survivors at the end of the
1970s, generally after going into exile in Europe, it was clear that the ESMA
naval authorities had organized an “elite” group of detainees, including pro-
fessionals, journalists, and leaders of the guerrilla group Montoneros. Known
as the “staff ” and “mini-staff ,” they were assigned special tasks based on their
abilities to draft reports, translate foreign language texts, and prepare archives
of news clippings.7 A perverse cultural trap ensued in which a portion of
