Argentine society came to suspect that there was some rationale to deten-
tion, disappearance, and survival. “Por algo será” (there must be a reason),
the catchphrase Argentines had resorted to in the seventies in an attempt to
understand arbitrary, covert detentions, was now shifted to the question of
who had survived: there had to be a reason explaining why certain detainees
had eventually been released. Th is feeling of suspicion and mistrust aff ected
the way the voices of survivors were received, though it did not diminish the
recognition of their suff ering or the trust in their descriptions of conditions
in the detention centers as the “truth.”
Th e suspicion surrounding “privileges” in detention centers focused more
on what these voices did not say (had they collaborated, snitched, betrayed
others?) than on what they did say. However, imagining that the detainees
were somehow involved in the decisions of what would become of them is
an illusion: the perpetrators held the power, and nothing that victims could
say or do held any sway. Th eir power was absolute and their decisions did not
follow any logic: being innocent of what the military considered subversive
activities was no guarantee of survival; similarly, some very engaged politi-
cal activists were later released. Nonetheless, reports, testimony, and fi ction
from this period are fi lled with portrayals of mistrust, disbelief, betrayals, and
tip-off s, especially the recurring image of the female activist who betrays her
cause and provides sexual services to her captors (Longoni 2005).
Th e commemoration of 24 March 2004 was emblematic in this context.
Th e ESMA survivors took center stage. Th ey crisscrossed the premises, walk-
ing other visitors through the itineraries of detention, the places where they
had been tortured and confi ned. Th ey touched the walls, their memories
limited to the movements of bodies, sounds, and smells, given that in most
cases, the hoods they were forced to wear had prevented them from seeing

148 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Figure 5.1. Façade of ESMA w ith cutouts representing the disappeared. Photograph by
Elizabeth Jelin.

Victims, Relatives, or Citizens? • 149
virtually anything. Th eir testimonies and accounts served as a backdrop, an
extraordinary frame, for the public commemoration ceremony.
Th e event had several stages, each with its own protagonists, with the
human rights organizations playing a prominent role throughout, especially
Mothers, Relatives, and HIJOS. Th ere was the moment when President
Néstor Kirchner and Buenos Aires mayor Aníbal Ibarra signed the docu-
ments, offi cially making the premises a memory site; the opening of the gates
to thousands of people who perused the buildings, following the routes of
repression and terror; and fi nally, the speeches made during the commemora-
tion. Let us look at this fi nal stage, the commemoration itself.
Th e city mayor off ered a speech, as did two young people who had been
born in ESMA: a young woman who spoke as a representative of HIJOS and
a young man illegally adopted by people involved in the repression who had
learned of his true identity not long before the event. President Kirchner also
spoke. A poem written by a disappeared woman during her detention was
read, and several popular singers performed.
With every word and every gesture, the speakers alluded to the site where
this ceremony was taking place: ESMA. Each and every speaker lay claim to a
personal connection to the site. Th e woman who had written the poem that
was read had been a compañera of Néstor Kirchner who had been detained at
ESMA; the city mayor Aníbal Ibarra mentioned a classmate who had disap-
peared at ESMA; the young speakers discussed the experience of having been
born there.8
Certain excerpts from the president’s speech are worth noting. His speech
begins by addressing “My dear Grandmothers, Mothers, and HIJOS: when I
was looking at your [raised] hands as you sang the anthem, I saw the arms of
my compañeros, the arms of a generation that believed that change was pos-
sible in this country. It is a belief those of us who are left continue to hold.”9
He then reiterates whom he is addressing, “Grandmothers, Mothers, and
Children of the detained-disappeared, compañeros and compañeras who aren’t
with us: I know you are present in every hand raised here and in many other
places across Argentina.”10
Toward the end of the speech, the president off ers thanks:
For that reason, brothers and sisters, compañeros and compañeras who are pres-
ent even if they are not here, and Mothers, Grandmothers, HIJOS: thank you
for your guidance in the struggle. We must defend [our ideals] with faith, with
our capacity for love, not letting them fi ll our spirit with hatred, because we
have no hatred, but that doesn’t mean we want impunity. We want to have
justice; we want to truly recover memory, to make it strong, so that all of us in
Argentina can remember, recover, and look for guidance to those who gave all
they had for their values. Th ere was a generation in Argentina that was capable
of that, a generation that set an example and that left a path, their lives, their

150 • Th e Struggle for the Past
mothers, grandmothers, and children. Today they are present in your [raised]
hands.11
Th e speech was directed at a very specifi c audience: the relatives—mothers,
grandmothers, sons, and daughters—of the disappeared. As the fi nal words
suggest, family members had an assigned role: to provide testimony in the
name of all those who were absent. In his speech, President Kirchner identi-
fi ed himself as a member of the political group of his generation, emphasiz-
ing his belonging to a generation of militants that fought for a better society
and was disappeared as a result, with several references to his compañeros and
compañeras.
What is striking about the speech is that he does not address society at
large—the people of Argentina—at any point, only the group of victims,
family members, and compañeros. Th ere were only two references to his role
as president of the country. In one, he said, “I come here not as a compañero
and brother to so many compañeros and brothers who shared that time, but as
president of the Argentine nation, to ask forgiveness on behalf of the national
state for its embarrassing silence regarding all these atrocities during twenty
years of democracy.”12 (Th is statement aroused much criticism because in
it, he disregarded what previous administrations had achieved, particularly
President Raúl Alfonsín. A few days after the ceremony, he off ered a public
apology.) Th e second reference emphasizes that being president takes second
place to being a compañero (in a statement that ignores the political identity
implicit in the use of the word): “I’m not here on behalf of any party. I’m here
as a compañero, and also as president of Argentina and of all Argentines.”13
What does all that mean? Why is it important to pay attention to this
event and this speech? What is signifi cant here, in my view, is the president’s
emphasis on particularistic ties to a specifi c group—in this case, the politi-
cal militants and activists of the 1970s who identifi ed with the Peronist left,
though the speakers did not utter the name of the group Montoneros at any
point. It is important to recall that there were many other victims of the
political repression of the military regime—the revolutionary left (ERP),
annihilated by the army rather than the navy, among others—and that
repression took place all over the country, not only at ESMA. Yet the cer-
emony was dominated by this particularistic language, reiterating once again
the centrality of familism and personal testimony.
Once Again, Victims and Families: What about Citizens?
Could things have been diff erent? Is there room in Argentina for a more uni-
versal approach to the redress of human rights violations under dictatorship?

Victims, Relatives, or Citizens? • 151
Is it possible to come up with a perspective that could build citizenship on a
principle of equality? Is the legitimacy of personal testimony an obstacle to
this process? Th eoretically, it does not have to be. Yet the visibility and legiti-
macy of the voices of family loss fi rst, and later the insistence on the direct
personal experience of repression and on past political activism of the 1970s,
sketched out a political scenario in which the notion of the “directly aff ected”
stands at odds with that of “citizen,” giving priority to the former.
Where does “familism” come from? What does it imply in political terms?
As a set of values and beliefs, its roots can be traced to the country’s cultural
and political history. In Argentina and other Latin American countries, the
Catholic Church has been a powerful cultural actor since colonial times.
Familism conceives the “natural” family as the basic cell of society, and its
discourse is anchored in a strong cultural tradition of Marianism, i.e. the
cultural primacy of motherhood embodied in the fi gure of the Virgin Mary.
Th is set of beliefs has guided state policies and family programs, as well as
the relationship between family and the public sphere.14 As an additional
element, during the last quarter of the nineteenth century and the fi rst half
of the twentieth century, Argentina welcomed a massive infl ux of European
immigrants who came with hopes of progress and social mobility, couched
not in the individualistic idea of the self-made man but in intergenerational,
family terms.15 Immigrants were not isolated individuals seeking to get ahead:
they were part of a broad family and community network governed by ties of
solidarity, reciprocity, and mutual responsibility. Th is pattern was replicated
in subsequent waves of migrants, mostly from other Latin American coun-
tries. In short, the ethics of family life has strong historical antecedents.
In broader terms, familism implies a personalized, particularistic basis for
interpersonal and political solidarity. How are these networks of solidarity
built? Who is the target of solidarity? What types of relationships are involved?
It is not just an abstract, anonymous relationship; there must be a personal
bond that connects both sides, be it through patriarchal and hierarchical
family and kin networks. In other cases, familism extends beyond blood ties
to public and political life through vertical bonds of personalized patron-
age, in a pattern that proved politically salient for the charismatic leader-
ship of Peronism.
In this context, despite great eff orts, it has not been possible to build a cul-
ture of universal citizenship. Th e contrast between ideas related to “formal”
democracy and justice, on the one hand, and “social” justice based on distrib-
uting benefi ts on the other, has become a hard-and-fast feature of the coun-
try’s political culture (Jelin et al. 1996). It could be said that Argentina has
never successfully introduced a culture based on the impersonal institutional
principles of rights and the law. What had been achieved in this regard—in
the fi eld of labor rights, for example—was obliterated during dictatorship, in

152 • Th e Struggle for the Past
which citizen rights were eradicated and the victimizers exercised absolute,
arbitrary power. Th e victims were not part of the human community; they
were outsiders and outcasts that had to be destroyed. With the bonds of the
political community destroyed, the only remaining ones were the primordial
bonds of kinship.
Transition and the reestablishment of legitimate state authority—espe-
cially in the new scenario created by the trial of the juntas in 1985— restored
the civic and political subjectivity of the victims. In a sense, the trial was a
performative event of reinstating citizenship and the rule of law. It was, so to
speak, a foundational moment, one that had further consequences and led to
developments of the relationship between the citizenry and the law (Jelin et
al. 1996).
However, historical processes are seldom linear. Th e trial of the juntas
was followed by a retreat and withdrawal of state actions aimed at settling
accounts with the violent past. Given social activism and the magnitude and
organizational capacity of the community of the “directly aff ected,” their
voices increasingly occupied the public sphere. Later on, when the state
could have again taken the lead, the political and cultural climate framed
the voices that were being heard (even the president’s) in the logic of the
family and of survivors, not in a broader understanding of the full political
community.
No one doubts the pain of the victims or their right—and the right of
society as a whole—to discover the truth about what took place during the
repressive regime. Nor is there any question regarding the leading role that
direct victims and their families had, in Argentina and elsewhere, as the
voices that denounce repression and demand truth and justice. Th e issue is a
diff erent one, and it has two sides. On the one hand, who comprises the “us”
with the legitimacy to remember? Is this an “us” that sets a boundary between
those who belong to the speaker’s community and “others” who listen and
observe but are clearly excluded? Or is it an inclusive “us” that invites the
interlocutor to be part of the same community? Two forms of memory
respond to these two notions of “us” or of community: one inclusive, the
other exclusive. Th e tension between the two, and the misunderstandings
and ambiguities they entail, are always present and can become culturally and
politically meaningful at certain critical junctures. As a result, the question
about the cultural climate in Argentina is whether the “us” that can remem-
ber the recent past is reserved only for those who lived through the events or
if the category can be broadened so as to activate legitimate mechanisms for
incorporating others.
Another question arises here: to what extent can memory and justice in
relation to the past expand the horizon of experiences and expectations for
the future? Or are they limited to the specifi c events that have to be remem-
bered? In a text on memory practices in Germany, Koonz says that the legacy

Victims, Relatives, or Citizens? • 153
of the concentration camps “must be to serve as warnings (Mahnmäler)
against all forms of political terror and racial hatred” (1994: 275). Without
denying that each experience is unique, the challenge lies in transforming
it into more generalized demands. Th rough analogy and generalization, a
recollection can become an example that enables learning, and the past can
be a guide for action in the present and in the future (Todorov 1998). Th is
implies, on the one hand, overcoming the pain caused by remembrance and
successfully containing it so that it does not invade every area of life. On the
other hand—and here we move from the personal and private to the public
realm—it suggests learning from this recollection so that the past can become
a guide for action. In this regard, the greatest responsibility lies with demo-
cratic states. And at this point, memory comes into play in another con-
text, that of justice and institutions. When it becomes possible to generalize
and universalize, memory and justice converge, in opposition to intentional
oblivion (Yerushalmi 1989).
Th e question of the authority of memory and truth can take on an even
more disquieting dimension. Th e danger is to anchor the legitimacy of the
expression of “truth” in an essentialized conception of biology and the body
(a refl ection of biological racism). Personal suff ering, especially that experi-
enced in the fl esh or through blood relatives, can be the basic determinant of
legitimacy and truth. At the risk of repetition, if the experience of physical
suff ering grants social legitimacy to express memory, this symbolic authority
can easily slip—consciously or unconsciously—into a monopolist claim for
the meaning and the content of memory and truth. Th e “us” that is recog-
nized is, then, exclusive and nontransferable. Taken to an extreme, this power
could obstruct the mechanisms for broadening societal engagement with
memory by not opening up the symbolic space for reinterpreting the mean-
ing of the experiences conveyed. Th e historical challenge, then, resides in the
process of building civic engagement with the past that is more democratic
and more inclusive.
Notes
1. Th e recovery of identities and the legal resolution of cases is an ongoing process. At the
end of 2019, 130 kidnapped children had recovered their identity. See www.abuelas.org
.ar.
2. Th e question as to why it was mothers and not fathers commonly yields two answers. Th e
fi rst is that being a mother granted more security in the face of terror, given that every-
one—the military included—respects motherhood as something sacred. Second, some-
one—generally the male household head—had to keep working to maintain the family.

154 • Th e Struggle for the Past
As tragically revealed a few months after the Mothers’ fi rst round, maternal status granted
no privileges: in December 1977, several Mothers disappeared, becoming visible victims
of the regime. Furthermore, not all of them were housewives, though that popular image
of them has taken hold.
3. Th e interpretation provided in this and the following paragraphs is based on González
Bombal (1987; 1995).
4. For an analysis of the eff ects of the verdict, especially “Point 30” on further mandatory
indictments, see Acuña and Smulovitz (1995).
5. One of the commercials run by Grandmothers says, “Don’t let your child inherit your
doubt,” alluding to unknown genetic inheritance for people taken from their parents and
illegally adopted.
6. Compañero (literally, partner, companion, comrade) is a key term in Argentina used to
refer to fellows in the Peronist movement.
7. Calveiro (1998) off ers a description and analysis of the perverse system of covert deten-
tion.
8. Speakers María Isabel Prigione Greco and Juan Cabandié were both born at ESMA to
detained mothers who were later disappeared; María Isabel had been raised by relatives,
while Juan had been kidnapped and raised under a false identity until learning who he
really was two months prior to the 2004 ceremony. Both of their speeches, and that of
President Néstor Kirchner, can be seen on YouTube (and also at www.archivoprisma
.com.ar/registro/acto-en-la-esma-2004).
9. “Queridas Abuelas, Madres, Hijos: cuando recién veía las manos, cuando cantaban el
himno, veía los brazos de mis compañeros, de la generación que creyó y que sigue cre-
yendo en los que quedamos que este país se puede cambiar.”
10. “Abuelas, Madres, Hijos de detenidos desaparecidos, compañeros y compañeras que
no están pero sé que están en cada mano que se levanta aquí y en tantos lugares de la
Argentina.”
11. “Por eso, hermanas y hermanos presentes, compañeras y compañeros que están presentes
por más que no estén aquí, Madres, Abuelas, chicos: gracias por el ejemplo de lucha.
Defendamos con fe, con capacidad de amar, que no nos llenen el espíritu de odio porque
no lo tenemos, pero tampoco queremos la impunidad. Queremos que haya justicia,
queremos que realmente haya una recuperación fortísima de la memoria y que en esta
Argentina se vuelva a recordar, recuperar y tomar como ejemplo a aquellos que son capa-
ces de dar todo por los valores que tienen y una generación en la Argentina que fue capaz
de hacer eso, que ha dejado un ejemplo, que ha dejado un sendero, su vida, sus madres,
que ha dejado sus abuelas y que ha dejado sus hijos. Hoy están presentes en las manos de
ustedes.”
12. “… ya no como compañero y hermano de tantos compañeros y hermanos que compar-
timos aquel tiempo, sino como presidente de la nación argentina, vengo a pedir perdón
de parte del Estado nacional por la vergüenza de haber callado durante veinte años de
democracia por tantas atrocidades.”
13. “Yo no vengo en nombre de ningún partido, vengo como compañero y también como
presidente de la nación argentina y de todos los argentinos.”
14. For a history of maternalism in political life in Argentina, see Nari (2004). Htun (2003)
provides a comparative analysis of family and gender policies in Argentina, Chile, and
Brazil.
15. Th e expectation was that children would reach higher levels of education than their par-
ents did, fostering social mobility from one generation to the next. Th e emblem of this
image of social mobility is M’hijo el dotor (My son, the doctor), the title of a popular play
by Florencio Sánchez at the beginning of the twentieth century.

Chapter 6
SEXUAL ABUSE AS A CRIME AGAINST
HUMANITY AND THE RIGHT TO PRIVACY
8
Th is text is very special for me, as it refl ects my thinking and feeling quite accu-
rately, bringing together personal concerns and academic issues in a way that I did
not accomplish in other writings. Let me explain: throughout my work on memo-
ries, I have attempted to incorporate the gender issues involved from a feminist
understanding of the subject. It was more of an approach—a sensibility—than
a theory or methodology. At times, it was very diffi cult to articulate my thinking
and feelings in a systematic, logical way. I had no map. While working with the
fellows of the Memory Program, I constantly urged and encouraged them to incor-
porate a gender perspective, with few specifi c indications about how to do so. Th e
result was somewhat frustrating, as few of the products of the program integrated
a gender-sensitive analysis. Maybe I was asking too much at the time.
Th e scenario is very diff erent two decades later, when gender is a core social and
political issue. Yet I think that “mainstreaming gender” remains a promise as yet
unfulfi lled.
I developed an initial conceptualization about gender and memory in a chap-
ter on the labors of memory (Jelin 2003). In the following years, gender issues
were present in my analysis of the dynamics of the human rights movement, truth
commissions, and familism and maternalism, as presented in chapter 5.
Th e fi rst version of the text that follows was written in 2012, when the silences
related to sexual violence in extreme situations began to lift. Th e interpretive
frameworks were being transformed both internationally and in the Argentine
courts. A fi rst rendering was published in the yearbook of the magazine Lucha
Armada en Argentina (Armed struggle in Argentina), a publication widely read
by former activists. Th at text had a great impact on women activists of the 1970s

156 • Th e Struggle for the Past
who had been kidnapped or imprisoned and were now survivors. Little had been
said in public until then, and that paper stimulated an open discussion, breaking
silences. A revised version of the paper saw the light in English in an academic
journal (Jelin 2012).
Come 2016: an invitation to participate in a congress in Santiago de
Compostela, Spain, was the occasion to return to this theme. Th e congress was
scheduled to begin on 18 July, commemorating the eightieth anniversary of
Franco’s uprising that led to the civil war and forty years of his dictatorial rule.
Th e title of the gathering was a question mark: “1936. A new story? Eighty years
between history and memory.” What was new in the story? What were the long-
kept silences that could help rewrite the script when broken? Since the clearest
example I had on such silences was sexual violence and rape, I put together the
lecture on this topic.
Th e keynote speech I was going to deliver was on a real stage in Santiago’s
foremost theater (Teatro Principal). I was introduced by someone whom I did
not know, and who introduced himself as an admirer and a faithful reader of
my work. I talked (read) for almost an hour. Th e silence was absolute. No one
moved, nobody got up, everyone was focused and attentive: the impact stood at
100 percent. When I fi nished my reading and the mediator asked for comments
or questions, the audience remained seated, immersed in a silence that, I think,
expressed the emotional impact of what they had just heard. It was not easy to get
a fi rst comment or question; nobody was ready to break the ice. At last, a colleague
made a comment. Afterward, he told me that he did it because so much silence
had made him uneasy. One of the organizers, who knew the audience well, com-
mented later that he expected an active participation of female colleagues. But
they did not utter a word. Upon leaving, one of them, very moved, hugged him
tightly and said, “Th ank you,” with tears in her eyes. Th e next day, a colleague
approached me in a hallway and said, “Th ank you. When will the day come
when we can talk about this in Spain?”
Th e coda of this experience, very moving for me, came a few days later at the
Tate Modern Gallery in London. One of the rooms of the exhibit, “Artists and
Society,” showed works dedicated to the Spanish Civil War. Th ere I came across
the painting by the French painter André Fougeron, Martyred Spain 1937. Th e
accompanying text reads, “Fougeron was among many Frenchmen who consid-
ered volunteering to fi ght in support of the Spanish Government, but decided
instead to dedicate his art to the cause. In ‘Martyred Spain,’ the decaying body of
a horse and a raped woman symbolize the innocent victims of a country devas-
tated by confl ict.”
Th ere isn’t much else to say, is there? Th ere were silences and there will con-
tinue to be, but there are also voices that speak up, though they may not be seen
or heard. Th e challenge is to learn to look and listen, because the signs are all
around, everywhere.

Sexual Abuse as a Crime against Humanity • 157
***
As in every hearing, they asked her about sexual violence.
“Yes,” she said. “When we were naked in the torture
rooms, some son-of-a-bitch always had his hands all over
you, saying, what great tits, what a great ass. I can’t call
it rape, exactly, but it was groping. Of course, I can’t say
it happened to every woman: I can only say what hap-
pened to me.”
— Página/12, 9 September 2010
In the trials that took place right around 2010 in Argentina, testimonies of
sexual violence during dictatorship were heard in court for the fi rst time.
How should these be interpreted? Many asked, why now? Was this some-
thing that wasn’t known, something that came up only later? Or was the
media paying more attention now?
Repeated rape, forced nudity, and the groping of not only women but also
men have been, and continue to be, regular practices in clandestine detention
centers, but also in the world at large. What happened during dictatorship?
How were these cases dealt with by the judiciary and by society? Are there
patterns common to diff erent countries? How should we think about the
victims of these humiliations? Which elements of gender theory contribute
to an interpretation of these issues? In this chapter, I intend to explore several
interrelated questions: the political meanings of gender violence, changes
in the international fi eld of human rights, transformations in subjectivities,
and cultural climates that drive and even sensationalize such indignities—or,
on the contrary, impose public silence. For this analysis, I will attempt to
maintain a historical-temporal thread and observe developments in recent
decades. Although the focus is on Argentina, there are notes on many other
places, not so much for purposes of comparison but to establish ties and
infl uences.
Rape as a Political Practice
A paper written by Julie Mostov had an enormous impact on my way of
thinking about this topic. In it, she analyzes the sexing of the nation and
the desexing of the body in the former Yugoslavia. According to Mostov, the
nation and gender are intimately engaged in one another’s formation: the
nation is always gendered, and national myths are anchored in images and
metaphors of traditional gender roles.

158 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Th e nation naturalizes constructions of masculinity and femininity: women
physically reproduce the nation, and men protect and avenge it. At the same
time, this notion of nation collectivizes and neutralizes the sexuality of female
(and, to some extent, male) members of the nation. (Mostov 2000: 89)
Let’s follow Mostov’s discussion. In the national identity policy of the former
Yugoslavia, the “ethnocrats” tried to establish boundaries and defend them.
In order to achieve this, they eroticized bonds to the nation: the masculine
heroism of the soldier embracing his rifl e, the feminized image of the ideal-
ized motherland. In this complex symbolic game, the female body becomes
the mother who gives birth to the sons of the nation, but also the place where
the other can penetrate—even violently so. Hence the need to protect and
discipline women, keeping watch over them and controlling them.
In the nationalist discourse and the policies of national identity, the
rape and violation of women—and the erasing of borders that comes when
“impure” children result from these rapes—is, fi rst and foremost, a violation
of the nation. It is also an aff ront to the men who have been unable to defend
the country’s border. As it constitutes a rape of the nation, the rape of women
becomes a war tactic. Th us, as in other cases, the mass rape of Bosnian
women has nothing to do with sexual pleasure. Rape here is an invasion
of Bosnian borders—an occupation of the other’s symbolic space, property
and territory—and a violation of masculinity. By violating the nation’s sov-
ereignty and autonomy (Mostov 2000), mass rape is thus a confl ict among
men.1 As Rita Segato has noted, “Sexual aggression against a woman’s body
amounts to a moral aggression against the men upon whom the woman
victim is dependent” (Segato 2010: 39).
In the Southern Cone, things are diff erent, though there are some points
in common. Th e power exercised as part of repression was framed in gender
relations. Th e prevalent gender model identifi ed masculinity with domina-
tion and aggressiveness, traits exacerbated in the military identity. Femininity
was ambivalent, merging women’s spiritual superiority with their passiveness
and submission to men’s desires and orders. Power rituals in public (military
salutes, parades, etc.) have a performative function that unequivocally dis-
plays the duality between action/masculine power, on the one hand, and pas-
sivity/feminized exclusion of the population or audience on the other.
Masculine military power in the public sphere, with its rituals and rep-
resentation practices reiterated in uniforms, parades, arms exhibits, etc.,
is accompanied by performances materialized in concrete practices on the
bodies of the victims in the specifi c spaces of repression, i.e. torture sites. In
fact, the masculinity of the torturers was affi rmed in their absolute power
to produce pain and suff ering. Torture was part of a rite of initiation at the
detention centers in which people were stripped of all identifying features:

Sexual Abuse as a Crime against Humanity • 159
their clothes, their personal belongings, their possibility to see and speak
(they were hooded and gagged). “Humanity itself was in abeyance. … Th e
hood and the resulting loss of one’s sight increased the sense of insecurity
and disorientation. … Th e torturers don’t see the faces of their victims; they
punish bodies without a face; they punish subversives, not men [or women]”
(Calveiro 1998: 62). Th e use of animal nicknames—tiger, jaguar, puma—
and the initiation ceremonies for new members of the torture teams were
“moments of exaltation, when the torturer felt like God, with the power
to reduce the other to a passive victim, a body to be penetrated” (Franco
1992: 107).
One reason for the direct repression of women was their role as activ-
ists. Yet women were also kidnapped and subjected to repression because
of their family identity, that is, their ties to men—partners and husbands
mainly, though in some cases, sons or daughters as well—in order to obtain
information about the political activities of their family members. Women’s
identifi cation with maternity and their role in the family also placed the
women-mothers in a very special place: they were deemed responsible for
allowing their children and other relatives to “stray from the path” (Filc
1997).
All existing information about torture points to women’s bodies as a “spe-
cial” object for torturers, and the torture of women included a high dose of
sexual violence. Vaginas, uteruses, and breasts, the body parts that identify
women as sexual objects, wives, and mothers, are clear targets of sexual
torture, and not only in Argentina (Bunster 1991; Taylor 1997). It is also
important to consider that many of the detained women were young and
attractive, and therefore more vulnerable to sexual assault.
For men, torture and prison entailed feminization in that it transformed
them into passive, impotent, dependent beings. Sexual violence was part of
torture, as were constant references to genitalia—if torturers noticed a Jewish
victim was circumcised, the torture became even more brutal; there were ref-
erences to the penis size of all victims; the electric prod was used on testicles,
etc. Th is was a way of making men into inferior beings and, in this act, estab-
lishing and consolidating the “virility” of the military. Men had to “live like
women” and become aware of their corporeal needs: “be like a woman or die
like a man” (for a testimony, see Tavares 1999).
Th e military had incorporated the polarization of masculine/active versus
feminine/passive as a constitutive element, but so had guerrilla groups and
society as a whole. Depictions of la guerrillera in Argentine mass media under
dictatorship highlighted that this fi gure’s femininity was ambiguous. Th e
media depicted the woman guerrilla as masculinized, dressed in uniform
and carrying weapons, her body free of any feminine features (Taylor 1997).
Th e guerrilla movement itself found it hard to integrate the femininity of

160 • Th e Struggle for the Past
militant women; the status of these women was always in doubt, and when
they did demonstrate their ability in an armed confrontation, they were seen
as “pseudo-men” (Franco 1992: 108).
In short, the military defended the country from the invasion of foreign
ideologies, from the external virus and bacteria that were infecting the nation.
Th e use of the infection and cancer metaphors revealed that a two-pronged
strategy was necessary to eliminate the carriers of this illness. In this regard,
women who were detained were both the carriers of this “international sub-
version” and the partners of the “subversives” who had to be destroyed and
degraded. Th e testimonies reveal that women being tortured were kept in
close proximity to their partners in order for the men to hear the humilia-
tions being infl icted on their women.
Rape during torture is not just an individual or group act aimed at satisfy-
ing the lust of the torturers. Acts of rape cannot be attributed to unleashed
sadistic arousal on the part of those who carried out the torture. Th ese were
acts based on quasi-scientifi c knowledge about the devastating personality
eff ects of such practices, part of a political strategy to destroy the enemy by
annihilating the cultural foundations of women’s belonging in a community.
It is very diffi cult, if not impossible, to put oneself in the place of a woman
whose physical integrity has been violated through sexual abuse and torture.
A painful invasion of the body is an attempt to destroy one’s personhood,
removing him or her from the social and human realm. Th ese are situations
in which humanity is drawn into question; hence the expression “extreme
situation,” which refers to the inordinances humans are capable of imagining.
In fact, in a broader sense, all torture implies a violation of the sexed body
of the person tortured. Forced nudity and torture that targets particularly
sensitive body parts—especially sexual organs and erogenous zones—are acts
of sexual violence. At the same time, sexual abuse exceeded another limit that
the victims themselves saw as qualitatively diff erent. Victims of sexual abuse
are not able to express the conditions in which this torture is and was expe-
rienced as qualitatively specifi c, or distinguish under what conditions it rep-
resented a continuation of an already insuff erable torture, until they recover
their subjectivity (Memoria Abierta 2012).
What’s Next?
We know that these acts happened in Argentina’s dictatorship, and that they
were by no means isolated. In his book on the drafting of Nunca más, Emilio
Crenzel indicates that CONADEP received testimonies on rape and decided
to include them in the report. However, the full names of victims were not
included:

Sexual Abuse as a Crime against Humanity • 161
Th e report presents the disappeared according to their personal identifying
particulars. … Th e cases where only initials are given have a specifi c gender
abuse profi le, as they all refer to women who suff ered sexual violence during
their captivity. (Crenzel 2011: 80)
In the CONADEP report, explicit mention of gender as an aspect of
repression only appears in reference to rape. By including only the initials of
the person reporting this form of torture, the aim was to limit the exposure of
questions tied to sexuality, “which it associates with personal honor” (Crenzel
2011: 80). Th ere were also testimonies about corpse rape that CONADEP
decided not to include in the Nunca más report, along with other particularly
horrifi c cases.
Th e exclusion was not because the CONADEP underestimated the ghastliness
of this act but because it hoped to avoid stories involving such extreme cruelty
that they could aff ect the credibility of the report—a decision that was clearly
a matter of opinion, since many of the stories that were included in the report
reveal similar levels of cruelty.2
In the trial of the military juntas of 1985, witnesses provided accounts of
torture targeting sexual organs, not only because of the sensitivity and poten-
tial for extreme physical pain but also because of the symbolic weight they
carried. Witnesses also recounted rape and other forms of sexual abuses.
However, these were not the most visible of the tales, or those that received
the most emphasis.
What was the social climate like in 1984–85? Th ere are various points of
interest here.
• In the cultural climate of the time, rape was seen as part and parcel to
torture, not seen as a gender-specifi c crime. It was, so to speak, just
another part of humiliating the body during torture.
• Th e trial focused on forced disappearances as the epitome of state ter-
rorism. Th e denunciation and search for “truth and justice” zeroed in
on those who had committed that supreme, incomparable crime. In
the face of disappearance, the rest seemed minor; painful and wrought
with suff ering, yes, but in no way deserving of so much attention.
• At the same time, legislation and the cultural climate of the period
treated rape as a “crime of indecency,” an interpretive framework that
could change later.
• Moreover, the visibility of the surviving victims (whose testimonies
were doubtlessly fundamental in the trial of 1985) was nowhere close
to that of the mothers, grandmothers, and family members of the
detained-disappeared. Th ese women took part in the investigation

162 • Th e Struggle for the Past
process to give testimony on what had happened to their sons, daugh-
ters, partners, and other relatives, but not to what had happened to
them—even in cases when they had also been direct victims.3 Th e
fundamental question of the period was not what had happened to
the disappeared who survived but rather what had happened to those
who did not return.
Changes in International Interpretive Frameworks4
In national and international law, what is now taken as a given about sexual
crimes in war contexts is in fact the outcome of gradual victories achieved
over the last part of the twentieth century. However, no international treaty
was ever drafted on rape, as did occur with genocide, apartheid, forced disap-
pearance, and torture. In spite of this, since the beginning of the 1990s, the
prohibition of sexual violence gradually became an explicit area for interna-
tional action.
In 1993, the United Nations Security Council included rape as a consid-
eration when discussing the creation of an international criminal tribunal
for Yugoslavia,5 and all along the 1990s it was recognized as a crime in the
international tribunals (former Yugoslavia, Sierra Leone, Rwanda) and the
International Criminal Court (ICC).6
One of the issues debated in these documents and in verdicts is “consent,”
which has also been a topic of discussion in academic and artistic fi elds. In
the past, the lack of a victim’s consent was necessary to initiate legal proceed-
ings in cases of sexual exploitation; gradually, this requirement has been
limited or eliminated, and references to context, circumstances, and the con-
dition of the victim/survivor have gradually been incorporated to consider
physical or psychological coercion and abuse of power.7
On the other hand, the topic has also been raised in the fi eld of wom-
en’s rights. In 1992, the United Nations Committee on the Elimination
of Discrimination against Women (CEDAW) recognized violence against
women as a form of discrimination that limits or prevents the enjoyment
of human rights and the fundamental freedoms protected by international
human rights law. Th e Beijing Platform for Action (United Nations 1995)
also addressed the situation of women and girls during armed confl icts, noting
that “massive violations of human rights, especially in the form of genocide,
ethnic cleansing as a strategy of war and its consequences, and rape …
are abhorrent practices.” Similarly, major cases have set case law precedents
in the Inter-American Court of Human Rights, which is responsible for
overseeing compliance of the American Convention on Human Rights, the
Convention of Belém do Pará, and other regional human rights treaties.

Sexual Abuse as a Crime against Humanity • 163
In many of these documents and treaties, sexual violence is treated as an
aff ront to “decency,” “dignity,” or “honor,” with all the moral implications of
such terms. Th e paradigm, however, is changing. In 2008, the UN Security
Council recognized sexual violence as a security concern and unanimously
approved Resolution 1820 stating that women and girls are particularly tar-
geted by sexual violence, which in some cases includes “a tactic of war to humil-
iate, dominate, instill fear in, disperse and/or forcibly relocate civilian members
of a community or ethnic group” (UN Security Council 2008). In other words,
the interpretive framework has begun to shift away from a conception of per-
sonal morals to one in which politics and the collective are central.8
Institutional Testimonies:
Sexual Violence at Trials and Commissions
In the transition periods following dictatorship and state terrorism, testimo-
nies are privileged sources in the processes of recovering and reconstructing
what happened. Records and archives are scarce, due to the covert nature of
the repression and the widespread practice of destroying and/or hiding docu-
ments as dictatorial regimes came to an end. Th e personal testimonies of the
victims of prison and exile, the survivors of clandestine detention centers,
and relatives and witnesses are vital to knowing what happened. First-person
accounts have come to hold a very special place in public expressions of the
memories of terror. Th is can be seen in the testimonies provided at investi-
gative commissions and at trials in court, in the statements required under
reparations policies, recordings for oral history archives, public interviews,
autobiographies, documentary fi lms, and a range of other supports.
Testimonies clearly diff er according to the institutional framework in
which they are given. In court, testimony has a script, an established format,
connected to the notion of legal, factual, cold, precise evidence. In general,
oral testimonies made in public and transcribed to “materialize the evidence”
are framed within expectations of justice and political change. Although
testimony in these spheres can empower the victim and lend legitimacy to
his/her voice, its function is to provide a factual description of the material
nature of torture and political violence with as much precision as possible.9
Th e less emotion and engagement a witness reveals in his/her account the
better, given that the oral testimony must replace the material evidence of the
crime. Th is type of public testimony diff ers signifi cantly from those gathered
in historical archives; those requested by investigators; the testimonial texts
written by survivors, witnesses, and victims; and the literary and performa-
tive representations that are more distanced from the literal events of the past
(Taylor 1997; Pollak and Heinich 2006; Pollak 1990).

164 • Th e Struggle for the Past
In addition to the CONADEP testimonies in the 1980s, there were other
testimonies about rape in the trial of the military juntas and in other less
institutionalized settings, such as media and research interviews. In that
period, there was no particular emphasis on asking about sexual violence or
encouraging victims to discuss it, though this gradually changed as the insti-
tutional climate shifted internationally. Years later, when the Peruvian Truth
and Reconciliation Commission (Comisión de Verdad y Reconciliación, or
CVR) began its work in 2001, a gender analysis was not included in its
mandate. Yet feminists pushed for the commission to consider sexual crimes
and to include a gender analysis in its report. Th is had not occurred with
CONADEP in Argentina or the Rettig Commission in Chile, both of which
had been blind to gender issues. Th ough initially it was not clear how to
approach gender violence, international treaties and case law had already
established a working defi nition for the term. As noted by Julissa Mantilla,
who was entrusted with the topic for the Peruvian commission, the exist-
ing international framework enabled greater awareness or sensitivity to such
issues (Mantilla 2010). As a result, the topic was critical to the commission’s
fi nal report, as it concluded that “in certain contexts, such as arbitrary deten-
tion, execution, and diff erent forms of torture, sexual violence was wide-
spread, a reiterated, persisting practice” (Mantilla 2010: 24).
Th e path undertaken by the Peruvian commission was no easy one. By
seeking to hear the voices of women—who made up 54 percent of the infor-
mants and 64 percent of the testimonies obtained in Ayacucho (Comisión
de la Verdad y Reconciliación 2003: 64), the commission broke with the
idea that “women don’t talk.” When they talk, women tend to describe
the suff ering of their relatives and the disruptions of their daily routines.10
Furthermore, the details of what they suff ered are often framed within a con-
text of ongoing injustices. Aware of this, the commission wanted women to
talk “about themselves,” and thus came up with specifi c strategies to capture
experiences of violence, as well as special training documents for personnel,
specifi c public hearings on the topic, etc. (Mantilla 2005). “Gender sensi-
tivity” suggested a particular concern with obtaining testimonies of sexual
violence and rape, and the defi nition of sexual violence utilized was vast: “A
type of human rights violation that includes forced prostitution, forced mar-
riage, sexual slavery, abortion, and forced nudity” (Comisión de la Verdad y
Reconciliación 2003: 89).
It is clear that the women spoke, but not necessarily about their own con-
dition as victims of sexual violence.11 Firsthand accounts of sexual violence
are rare; generally, there are reports of what happened to “other” women in
Peru, Argentina, and other places.12 However, on the basis of the qualitative
evidence gathered, the CVM was able to establish that sexual violence was a
widespread practice during the armed confl ict. Th is sexual violence during

Sexual Abuse as a Crime against Humanity • 165
the armed confl ict in Peru should be interpreted within a historical context of
rooted beliefs on race and gender relations.13
Why do women mention rape as witnesses but not as victims?14 Th e expla-
nations range from shame to guilt; they may also refl ect the fact that rape is
never a single, isolated act in the context of repression but one that generally
accompanies massacre and genocide, and testimonies on these atrocities can
overshadow that of rape (Mantilla 2005). Th eidon also makes reference to
the “war-peace continuum of violence against women” and to the “gendered
nature of memory specialization.”
In the thick description women provided, they narrated a much broader set
of truths about systemic injustice, the gross violations of their socioeconomic
rights, the lacerating sting of ethnic discrimination, and the futility of seek-
ing justice from the legal systems. … When women talk about the suff ering
of family members and of their communities; … when they remember with
outrage how they were subjected to ethnic insults … they are talking about
themselves and the gendered dimensions of war. (Th eidon 2007: 458–59,
original emphasis).
Truth commissions focus on victims within the interpretive framework of
“human rights violations,” which emphasizes physical integrity. Th is creates a
tension between the fi rsthand report of the “individual victim” and the more
holistic quality of women’s memory. Th ere is little room for narratives of
heroic actions by women, or of their involvement in defending their commu-
nities and families. In fact, many women frame their memories of repression
from their traditional role of “living for others” and from an identity centered
on looking after and caring for others, almost always family members. Th e
ambiguity concerning the positions of the active subject versus the passive
companion or caretaker can thus be manifested in pushing aside one’s iden-
tity out of an eagerness to “narrate the other.” A choice is made to be the
witness-observer of the experience of the other (a detained-disappeared son,
for example), turning away from or silencing testimony of one’s own experi-
ences—even though these may “fi lter” into stories ostensibly centered on the
experience of others.
In addition, the need to organize massive quantities of information based
on victim categorizations yields standardized narratives, silencing experiences
that do not fi t into the established framework:
Th ere is a bit of irony; commissions are charged with investigating the truth,
and yet the broader truths that women narrated were too frequently reduced
to the sexual harm they had experienced … commissions must move beyond
their victim-driven logic to open up narrative space for women to provide
testimony that is not limited to suff ering and grief. Th us “gender sensitivity”

166 • Th e Struggle for the Past
would focus less on strategies designed to make women talk about “their rapes”
and more on developing new ways of listening to what women say about
war—and how they say it. (Th eidon 2007: 474)
Undoubtedly, the CVR report exposed the sexual violence that occurred
during the confl ict. Th e disturbing aftereff ect is the possibility of reinforcing
gender stereotypes of woman as victim, silencing her potential and her active
role in society. In Peru, there was no public discussion on this topic or any
social or political response to these painful experiences. Th e documentation
of the atrocities according to the CVR categorization is available for any
interested reader; at the same time, the memories remain in the sphere of
personal experiences and do not produce any symbolic or material transfor-
mations in gender relations.15
Diff erent Contexts, the Same Dilemmas?
Personal memories of torture and prison are strongly marked by the central-
ity of the body. Incorporating such memories to the fi eld of social memories
entails a paradox for the victims: the act of repression violated one’s privacy
and intimacy, thus splintering the cultural division between the public sphere
and private experience. To overcome the void forged by repression, the victim
has to build a narrative memory of the experience, a narrative that is neces-
sarily public in the sense that it must be shared with others—not those others
who tortured and not anonymous others, but those who can understand and
provide care. In order to speak, one needs a space of trust and the presence of
others with the ability to listen.
Nonetheless, those who listen are still “others.” In order to be “normal”
again, it is necessary to rebuild oneself, regaining one’s intimacy and privacy.
Silences in personal narratives are, in this regard, fundamental. Often, rather
than memory lapses, these are personal choices, “a way of managing identity”
(Pollak and Heinich 2006) connected to the process of “recovering shame”
(Amati Sas 1991). How can the need to build a public narrative be combined
with that of recovering one’s intimacy and privacy?
In personal testimony, those who suff ered directly begin to talk, narrating
their experience. Th is is a fundamental source for gathering information on
what has happened; it is also an exercise in personal and social memory and,
as such, a narrative construction that tries to make sense of the past. Yet not
all women want to or can speak in any circumstance or space. Managing
silence—and the right to silence—are also part of this story. In many places,
there are silences born from fear; silences that are the product of a long his-
tory of domination and subordination; silences to protect loved ones, in

Sexual Abuse as a Crime against Humanity • 167
order to spare them one’s own suff ering; and silences as a personal option and
for self-affi rmation.16
From the point of view of society and its institutional system, fi rst-person
accounts are fundamental to building legal proof or attempting to discover
the “truth” about what occurred. International recognition of sexual violence
as a crime against humanity provides an opportunity for systematically incor-
porating such testimonies in trials. In Argentina, in fact, there have been
several convictions for these crimes, and sexual violence and rape continue to
come up in other trials.17 Th is type of testimony is fundamental.
Should victims be asked about rape? Th ere are diverging opinions on this
question. Julissa Mantilla (2010) argues that women do not talk about it
because the question is not posed. During the early years after transition,
nobody asked the question, nor did it come up in fi eldwork or oral history
interviews, which often make room for silence.
I asked a colleague who had done research with former women political
prisoners whether she had encountered cases of rape. Her response was,
I interviewed thirty-two women on several occasions and in diff erent settings
between 1999 and 2003. Going back over these interviews, I see just one case
where a woman said she had been raped. She didn’t make a clear, forceful
accusation of rape, but mentioned it in passing as something she was dealing
with after being freed, something that motivated her to go back into therapy.
She had to work on this issue, she said, because she was afraid of feeling guilty,
or thinking that while being tortured, she had behaved in a way that could
have led to the rape. In the rest of my interviews, no one spoke of rape in the
fi rst person. When rape did come up, it was in comments like, “When other
women were brought in after being tortured, raped,” “the other women who
had been raped,” etc. Yet this is not a topic they went into, nor did they cite
examples or specifi c cases. At the time I did these interviews, these women
were very set on recounting their experience in prison, their resistance, and all
they had done inside prison or with family members. In fact, I would say that
I have no descriptions of torture sessions.18
In her account on Chile, Lorena Fries writes,
Th e women we worked with who reported [rape] normally do not want to take
their cases to court. Th ere are several reasons, one of which is that there are
other identities that are stronger for them, like the activist identity. According
to this logic, they prioritize—and that’s how they put it—a sort of activist unity,
which contrasts with a gender unity or solidarity that could threaten their activ-
ist unity. Another reason is that they don’t trust the courts, not only because
they [the courts] still have many ties to dictatorship, but also because women
don’t trust the courts when it comes to sexual violence. Th erefore, it is diffi cult
for them to think of the courts as a place for reparations. (Fries 2010: 29)

168 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Th is fi nal quote brings into view a longer-term continuity that dates far
back, and an optimism associated with the possibility of making a break
from it. On the one hand, there is a timeline of continuities between sexual
violence prior to dictatorships, the brutality of political sexual violence under
dictatorships, and the level of violence against women in society today. On
the other hand, there is the optimism sparked by convictions for rape in
recent trials, combined with greater concern and visibility of the violence
women suff er in daily life, including sexual violence.
Sexual Violence: Private or Public?
One remaining question brings us full circle to the initial topic of this chap-
ter: why is sexual violence viewed as private? Why not think of the violence
women suff er in political processes as a public topic? In this regard, Rita
Segato argues that
one of the reasons this happens is because people think that rape is actually
rape. Th is is one of the biggest changes that is needed. … Hence the confusion
between the moral dimension and the purely war-like dimension of sexual
aggression prevents us from taking action. (2010: 38)
Historical changes in moral notions and in the defi nition of the boundary
between private and public are slow and confl ictive, producing new ten-
sions. In the times we live in, mass media has made private life public on talk
shows, reality shows, and more recently on YouTube live streams, Instagram,
and other social media. By banalizing feelings and intimacy, there is a risk
that the genre of testimony could be reduced to a tell-all that sensationalizes
the horror. If state terrorism and repression violated intimacy and human
bodies, the (re)building of normalcy requires that we build new concepts of
morality and redefi ne private spaces, intimacy, and the public realm. In this
context, testimonial “fads,” or as Wiewiorka (1998) refers to them, “the era
of the witness,” entails dangers. Th is is because it often seems to be geared to
publicly airing intimacies, which may complicate the narrator’s reconstruc-
tion and overwhelm the listener.
Th e question is not whether or not sexual violence happened but how to
address testimony: how many personal testimonies—and how much detail—
are needed? Who are the target audiences? Beyond the value of serving as
evidence at trials, there is social and legal pressure for women to tell all,
sharing details and circumstances. And this is the point where the moral,
emotional, and political dilemma becomes clearest. Th is pressure is applied
to women whose subjectivity oscillates between exposing their body and

Sexual Abuse as a Crime against Humanity • 169
intimacy to social judgment—which can result in sensationalizing terror—
and the urgency to maintain or recover intimacy, keeping it for oneself or
sharing it with those whom one chooses. Off camera, far from the public and
probing gaze of the mass media.
Notes
1. Gang rape is a way to build brotherhood among the perpetrators, as well as an initiation
rite. Yet, “committing morally abhorrent acts in front of others not only forges bonds
between the perpetrators but also forges sinvergüenzas—shameless people—capable of
tremendous brutality. To lose the sense of shame—a regulatory emotion because shame
implies an Other in front of whom one feels ashamed—creates men with a recalibrated
capacity for atrocity” (Th eidon 2013: 132).
2. Emilio Crenzel, personal communication.
3. In her analysis of Chile, Lorena Fries notes, “Th ere is no register that allows women to
identify what they experienced as torture. We are still hearing testimonies like, ‘No, I was
only raped. Th ey just left me naked. But they used the electric prod on my partner …’
etc. etc. Because the violence against women is naturalized” (Fries 2010: 30).
4. Th is section is based on several international documents, especially in the compilation
and analysis by Viseur-Sellers (2007).
5. In April 1993, Resolution 820 of the UN Security Council condemned “once again
all violations of international humanitarian law, including in particular the practice
of ‘ethnic cleansing’ and the massive, organized and systematic detention and rape of
women.”
6. Article 7 of the Rome Statute International Criminal Court establishes rape, sexual slav-
ery, enforced prostitution, forced pregnancy, enforced sterilization, or any other form of
sexual violence of comparable gravity as crimes against humanity. In other articles, these
crimes are defi ned as “serious violations of the laws and customs applicable in interna-
tional armed confl ict” and as a serious violation of Article 3, which is included in the four
Geneva Conventions on noninternational armed confl icts.
7. In another context, “forced” consent is captured in the William Styron novel Sophie’s
Choice (made into a fi lm in 1982). Since then, a “Sophie’s choice” is a tragic decision
between two equally unbearable options. One survivor of a clandestine detention center
in Argentina recounted that before she was freed, one of the perpetrators decided to rape
her, but allowed her to choose whether he would do so in a hotel or in her own home.
She had to make the choice. Does that constitute “consent”?
8. In Argentina, the criminal code contemplates these acts in the section “Crimes against
honor,” a name that was changed to “Crimes against sexual integrity” in 1999 under Law
25,087.
9. In addition, as Pollak notes, the diffi culties of testimony multiply: “If the concentrative
experience constitutes an extreme case of all human experience, testimonial experiences
are equally extreme. … Being asked to speak of humiliating memories and the diffi culty
of doing so can easily make one feel like s/he is obliged to give testimony, but also needs
to justify him/herself with regards to the events recounted, thus making a witness feel
more like a defendant” (Pollak 1990: 186).

170 • Th e Struggle for the Past
10. It is known that women and men develop diff erent abilities in relation to memory.
Gender socialization involves paying more attention to certain social and cultural experi-
ences than others and anchoring identities in certain activities more than in others (work
or family, for example). It is logical then to expect something similar in recollection and
narrative memory practices: women tend to remember events with greater detail, while
men tend to be more synthetic in their narratives. While women express feelings, men
draw on rational and political logics; women also make more reference to the intimate
sphere and personal relations, whether discussing family or political activism. Women
tend to remember their daily life, their family’s economic situation, what they were sup-
posed to be doing at every point in the day, what was happening in their neighborhoods
and communities, their fears and feelings of insecurity. Th eir recollections occur within
the framework of family relations, because the subjective time of women is organized—
and associated with—reproductive events and emotional bonds (Leydesdorff , Passerini,
and Th ompson 1996).
11. Th e number of rapes reported was relatively low, just 538 out of 17,000 testimonies, 83
percent of which were perpetrated by the armed forces.
12. Th is is also the case of the testimonies of women detained at ESMA (Calveiro 1998; Actis
et al. 2001). ESMA survivors recount cases of rape in their testimonies, but almost always
in reference to others. Th e book published by Memoria Abierta (2012) systematically
presents some sixty testimonies by women interviewed in a friendly, supportive setting—
that of putting together an oral history archive—where they had the freedom to speak
and remain silent on issues they preferred not to discuss. Several literary texts present the
recurring image of the female “traitor,” who betrays her fellow political activists and falls
in love with her torturer. Longoni (2005) has analyzed these literary representations in
Argentina, while Richard (1998) has done so for Chile.
13. Kimberly Th eidon speaks of “racing rape” in reference to soldiers uttering ethnic slurs
against women as they raped them. By referring to them as india, the soldiers—most
of whom were cholos themselves—transferred the stigma to the women (Th eidon 2007;
2013).
14. Th is analysis of women’s voices in relation to sexual violence draws heavily on the
research of Kimberly Th eidon (2007; 2013).
15. In Chile, the National Commission on Political Imprisonment and Torture (Comisión
Nacional sobre Prisión Política y Tortura) made special eff orts to hear reports from
women and testimonies on sexual crimes. Th ere were close to 37,000 reports, including
316 reported rapes, with several cases of ensuing pregnancies that resulted in both abor-
tions and births.
16. Returning to Peru once again: as Th eidon notes, in response to the Truth Commission’s
insistence that “talking will bring something good,” many Andean women felt that “talk-
ing was dangerous, words were frequently weapons. Silence was protective and powerful”
(Th eidon 2013: 115–16).
17. Th e fi rst court ruling to establish rape as a crime against humanity was in 2010, handed
down by the Federal Court of the Province of Santa Fe. In the ruling, the judges noted
that the defendant’s sexual violence against victims of repression has to be considered a
form of torture and thus constitutes a crime against humanity (“Violencia sexual es lesa
humanidad,” Página/12, 23 April 2010). By the end of 2019, there had been ninety-four
convictions for rape and sexual violence as crimes against humanity (retrieved 21 January
2020 from www.fi scales.gov.ar).
18. Silvina Merenson, personal communication. See also Merenson (2014).

Chapter 7
TAKING THE FLOOR
Testimonial Voices over Time
8
Th ere are important continuities and convergences between my personal joys and
concerns and the questions I pose in research projects. “Th e personal is politi-
cal” is a feminist motto; I like to turn it on its head to add, “the political is also
personal.” Th us, in the 1980s, excited by the prospect of building participatory
democracies, I became interested in investigating what leads people to get involved
in the public sphere—or dissuades them from doing so. Th e human rights move-
ment and the memories of dictatorships brought the presence of militants and
activists to the fore. What about the rest? What about the majority of people who
live their lives far from the big political scenarios? How do events in the public
arena impact private lives? What makes some people momentarily abandon their
privacy and individuality and commit themselves to issues that involve others
unknown? Where does the responsibility to others come from? How is all this
linked to the expansion of rights and citizenship?
Th ese were some of the concerns that guided my research agenda in the early
nineties. To get some clues for such questions, I worked with colleagues to conduct
a series of life history interviews, trying to understand the link between life expe-
riences and the socio-political circumstances encountered at each stage of the life
course (Jelin et al. 1996). Some time later, my colleague Susana Kaufman and I
carefully read the transcripts of the interviews, looking more specifi cally for hints
of the dictatorial period. In several cases, the references were more than hints, clear
and explicit. A young woman, whom we call Julia, unveiled to us the naïve and
childish gaze of a young girl during dictatorship and the importance of “realizing”

172 • Th e Struggle for the Past
later, upon reaching adolescence, what was going on. Th at interview, along with
two others, appears in the text that follows.
Th e text makes use of interviews that we analyzed as part of an article that
was translated into English (Jelin and Kaufman 2000) and a year later, in a
revised Spanish-language version (Jelin and Kaufman 2001). In that paper, we
presented some social initiatives linked to the public visibility of memories of state
terrorism, and then the personal and subjective ways of remembering as refl ected
in the interviews. Th e publishers in London asked us to spell out how these two
levels of analysis were connected. Our response, which became part of the intro-
duction, was the following:
Insofar as reality is complex, multiple and contradictory, and the subjective
inscriptions of experiences are never a direct mirror-like refl ection of public events,
one should not expect an “integration” or “gelling” of individual and public
memories, or the presence of a single memory. Th ere are contradictions, tensions,
silences, confl icts, gaps, disjunctions, as well as “integration.” Social reality is con-
tradictory, full of tensions and confl icts. Memory is not an exception. (Jelin and
Kaufman 2000: 90)1
Years later, this statement holds as true as it did then.
I revisited these interviews and interpretations when I was invited to give the
opening speech at the World Congress of Oral History held in Buenos Aires in
2012. I took that opportunity to pay homage to Dorita—Dora Schwarzstein—a
researcher friend and colleague who had done so much to introduce and legiti-
mize the fi eld of oral history and testimony in Argentina, on the tenth anniversary
of her early death. Th e text, with the usual revisions, was published in a special
issue of a journal dealing with testimony (Jelin 2014a). And, layer upon layer, it
is revised again for this book.
***
Remembering is not a passionate or dispassionate retell-
ing of a reality that is no more, but a new birth of the
past, when time goes in reverse.
—Svetlana Alexievich,
Th e Unwomanly Face of War, 2017
When Svetlana Alexievich won the Nobel Prize for Literature in 2015, the
Royal Swedish Academy recognized her for her “polyphonic writings, a mon-
ument to suff ering and courage in our time.” From 1978 until 1985, she
interviewed some fi ve hundred women who had fought with the Red Army

Taking the Floor • 173
during World War II. Th ese interviews were the basis for one of her books
(Alexievich 2017). Th e prologue is also a testimony—the author’s own—on
the history of writing the book and getting it published. Although she began
working on the book in 1978, her account begins with childhood memories
of relatives who died and the ubiquity of war both in daily life and in the
games children played. A war told by women, in contrast to what we “know”
of war, always narrated by men’s voices.
How many times are refl ected in in Alexievich’s story? How do these times
overlap and interact? Th ere is the time of war that she wishes to recover
through the testimonies of the protagonists. Yet that time is in the past, and
it has to be recovered in another moment in life. For that reason, “suddenly
comes this long-awaited moment when the person … begins to remember
not the war but her youth. A piece of her life … I must seize that moment.
Not miss it!” (2017: xvii) Th e question, then, is being able to frame the sto-
ries in another temporality, a biographical time, the time of life—the story of
youth, forty years later. Not feats or heroic acts, but the simple and human.
Th ere is, however, another diffi culty tied to the past: “Th e problem is also
that we speak about the past in present-day language. How can we convey
the feelings of those days?” (Alexievich 2017: xvii). Th e author is fully aware
of the fact that “Just after the war this woman would have told of one war;
after decades, of course, it changes somewhat, because she adds her whole life
to this memory” (Alexievich 2017: xx).
In addition to these multiple temporalities, there are also the times of
the researcher’s life and the changes that occurred in her political and social
milieu. Once she had fi nished writing and had the manuscript ready, she
spent two years getting turned down by publishing houses (“not the right
kind of war”) and arguing with the censors, until fi nally, thanks to Gorbachev
and perestroika, the book was published … with a print run of two million
copies.
Sixteen years later (2002–4), Alexievich refl ects on the process anew: “I
think that today I would probably ask diff erent questions and hear diff er-
ent answers. … Th e documents (the ones I deal with) are living witnesses;
they don’t harden like cooled clay. Th ey don’t grow mute” (2017: xxix). Th e
author thus decides to add a few excerpts not included in the original ver-
sion, and others cut by the censors. And years later, she receives the Nobel
Prize.
Like the prologue to Alexievich’s book, this text explores the multiple
temporalities involved in testimonial narratives and establishes a diff erence
between the times and contexts in which testimonies are crafted, and between
the other times involved: the times these testimonies make reference to, the
times of history, and biographical time.

174 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Testimony in Time
Personal narrative is necessarily a fi rst-person story that conveys the subject’s
experiences to others. It doesn’t consist of recovering or extracting something
that is crystallized and stored within someone; it is a cultural construction
generated at a given moment—which in turn condenses multiple temporali-
ties—within a context of interaction with numerous “others.” What times or
temporalities (outside the subject, so to speak) are present in testimonies, in
those who speak, in when they speak, in what they do and do not say?
In personal testimonies, the historicity of memories—the leitmotif of this
book—entails the historicity of multiple temporalities. Th e institutional con-
text where a person gives testimony is of the utmost importance (Pollak
1990; 2006) and in fact frames the story. Th ere is the question of what can
and cannot be asked, what can be expected, and what could be experienced
as a transgression; all depend on where and with whom the person is talk-
ing—from the broadest institutionalized setting to close interpersonal con-
versation. In turn, the persons who speak can simply answer (or refuse to
answer) the questions posed or introduce other topics, other issues, includ-
ing, without a doubt, their own sensitivity and emotions. Th is happens
during interviews for oral history archives, witness testimonies at trials, news
and research interviews, and in multiple forms of dialogue in which people
recount what happened to them or what they saw. Th e one who does the
asking—the judge or the prosecutor, the researcher or the journalist—has
the power to confi gure the scenario. Th e person who gives testimony has
the power to speak or remain silent. Although testimonial literature is often
interpreted as a way of “giving voice to the voiceless,” i.e. the subaltern sub-
ject, the evidence indicates that it is always a negotiation in which the person
who gives testimony at least has the power of remaining silent.2 In autobiog-
raphies and in stories written at the initiative of the testimony-giver, this dia-
logue does not ostensibly exist; the witness appears to be the absolute owner
of the spoken or written word and of the silence. Yet despite the high degree
of autonomy of this witness, there is always a recipient, an interlocutor, or a
listener, albeit an imaginary one.
Th is framework is conditioned by specifi c objectives and goals, but also
by the cultural climate of each period, language parameters, implicit under-
standings of what questions can and cannot be asked, and taboos and silences,
as discussed with regards to testimonies on sexual violence and torture in
chapter 6. In relation to the atrocities of the Nazi regime and World War II,
a universal trope that has marked the fi eld of memories of extreme situations
worldwide, Wiewiorka (1998) refers to three successive phases in the role of
the witness and its modifi cation over time. Th e fi rst is testimonies by those

Taking the Floor • 175
who did not survive but managed to leave some record of their experiences.
Th e second is centered on the Adolf Eichmann trial in Jerusalem (1961–62),
when survivors came to be recognized as the bearers of history by sharing
their stories. Th e third phase, “the era of the witness” as Wiewiorka calls it, is
that in which testimonies shape the building of history and memory. All of
this is immersed in the codes and language of class, gender, and age.
However, neither the climate of the times nor the parameters are unequiv-
ocal, homogeneous, or the same for everyone at every point in history. At
any specifi c moment, there are multiple paradigms and frameworks at work:
a dominant or hegemonic way of framing interpretations coexists with the
residuals of the past and with the emerging features that will mark the future
(Williams 1977). In this regard, anachronism—understood as the presence
of something “from another time”—is not only inevitable but also creative
and productive (Didi-Huberman 2000).
Besides historical changes in the cultural climate and political settings,
there is also the temporality of the life course. Each stage of life gives its
own tone on stories and narratives, linked to the social expectations associ-
ated with the process of aging and the social roles associated with it. What
Svetlana Alexievich asked of the women she interviewed was to tell her a
story of their youth forty years after the fact. She notes the diffi culties this
task entails and her eff orts to “Not miss it!” (2017: xvii). Th is is because social
expectations associated with the life course and—especially for women—the
stage of family formation condition the issues to be talked about, allowing
some while imposing silence on others. Will people talk about their parents,
partners, children, or grandchildren? Will sexualities be talked about—sexual
orientations, sexual violence, loves, and disenchantments? Importantly, tes-
timonies on political militancy often entail silences: there is no discussing
collaboration with the perpetrators, snitching, or one’s own violence against
others. Th ese issues are often more openly relayed in fi ction or fi ctionalized
accounts than in testimonies.
Time in Testimony
Th e diff erent contexts in which testimonial narratives are produced as life
stories—at trials, in documents “for posterity,” in interviews and autobiog-
raphies—involve diff erent forms of narration and give the subject diff erent
degrees of control over his/her own words. Th is topic begs several questions,
the fi rst being the conditions pertaining to the subject who speaks. When
does the subject speak? What determines what s/he tells and decides not to
tell? What is the purpose of speaking?

176 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Over the course of the previous chapters, references were made to the tes-
timonies of victims, survivors, relatives, and activists to shed light on how the
repression worked and how it was implemented. Above all, testimony is used
to fi nd out the “truth” and reconstruct what happened, especially when no
