documents are available. Th is is crucial at the legal level, yet the testimonial
voice is much more than that.
Th ere are layers that go beyond conveying factual information, because
testimony involves human subjects elaborating a narrative, and each narra-
tive and each subject are unique. A subject’s voice is singular and personal,
even while it conveys collective and shared experiences. As Pollak notes,
Th e biographical approach thus evolves into a privileged research tool through
situations of transition or trauma that represent a break from the individual’s
everyday world. … Each testimony about a given experience brings into play
not only memory but also a refl ection on oneself. For this reason, testimonies
should be considered true instruments for rebuilding identity, not just factual
accounts that serve only to provide information. (Pollak 2006: 54–55)
In these cases, the context in which the testimonial narrative is produced
becomes fundamental: who asks, what is being asked and why, what room is
provided for refl ection, and how emotions and silences are handled.3 For the
person narrating, recounting experience has a special signifi cance. As noted
by Kaufman, “Th e witness is also exposed to the psychological repercussions
of his/her own memories and to the pressures and traces of violence that may
persist in the political arena” (2014: 103).4 Testimonies that recreate violent
scenes the subject has experienced display the “cracks that the suff ering has
imposed on memory and against which the witness must battle in his/her
need and desire to give testimony” (2014: 106). In testimonial narratives,
temporal categories overlap, as occurs in any exercise involving memory.
While narrating, subjects draw on various layers of recollections, which are
anchored in events that serve as points of reference—at times, crystal clear,
and at others, blurry—for reconstructing history. And in this interplay of
times and memories, a story is developed, one that recreates the experience
and interprets accounts of the past from the vantage point of the present,
leading to the construction of meanings.
It is worth noting that, as Alexievich has indicated, personal memories
are anchored in what someone has experienced in the fl esh, but also in
that which was conveyed in the recollections, silences, traditions, and narra-
tives of others. In other words, the memories of others are narratives loaded
with feelings and meanings that a subject may transform into his/her own
experiences.

Taking the Floor • 177
Time and Temporality in Telling5
Th e personal narratives of the “common folk” who lived through periods
of political violence and state repression refl ect the many levels or layers
in which memories are expressed, perhaps not as dramatically, but no less
eloquently.
In life history interviews, narratives tied to memory are built in diff er-
ent ways: at times, an apparently innocuous question yields anecdotes and
recollections of times and experiences past. In others, the memories appear
without any prompts, as crucial events that over the years are recognized as
watershed moments. Th e recollections of a personal experience, or that of a
relative or close friend, can be narrated in a way in which the personal story is
inextricably merged with the larger sociopolitical history of one’s country or
community, joined in a history one and the same. On occasion, narratives are
much more private and do not incorporate the political context. Let us look
at three testimonies, so as to analyze their narratives and their life contexts in
greater depth.6
Julia7
Julia, a middle-class young woman born in Buenos Aires in 1970, recounts
the following:
Th ere were plenty of cases of bombs being planted at the school I attended,
and they were bombs for real, not fake ones. … My mom bought me earplugs
the size of the fi lter of this cigarette, and I would go down to the square and
play with my friends. At three o’clock each afternoon, a siren began to wail.
It was like a factory siren but much louder and we knew that when the siren
started, we had to put in our earplugs because, according to my Mom, they
were knocking down walls at that hour. … So I would put in the earplugs to
not hear all the racket.
Th is is a narrative of events from childhood: bombs “for real” at the school
she attended, noise because someone was “knocking down walls” at three
o’clock in the afternoon, earplugs to protect her ears. Th ere is a biographical
time—childhood—and a historical time—the second half of the 1970s—
in a Buenos Aires neighborhood. Immediately, Julia refl ects on what she
thought back then but with the distance the present-day provides (“I saw
through a child’s eyes”):
When I was a girl, it was perfectly normal to have to evacuate school because a
bomb had been planted. … I was seven years old, and for me, the bad guy was

178 • Th e Struggle for the Past
the one who died. … All of that was perfectly normal throughout my childhood. I
saw everything that happened through a child’s eyes.8
Th e search for meaning of what had happened was a constant for Julia in
her adolescence, which coincided with Argentina’s democratic transition. She
was continuously rethinking her experiences and reinterpreting them, very
much aware of her own biography and of the country’s history:
And many years later I realized. … Th ey weren’t knocking down walls, the
fi ring squads were shooting people! Th e siren was to cover up the sound of the
gunshots. But I didn’t fi nd out any of that until I was older. … I was born in a
period in which “Don’t get involved” was all the rage, along with “Stay quiet,”
“You didn’t see a thing,” “Keep your mouth shut,” and “Don’t do that.”
Julia explains that, back then, she lived in an apartment building next
door to a coast guard outpost and a complex of residential buildings for their
families. And she remembers other everyday details about her childhood.
Th ere’s nothing funny about this story I’m going to tell you but it might seem
funny told from the perspective of a child. I was eight or nine years old and
one of my friends at school lived in one of the coast guard buildings, which
had fi fteen fl oors. Our building only had ten. … One day, I went over to
her house and she said, “I have to show you what the soldiers have inside
their pool!” So I went over to her house to play and [looking down from the
window we saw that] inside the pool, they had mannequins.9 At night, she
told me, they would gather them up and put them away. Sometimes they
wouldn’t put them out the next day, but then the following day, the manne-
quins were back. You can imagine that as soon as I got home, I told my par-
ents what I had seen from the window. My mother almost fainted; my father
told me I was never going to this little girl’s house again. You can imagine that
I didn’t understand why they were so upset because, as I said, kids don’t see
any evil in things: I didn’t see anything evil about this little girl or her parents
or anything.
What she lived as a child was “normal” for Julia. She only began to unravel
what had happened years later. And then, she got scared:
I started putting together all that I told you about the time of the dictatorship
in context, when I realized the horrifi c things that had happened during those
times. I was so afraid! I was even afraid of being an activist, because I thought
that … the military could seize power again. And if I actually was a member
of a political party, I would be just another person disappeared. I related that
period to the fear—which my parents transmitted to me, to some degree. It

Taking the Floor • 179
was a fear that paralyzed many people for years. But, well, I gradually came to
see that the military weren’t going to come back.
At another point in the interview, Julia situates in time her stage of
“realization”:
So those things had an eff ect on me, not when I was little, but when I was
older. … When I got politically active. I didn’t take activism all that seriously
at the beginning—like most teens. … It [my involvement] coincided with the
publication of the book Nunca más, which had just been released. And the
next year, the book entered its second edition. And well, I got involved in a
group of political activists along with some other teens. Plus, it was very easy
to become an activist, because there was a party branch right across the street
from my school …
Finally, her account moves up to the present (the early 1990s). A present
where there is still fear in the public sphere, but she does not experience it as
her own. Julia traces the fear back to her parents.
Because in my house, words like guerrilla, Montonero, and revolución were bad
words, not because my parents were put off by such people, but out of fear. …
Th e years have passed, but my father is still afraid. Yesterday I went to the stu-
dent march at Plaza de Mayo and my husband was part of the security outfi t. …
And a TV camera got a shot of him. … And well, my father was so upset. He
phoned and I didn’t answer so he left a message asking how we could do things
like that, saying we had to be careful, saying that a time would come when
we’d be in too deep. So it’s like he takes that image and pushes it back to the past.
Something like that, right? Th e fear is there.
Julia lives her present and future from a whole diff erent perspective. At
the time of the interview (in the 1990s), she was putting all of her energy
into diverse types of political and social activism, working with women in a
popular-sector neighborhood, which inspired great expectations on her part.
Her fears are connected to that vision of the future, with the ghosts of the past:
Sometimes I’m afraid. But you know what I’m afraid of? Not of disappearing.
I’m afraid of two things: one is exile, because that is a real possibility, and it
would be devastating for me, because I’m very much a nationalist. I love my
country, my land. Imagine leaving my friends, my people, my customs! Just
thinking about it makes me queasy. Th at’s one of my fears. Th e other fear is of
disappearing and not having done anything that leaves a mark, “Here was Julia.”
It’s not about a plaque, because the plaque has nothing to do with it—it’s
about concrete achievements.

180 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Horacio
At the time of the interview, Horacio owned a small industrial facility. He
was forty-four, married, the father of three teenagers; a man committed to his
family’s well-being. Th e fi rst level of his personal memory is a “factual” story
of his own experience:
J. [a close relative] was assassinated. He was a political leader who was kid-
napped and then turned up dead. … We had to live in hiding for a time,
not because we were involved in any of that, but because our name was in
his phone book. … We did all we could at the level of people who weren’t
involved.
Horacio has points of reference to interpret what was going on at the time:
he was a young adult and had information about the political confl icts and
repression.
Back then, you had no way of knowing if the person next to you was one of
the ones looking for you. … It’s like, people working in an offi ce were suspi-
cious of the people sitting next to them. … You had to be careful with others
because you never knew what side anyone was on. … I chatted, sure, but I
didn’t speak my mind. I never said, “If they kidnapped him, por algo será …”
(there must be a reason). Nor did I say the opposite. I was worried because
things were happening. … I tried not to talk much. … I knew too much or
was I inferring too much from what I read. … I remember reading the paper
back then and getting sick to my stomach. I would get dizzy and even faint.
I had the jitters and I had almost no control over it. What I mean is, I read
between the lines and realized things …
Over time, Horacio reinterprets his feelings and anguish during the
dictatorship.
I guess it was the powerlessness of thinking that things were happening, a lot
was happening, and most of us just went on working, we went on. … We just
kept going, as if we didn’t care. … Like that feeling that what is happening is
happening elsewhere, not anywhere near me, not to anyone who could be a friend
of mine. Over time, people I knew were disappeared. … And we’ve heard more
and more. … It’s like hard not to overdose on the information as if the informa-
tion were making me dizzy on the inside. Because that’s what I had. Dizzy spells.
It was like, I’d be reading the paper, and suddenly my eyes would glaze over …
Horacio experiences traces of the past in his fears and recurring illnesses.
He speaks of the dread associated with the past, the marks it left on the pres-
ent, and conveying them to his children.

Taking the Floor • 181
Th ose were hard times. Th ere was a lot of fear. I remember being afraid when-
ever I left the house. Although I had an easy conscience, you just had that
feeling that even if you weren’t involved. … I feel like I was marked for life. …
For example, I drive down Avenue Liberatador, and I can’t help but stare at
ESMA (the Naval Mechanics School), I can’t help but turn. If I’m driving on
this side of the street, I turn there; if I’m coming from the opposite direction,
I turn in the other direction. … I can’t get that building out of my sight.
Th ings like this leave me thinking. Why? Why? Why do I always look? It’s
stronger than me, I simply can’t resist it. When I go with my children, I say,
“Th ey were here. … And they disappeared, and they murdered.” I tell them
a little about it so they know. Mainly, so they know what their parents went
through.
Horacio’s personal refl ections are centered on his relationship with others,
in what one does or doesn’t do, or what one can do:
I don’t know: there are a lot of things you question in life. So many things
that later you say, how could I not have realized that? Sometimes I wonder if
it is worth looking back. In some ways. … Th at moment, yes, that was fear.
I suppose that feeling of fear, that might be what remained for me. … When
there’s a moment of personal refl ection on the things you didn’t do, well, I get
a powerless feeling when I think I did nothing, though I also don’t know what
I should have done. You say, “OK, I have a family, I’m going to protect them.”
At any rate, I have the peace of mind that comes from knowing that I didn’t
get my neighbor in trouble in order to protect my family. But who knows? I
didn’t do anything to help my neighbor either. I think I belong to the major-
ity of the “mind your own business.” But I still wonder about it and, fi rst and
foremost I try to be honest with myself: I don’t think I would have done much
if I had known something was going on here next door. And I don’t know,
maybe I would have said, “Th ere must be a reason,” like many people did. “If
they took them away, there must be a reason.” Th at’s something I ask myself.
It’s a dilemma you carry inside yourself and never know … I mean, I can
work it out, I can fi nd the answer I like the most—I can say to myself, “No, I
wouldn’t [have said that].” But it’s not true, I’m not sure that I … Because I’m
not ready to fool myself.
Lucía
At the time of the interview, Lucía was a sixty-fi ve-year-old woman origi-
nally from a popular-sector rural community. She migrated from Tucumán
to Buenos Aires at the end of the 1940s, when the Peronist government was
introducing welfare policies for the working classes. After working fi rst at
a factory and later as an employee at a doctor’s offi ce, she had retired a few
years prior to the interview. Lucía never married.

182 • Th e Struggle for the Past
One year, in 1975, I had to make an urgent trip to Tucumán because one of
my sisters had died. It was a terribly cold day. I took a plane. … She lived in
a small town. … I then took a bus and the police pulled the bus over. … Th e
soldiers had a kettle and were drinking mate. Oh, Christ, was it cold! A police-
man gets on and says, “Documents, documents.” I pull out my ID card and
show it to him. And he says, “What’s the problem, ma’am?” “Look, sir, please
don’t make me get off the bus. I’m a wreck: I’m on my way to bury my sister.”
Th at man ordered all the people back onto the bus and we went on our way.
Th at was my experience.
Th ose times—in 1975, ’76, until at least 1980 … you heard so many things.
Th at they killed so many people, that people disappeared and were never seen
again. … Did you read that book, Nunca más? I’ve read a few chapters, not
all of them. Someone lent me the book. I was so traumatized whenever I read
anything. … I don’t take sides. Not in politics, not with governments, not with
the military, not with anything else. But the military did some awful things, no
doubt about that! You know how politics work: one person tells you one story
and somebody else has a diff erent version …
In Lucía’s interview, the memories of the military dictatorship of the
seventies appear as one episode in a longer-term story, one more case of the
political instability and uncertainty that dominated Argentine history in
recent decades. Lucía had the impression that she had been living in a threat-
ening environment for years, not feeling safe or protected politically in her
day-to-day life.
Th e political point of reference in Lucía’s tale is the fi rst Peronist adminis-
tration (1946–55). She spoke a lot about that time, and her move from the
province to the country’s capital in that period. Th e fi rst Peronist administra-
tion is part of her personal life:
[I came to Buenos Aires] in February ’46, at the time when Peronism was
coming into power and was wildly popular. Everyone said, “We’re going to
earn a lot under Perón; we’re going to do great under Perón.” And things were
good under Perón, for a time. I have to admit that Perón’s fi rst administration
did magnifi cent things, I remember them well. … But in his second adminis-
tration …10
Th roughout the interview, Lucía reveals a strong emotional engage-
ment with that period. What came later was part of her “oblivion” and her
“disenchantment.”
After Perón came the military. How do they call it? A de facto government?
Th ey came and went. … It’s hard for me to remember because it comes to a
point where you’ve seen so much that it’s disheartening …

Taking the Floor • 183
Th e Layers of Memory
Th e three interviewees remember their personal experiences connected with
state repression during dictatorship in very diff erent ways. In addition to class
and gender belonging, age, or to put it more precisely, the moment in the life
course in which the events remembered took place, is a crucial factor. It marks
the way a person experiences the event, both at the time and afterward: the eyes
of the girl, the informed realism of a young male adult, the disillusionment and
oblivion of the person who situates the events within a longer life span.
Th e interviews were done twenty years after the dictatorial repression, and
the informants’ later experiences—which provided more information and a
considerable temporal distance—allowed them to reinterpret these experi-
ences and give them new meanings. Th is is by no means an unequivocal pro-
cess, since the questions and anxieties they develop later on vary according to
their life course and biographical time.
It is worth noting that in fact, like most of the people with whom we
spoke, Julia, Horacio, and Lucía remember the publication of the book
Nunca más—regardless of whether they had read it—as a moment in which
their personal life experience “touched” the public recognition of human
rights violations. Th e book has unquestionably become a reference point for
a large part of the population that lived the experience of its release in 1984.
At critical points such as these, the historical moment transcends biographies
and cohorts.
When approaching the present—that is, the time the interviews were
conducted—the marks and traces also diff er. Th ese are not the personal nar-
ratives of victims, the “directly aff ected,” or public fi gures who gave—and
continue giving—testimony on dictatorship in a variety of settings. Th ey are
the “average Joe and Jane,” people for whom these topics are not part of daily
life. Th erefore, speaking and refl ecting on dictatorship in the interview set-
ting was a special opportunity:
[Th e interview] shook things up a little—the memories, the past. But it’s nice
to be shaken up once in a while, because as you remember, details come back
that you’ve forgotten in your routine. Th e other day, I was telling a friend
about the interview I had with you, and because of the interview, a fl ood
of memories came rushing out, memories you have inside but didn’t even
remember … (Julia)
It’s like when I’m talking to you, I feel as if I were talking to myself …
(Horacio)
In sum, remembering the past and including it in the subjectivity of
the present brings to light the diff erent levels and multiple temporalities

184 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Figure 7.1. Cover of the book Nunca más (offi cial report of the CONADEP).
Photograph by Agustina Triquell. Published with permission.
involved. Th ere is an initial register, which we could refer to as “factual”: the
historical moment in which the events occurred, which overlaps with one’s
biographical moment and intergenerational family temporalities. Th e diff er-
ent levels of layers of memory and subjectivity form atop this background
• as narratives of the events one remembers from that past;
• as memories of one’s feelings at that moment and that period;
• as present-day feelings connected to the past—fears about loved ones,
personal and group silences, retractions;
• as forms of intergenerational transmission: who speaks, who listens,
who shushes;
• as refl ections on what one has lived through, based on the moment
of the life in which one experienced it and on current perspectives on
that past;
• as a refl ection of one’s place in the world, and one’s social responsibility;
• and fi nally, as an expression of the feelings produced in the act of
remembering during the interview.

Taking the Floor • 185
Th e imbrication of these layers and their interactions reveal the multiplicity
of temporalities: the biographical moment of the contents shared; the histori-
cal time in which these events occurred; and the historical-cultural times of
testimony.
Notes
1. We continued to work jointly with Susana Kaufman, especially in the Memories of
Repression program. We edited one of the books in the series (Jelin and Kaufman
2006b), where we included a text that deals with our own experience of intergenerational
dialogue with the young people who participated in the program (Jelin and Kaufman
2006a).
2. In fact, this is not about being voiceless but rather about a diffi culty on the part of the lis-
tener. In this regard, Rigoberta Menchú insists on agency: “Indians have been very care-
ful not to disclose any details of their community” and have maintained their distance
from intermediaries to their testimony. Sommer (1991) provides an analysis of Menchú’s
words and silences. Th is topic has been amply discussed since the publication of Gayatri
Spivak’s classic work (1988). Years later, the author herself reconsidered her position on
this issue.
3. I analyzed this topic in Jelin (2003), particularly chapter 5.
4. Th e notion of trauma has been central to analyzing survivors’ capacity to narrate their
experiences (LaCapra 2001).
5. Th is section of the text draws on the testimonies presented in Jelin and Kaufman (2001).
I thank Susana Kaufman for generously allowing me to use these materials.
6. Th e interviews were conducted between 1994 and 1996 as part of a project titled “Rights
and Responsibilities: Vital Experiences of Citizenship in an Emerging Democracy.” Th e
work was funded by a John C. and Catherine T. MacArthur Foundation Research and
Writing Fellowship I received in 1994. Susana Kaufman, Silvia Rabich de Galperín,
Marcelo Leiras, and Marina Elberger were the members of the research team. Other pub-
lications on the fi ndings of this project include Galperín, Jelin, and Kaufman (1998) and
Jelin et al. (1996).
7. Th e names of the interviewees have been changed.
8. In all the excerpts of interviews, the emphases are my own.
9. With time, Julia realized the “mannequins” were in fact corpses.
10. Juan D. Perón was president of Argentina from 1946 to 1955, and then again in 1973
until his death in 1974. Th ese are known, respectively, as the fi rst and the second Peronist
administrations.

Chapter 8
MEMORY—FOR WHAT?
Toward a More Democratic Future
8
How should this book end? How can we envision the future from within this interplay of
times, where the past becomes present even as it reaches into the future? What about the
past can help serve as a guide? How can the past be useful? What future are we moving
toward? How do memories of past repression and violence relate to building a democratic
future? Are those who remember the past better at building the future than those who
ignore or forget it?
I began asking myself these questions at the dawn of the twenty-fi rst century when
commemorative projects and initiatives abounded in Argentina. Was there a causal link
between that boom—which was perhaps pushing the limits of what Régine Robin calls
mémoire saturée, the saturation of memory (2012)—and the quality of democracy?
Where did democratic Argentina, where memorialization was so central, stand in relation
to other countries in the region, where concern with the past was less intense?
After sharing these thoughts in a number of settings and granting a number of inter-
views that were later published (Badaró 2011; Mombello 2014), I made the decision to
carry out a more systematic investigation so as to ground my questions in a more rigorous
empirical study.
On the fortieth anniversary of General Pinochet’s coup d’état in Chile, a special edi-
tion of the journal Política: Revista de Ciencia Política gave me an opportunity to pres-
ent and discuss that research (Jelin 2013). I also presented it at the Universidad de Chile,
Harvard University, a plenary session of the Latin American Studies Association (LASA)
and the forum of the IDES Memory Studies Group.
As I undertook this research and formulated its questions, I was guided not by the
past but by the future. What is the past for? Th e past should be approached not from the

Memory—for What? • 187
perspective of the survivors but from that of the “desired [social] order,” to borrow Norbert
Lechner’s phrase.
***
As we have seen throughout this book, memories of the recent past—mem-
ories of suff ering and political violence—have inspired countless rituals,
public policies, and cultural productions. Th ey have also fostered a constant
search for interpretations and explanations. Indeed, institutional, territorial,
and symbolic markers explicitly anchored in the recent past of violence and
repression have proliferated in today’s world, produced by a range of actors
and social movements, and by state policies that respond to these demands.
Th e rationale for those expressions is both individual and collective and aims
to work through suff ering, show solidarity with victims, and pay tribute to
those who are no longer among us. Yet there is a second reason for these
expressions of memory, namely the belief that the construction of more
democratic futures free from violence depends on the “duty to remember.”
In building these futures, the emphasis is on relaying these legacies to the
new generations, or what could be defi ned as the “pedagogical” dimension
of memory.
Commemorative processes, territorial markers, the reclaiming of sites,
commemorative dates, the changing of street names, and similar acts entail at
least two stages. In the fi rst, a group of actors establishes the issue, pressuring
the state to take responsibility for the past through truth commissions and
trials, reparations, and memory policies. Demands on the state may include
the observation of certain dates and the marking of certain sites, be they
physical or symbolic. Nothing happens by chance; in a present bound to past
events, the outcome depends on the actions of committed subjects engaged
in current political struggles. As also stated before, even when the actors use
every means available to try to promote their interpretations of the past, these
will never entirely be set in the stone of a monument or crystalized in the
engraving on a commemorative plaque. Th e monument and the plaque are
just supports—ones riddled with ambiguities—for the subjective work and
the collective, political, and symbolic action of other actors at other historical
moments, in the political-social junctures to come.
In a second stage, which may coincide in temporal terms with the moment
when the issue is established, the “pedagogical” intention comes to the fore:
actors want to convey specifi c meanings of the past to the general public and
to future generations. In addition to paying tribute, then, markers connected
to the past are inscribed with a projection for the future, that is, an idea etched
today in relation to the message yesterday holds for tomorrow. Th e aim is to
ensure a better future and avoid repeating the errors and horrors of the past.

188 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Th is manner of interpreting and acting responds to an ethical imperative
many experience, the “duty to remember,” which accepts as a given the rela-
tionship between memory (preservation, conservation, and transmission),
justice, and democracy. A critical analysis of the connections between the
three requires repositioning memories in a broader context that takes into
consideration multiple long-term structural inequalities as well as the link
between memory and present-day politics.
In the initial stages of democratic transitions, a slogan took hold. Th ough
formulated in various ways, nunca más was repeated across the board to
convey the pressing need to ensure that the violence under dictatorship
would “never again” be repeated in the future. How can that catchphrase be
interpreted? What was it that should never be repeated? What conditions
had to be in place to ensure this? Th e “duty to remember,” a golden rule tied
to the idea of recordar para no repetir (remember to not repeat), was present
right from the start. What has to be remembered? To put it more precisely,
was it the violence itself that should never be repeated—or the conditions
that led to the violence?
According to the main argument put forth during the transition, transmit-
ting the memory of violence and past atrocities was fundamental to building
democratic systems and laying the groundwork for a democratic citizenry in
the future. In fact, when the countries of the Southern Cone were making
the transition from dictatorship to democracy in the 1980s and early 1990s,
activating memories of the repressive past was considered essential for con-
solidating democratic institutions: it was impossible to build democracy as
long as the crimes committed by the state went unpunished. Th is certainty
was transformed into a slogan that scholars, activists and progressive politi-
cians alike treated as irrefutable. And many continue to do so. Th is slogan
implied that we can only build a forward-looking democracy if we remember
our dictatorial past and face it head-on. Th is was the assumption behind the
memory project that drove political engagement. Yet as the years passed, this
assumption grew into a formidable question: Are active politics of memory
necessary to build and sustain democratic institutions? And to take this a step
further, what kind of democracy? Are we talking about formal institutions or
democratic social practices?
Th is takes us back to the question of what it is that has to be remembered.
What specifi c aspects of democracy are connected to the memories of a
dictatorial past and violence—and which specifi c memories are at stake? In
order to explore some of its most concrete aspects, I propose breaking down
the relationship between memory and the various elements of democracy.
To do so, I will fi rst analyze the working of some political institutions, then
turn to some of the symbolic processes, and fi nally examine certain initiatives
in the area of education and the transmission of memory. My aim is not to

Memory—for What? • 189
exhaustively analyze all the processes and dimensions of change in these areas
but rather to focus on certain scenarios that will enable new questions on the
relationship between memory and democracy.
Institutional Processes
In the arguments presented in this chapter, the word “democracy” appears
frequently. A controversial concept, democracy has multiple meanings and
descriptors: formal, representative, delegative, substantive, participatory, etc.
Th ose who discuss and draw associations between memories of the recent
violent past and the democratic horizon of the present and future are actually
referring to multiple dimensions and conceptions of “democracy.”1
I will start with the formal and institutional aspects of a democratic
political system. Let us look fi rst at the transition to democracy in Spain.
Beginning in 1975, after forty years of silence and the repression of memory
under the rule of General Francisco Franco, the country was fl ooded with
recollections about the civil war in both testimonies and a range of cultural
productions, including fi lm, literature, and music. At the institutional level,
however, the reference to the past took on a special meaning: it was the deep
confrontations and profound confl icts that led up to the civil war that should
never be repeated. In fact, the Spanish government has hardly acknowledged
the atrocities committed under the Franco dictatorship or the violence and
suff ering that occurred during the Spanish Civil War. Th e state did not
introduce any policies aimed at justice or memory during the crucial period
between the death of General Franco in 1975 and the ratifi cation of the
new Spanish Constitution in 1978, nor have they done so since, even after
government institutions were fully operational. Th ere were no trials to bring
those responsible to justice, nor was there a single proposal to remove the
symbols of the Franco dictatorship or acknowledge its victims. Th e state took
no measures to implement what would become known a few decades later
internationally as “transitional justice.”
On the surface, it was a transition anchored in silence, perhaps with the
intention to foster oblivion. According to Aguilar Fernández (1996), how-
ever, the decisions made by Spain’s key political actors were in fact clearly
and specifi cally tied to memory, since their goal was to fi nd an institutional
solution that would help prevent the repetition of the conditions that led to
the Spanish Civil War. Th ese actors zeroed in on the political confl icts that
had arisen during the Second Spanish Republic rather than on the disputes
and suff ering that had occurred during the civil war and under Franco. In
fact, policies related to memory did not appear in Spain until decades later,
in 2007, when the Ley de Memoria Histórica (historical memory act) was

190 • Th e Struggle for the Past
enacted. Th us, the discussion about the past, its memories, and its legacies
intensifi ed more than seventy years after the start of the Spanish Civil War
and nearly forty years after Franco’s death (Vinyes 2009). Yet despite the
confl ict and ambivalence in the state’s position on memory, no one doubts
that formal democracy—the electoral system, the division of powers, etc.—is
consolidated in Spain.
Something analogous can be said of the transition to democracy in Chile,
when democratic political parties—Christian Democrats, Socialists, and
other smaller center-left parties—formed a political coalition that would
have been unthinkable before the dictatorship. Th e Concertación de Partidos
por la Democracia (Party Coalition for Democracy), or its shorter name,
Concertación, as the coalition became known, held steady for at least two
decades. Th e aim of replacing confrontation with concertación was to avoid
repeating the political strife characteristic of Chile before the 1973 coup
that toppled the democratically elected Socialist government of President
Salvador Allende. Th e electoral campaigns of 1989 focused not on the past
but on the future, as expressed in the slogan of the winning Concertación
candidate, “El futuro comienza ya” (Th e future starts now).
In both Spain and Chile, political actors wagered on building a new
order that would prevent confl icts similar to those of the past. At the insti-
tutional level, memory to avoid repetition was paramount to the democratic
transitions in both countries, though memory in these cases did not refer
specifi cally to the violence of the war or dictatorship but to the institutional
conditions that provoked discord and violence. Saying nunca más was about
more than simply dictatorship, and expressed the need to avoid repeating the
political conditions that led up to one. In historical and normative terms,
there is no reason to privilege memories corresponding to one particular
historical period. Th e memory of both periods can be equally valid and even
necessary. Likewise, actors may bring to the fore references to many other
historical moments of confl ict and violence waged against subaltern social
groups. Th ere is evidence, however, of historicity in dominant memories.
Historical actors “use” or select the milestones from the past they wish to
integrate into the present-day confrontations they face in their current politi-
cal and social milieu. Th e past that reappears in memories, then, interacts
with the present.
In Chile, Spain, and many other countries, institutions remained largely
silent about dictatorship. Over time, this opened the door for a range of
actors—including, eventually, the state itself—to take actions aimed at set-
tling accounts with the dictatorial past. Th ese included demands for truth
and reconciliation commissions, reparations for exiles (in Chile, for exam-
ple), and the promotion of cultural activities, monuments, museums, and
territorial markers of various sorts. Of course, if a broader view of human

Memory—for What? • 191
rights and democracy is taken when assessing democratic transitions, many
accounts have yet to be settled.2
Th e case of Argentina was diff erent. As we saw in chapter 3, the emphasis
was on justice. At the time of the transition, one of the key ideas was that
impunity for past crimes would lead to impunity in the future and thus have
implications for establishing rule of law. In Argentina, those responsible for
state crimes during dictatorship were tried in court, while other countries
in the world declared amnesties. Yet beyond the specifi c workings of the
judicial system in cases related to state repression, it is important to ask how
these trials aff ected the social visibility of the judicial system and the quality
of democratic institutions. Has the judiciary improved as a result of having
prosecuted perpetrators and of continuing to hold trials linked to past repres-
sion thirty or more years after the transition to democracy?
Th e question of the strategies and possibilities of judicial intervention in
specifi c historical contexts has been a topic of normative and political debate
for a number of political actors and scholars in diff erent parts of the world.
Nevertheless, it is critical to acknowledge that the crimes were committed
and should be dealt with in court, so as to determine who was responsible
and bring them to justice. Th ese trials also show that the judicial system
functions properly and takes action. Given the long period in which the
vast majority of the population knew nothing of the workings of the judicial
branch, a demonstration of this sort is both basic and fundamental.
Th e trials held in the 1980s provided evidence that a functional, indepen-
dent judicial system was being constructed, changing civic understanding of
what institutional justice implies and bringing the judicial branch closer to
society. Th is is important, given that the judicial branch in Argentina (and
in other countries in the region) has a long history of connections to the
dominant elites and has been used as an instrument of power. Th is began to
change—at least in Argentina—when the human rights movement began
to voice its demands. A spatial expression of this shift in perception can be
seen in the way protestors used public space within the city of Buenos Aires.
Traditionally, political street mobilizations of all types—led by trade unions,
political forces, and social actors—walked the avenue line that links Congress
to the Casa Rosada (the seat of the executive branch). During the last period
of dictatorship and during the transition, marchers began to trace a triangle,
incorporating the Palace of Justice to the route. Th e arrival of protesters to
the most emblematic building of the court system is the spatial expression of
this change, a metaphor of a cultural shift in which citizens incorporated the
judiciary as an institution to which they could relate directly.
At the time of the trial of the juntas, Argentine society had accepted the
judiciary as a working institution, though its belief in the system waned
in later decades. Broadly speaking, that fi rst round of trials of the former

192 • Th e Struggle for the Past
military commanders in 1985 had a signifi cant cultural impact, establish-
ing the courts as a legitimate forum for the social demands for rights. One
example of this newfound citizen awareness is the enormous number of social
security lawsuits brought by retirees over the years. Hundreds of thousands
of retirees have seen their rights violated and now know that legal recourse is
available. Th is seemingly “simple” idea was not so simple: fi rst and foremost,
it required that these individuals interpret a decision they perceived as unjust
as a violation of a right. It then required a realization that they could appeal
to a specifi c institution to address this injustice. Th is is by no means a univer-
sal belief, but a historical and cultural product that had to be constructed and
must thus not be taken for granted. In Argentina, the trials on the violations
of human rights during dictatorship played a crucial role in this cultural con-
struction. Importantly, the strength of this belief goes beyond the fact that a
trial was held and people were found guilty of such crimes. Nonetheless, this
is no guarantee of the quality of the judicial system: in the decades following
the fi rst trial of the military commanders, there has been ample proof of the
shortcomings of the judicial system in the country.
Th is visibility of the judiciary may have led to what some qualify as
“excesses,” that is, a trend toward an increasing judicialization of political
confl icts. In the face of a range of political confl icts, political actors often
forgo the negotiating table for a day in court. At the same time, the visibility
of the judiciary has led to power struggles and ideological and political dis-
putes over the democratic workings (or lack thereof) of the judicial branch.
Th is has been referred to as the politicization of the judiciary, and involves
disputes over the recruitment of judges, a demand that certain posts within
the judiciary be elective positions, and signifi cant confrontations between the
executive and members of the judicial branch (Smulovitz 2008).
Perhaps it is still too early to compare and analyze the evolution of the
courts at the transnational level in post-transitional periods. Starting in the
1980s, amnesties and attempts at consensus and reconciliation were pro-
posed in many places in the world. Th e argument was that holding trials
would only serve to sustain or worsen political confl icts and violence, and
thus prevent wounds from healing. In some cases, it was believed that truth
commissions, with their focus on acknowledging the suff ering of victims
and giving them a voice, could be a palliative and reconciliatory option
preferable to justice (Minow 1998, among others). Th e establishment of the
International Criminal Court in 1998 may have been a landmark and con-
tributed to changing this perspective.
Th e international information available on trials for human rights viola-
tions indicates that since the Argentine trials of the 1980s, there has been a
“justice cascade” (Sikkink 2011), a rising tide of trials of this type worldwide.
Sikkink also discusses the false dilemma implicit in the understanding that

Memory—for What? • 193
trials and truth commissions (along with other types of victim assistance)
are mutually exclusive policy alternatives. In other words, “justice” is not
opposed to “truth”; rather, taking action in one direction can lead to further
action in others. When jointly implemented, the two support one another
in a virtuous circle of “truth, justice and memory.” A third and important
clarifi cation is that there is no evidence indicating that trials on human
rights violations pose a threat to democratic stability. Of course, there are not
that many cases, nor has enough time elapsed, to gauge the more long-term
eff ects of such trials. What is clear, however, is that the institutional measures
for addressing the past have no negative eff ects on the political system. Th e
question on how institutions benefi t from the process, however, remains to
be seen.
Th ese are merely examples of how elaborating responses to the recent past
connects with building more democratic institutions, though these connec-
tions may not be certain, direct, or linear. Others could be added in relation
to issues of administration and governance and as mechanisms for citizenship
participation. Furthermore, questions also remain with regard to the specifi c
institutional impacts of symbolic memory policies, which are undoubtedly
more diffi cult to ascertain. In broader terms, a comparative analysis of coun-
tries and societies that have undergone dictatorships and periods of political
violence would be useful to determine how diff erent “transitional justice”
instruments aff ect the quality of institutions.
Social, Cultural, and Symbolic Processes
As already noted, the framework of human rights is a relatively recent addi-
tion to societal thinking and action. In the 1970s, with the emergence and
expansion of repressive regimes in the Southern Cone (fi rst in Uruguay and
Chile, then in Argentina, plus Paraguay, Bolivia, and Brazil, where dictators
were already in power), the interpretation of political repression as “human
rights violations” emerged and gained momentum (Keck and Sikkink 1998;
Markarian 2005). Up until then, and despite the fact that the countries
of Latin America had supported and signed the Universal Declaration of
Human Rights in 1948, political confl icts and repressive violence were
described in terms of “winners” and “losers” rather than in terms of “perpe-
trators” and “victims.” Th e new international paradigm of human rights, and
its way of conceiving and punishing violations, slowly and gradually became
hegemonic.
In this regard, there are various questions to explore, and I would like to
put forth the hypothesis that memory of the recent past and the expansion of
the human rights paradigm do not necessarily go together; at times, they may

194 • Th e Struggle for the Past
even be disconnected. To illustrate these developments, I will start by consid-
ering Argentina, where, starting in the mid-1970s, a strong movement began
denouncing the crimes that were being committed under the dictatorship.
Gradually and steadily, these crimes came to be interpreted within the para-
digm of human rights violations. It is unquestionable that human rights were
violated: torture, disappearance, murder, illegal detention, and kidnapped
children were all horrifi c crimes against people’s lives and integrity. In current
international law and legal discourse, these are referred to as “crimes against
humanity,” and no statutory limitation applies to them.
As analyzed in several chapters of this book, the government has devised a
wide range of memory policies since the transition with the aim of address-
ing and attempting to mitigate the eff ects and legacies of the dictatorship,
though the extent of these eff orts has varied with each administration. Once
the new paradigm of human rights was established globally in the 1970s,
the group of organizations and activists denouncing the crimes perpetrated
under dictatorships became known as the “human rights movement.” As
a result, at least in Argentina, the expression “human rights” is tied to the
demands surrounding violations committed during the military regime.
Nonetheless, the notion of human rights is much wider, encompassing
civil and political rights, but also economic, social, cultural, individual, and
collective rights. Th e question then remains as to how the emphasis on the
memory of dictatorships helps or hinders a social or state-level acceptance
of this broader conceptualization of human rights. Th erefore, the question
as to what the notion of human rights currently comprises is a relevant one.
Are human rights restricted to settling accounts with the victims of repres-
sion and the violence of recent history? Or is there room for this broader
perspective?
Th e issue now is whether human rights policies cover more than the vic-
tims of political violence and the repression suff ered in recent history. Th e
overemphasis on the recent political past leads to a disconnect between this
relatively narrow or limited meaning of human rights linked to dictatorship
and a broader understanding of the term. Human rights covers broader cat-
egories of the population (indigenous groups, ethnic minorities, women, the
elderly, people with disabilities, and so on) and issues related to economic,
social, environmental, and cultural rights. It also refers to longer historical
periods—a long-term history and a focus on violations occurring even now
under formally democratic regimes. In spite of the fact that this extensive and
encompassing human rights agenda is supported internationally, in Argentina
the expression “human rights” often evokes memories of dictatorship, not an
indigenous child dying from hunger or a curable illness. Furthermore, many
of the protagonists of the struggles associated with the memories of the past

Memory—for What? • 195
do not address how memories of dictatorship related to the construction of a
broader human rights culture.
At this point, it is important to note the temporality of the phenomena
under discussion. Th ere is a “short” time—that of dictatorships and violence,
and the subsequent transition—in which we are immersed. And there is
a “long” time of social and historical construction and structuring, a time
in which inequalities, discrimination, and exclusion are embedded. When
studying memories of specifi c groups, one fi nds that the long time and the
short time are condensed: long-lasting memories and shorter-term memories
are inextricably tied. In her study on the memories of dictatorship among
communities in northern Argentina, Ludmila da Silva Catela (2007) intro-
duced these notions of long- and short-term memory. Th is double temporal-
ity is also present in Kimberly Th eidon’s analysis of the memories of women
in Peruvian indigenous communities (2007). Ethnographic studies of these
types of communities show how dictatorship, repression, and violence from
the recent past are interwoven with structural discrimination and violence
dating far back. Th e frame of reference for the recent past, then, is longer
term.3 I would like to pose a question related to an entirely diff erent context:
Japan, the 2011 tsunami, the earthquake, and the radioactive leak. What
place does the memory of Hiroshima hold in the Japanese people’s experience
of Fukushima?
Th ese questions appear when a given moment in time is considered from
a historical perspective, one based on long-term memory. In this regard, the
preface to the report by the Peruvian Truth and Reconciliation Commission
(Comisión de Verdad y Reconciliación) is particularly telling. In its analysis
of the violence that occurred from 1980 through 2000, it notes that
Th ree out of every four victims were peasants whose mother tongue was
Quechua. As Peruvians know very well, this is a sector of the population
historically ignored by the state and urban society, that society that reaps the
benefi ts of our political community. Th e Commission has not found any basis
to assert that this was an ethnic confl ict, despite allegations to the contrary.
Yet there are grounds to assert that these two decades of destruction and death
could not have been possible without the deep contempt or disrespect towards
the most dispossessed population of the country, as shown equally by the
members of PCP-Sendero Luminoso and by agents of the state. It is a disre-
spect that is woven into each moment of everyday life of the Peruvian people.
(Comisión de la Verdad y Reconciliación 2003: 1–2)
In general, when we speak of memory, we usually refer to violence and suff er-
ing, dictatorship, human rights violation, crimes committed by the regime,
etc. Th e memories cited allude to these extreme situations. Th e question

196 • Th e Struggle for the Past
remains as to the continuity of violence—the violence rooted in history and
in the structure of domination and inequality.
If we connect this topic to the earlier discussion on institutional changes,
it is important to note that in recent decades, constitutional reforms in coun-
tries across the region have recognized many “new” rights connected to his-
torical discrimination and structural inequality, especially among indigenous
people. Did the memories of dictatorships and violence play a role in these
changes? Or were these changes the result of shifts in the international politi-
cal climate and/or the relative power of domestic groups? It is also relevant
to ask whether and how normative changes (such as constitutional amend-
ments) are evident in state practices (policies aimed at ending inequalities)
and social practices (ethnic and gender discrimination, among others). Th e
purpose of these changes is to expand rights and citizen participation, yet the
question remains: what does memory of the recent past have to do with the
implementation of new rights?
On the other hand, the dissemination and broad acceptance of the human
rights paradigm emphasizes the centrality of the victim, the acknowledgment
of their suff ering, and the intention to compensate them. Defi ning someone
as a victim of human rights violations means focusing on the harm one has
experienced, which in turn means relegating—or even silencing—the vic-
tim’s capacity for action as a refl ective, purposeful subject. Th is can lead to
a growing number of claims anchored in one’s self-identifi cation as a victim.
Th e rights associated with being a victim make victimization a strategy, even
for perpetrators who seek to minimize or deny their criminal acts. Members
of the Argentine military often present themselves in public as victims (Salvi
2012), as do some politicians, who claim to be victims of the media time and
again.
In short, it is important to ask whether the victimization inherent to the
human rights paradigm contradicts the demands of an active citizenry in a
participatory democracy. If victimhood is the dominant language that frames
the understanding of human rights, a perspective that fosters active citizen-
ship runs the risk of being seen—and therefore blamed—as one that denies
the importance of pain, suff ering, and the violation of rights.
Educational and Pedagogical Aims of Memory and
Its Transmission: For Whom? Memory of What?
Children and adolescents regularly visit sites of memory, memorials, and
museums, accompanied by their teachers and/or guides. School groups
of diff erent ages are a common sight at Auschwitz and Sachsenhausen,
Germany; the D2 detention center in Córdoba, Argentina; and the Museo

Memory—for What? • 197
de la Memoria in Santiago, Chile. Students walk, stopping at certain spots
to hear an explanation, and continue walking. As with any group of children
or teens, some stay close to their instructors and pay attention to what is
said, while others are distracted, fooling around, or looking elsewhere. What
happens before or after these visits—that is, what preparation the students
receive beforehand and how much they will discuss, analyze, or debate in
class what they have seen on their own—remains unknown. Th e “explana-
tions” provided at such places are frequently literal descriptions of what hap-
pened there: “Th e prisoners lived here, in these barracks.” “Th ere was room
for X number of prisoners, but at times three, fi ve, or fi fty times more were
living here—if you can call that living.” “Th ere weren’t enough bathrooms.”
“Th ey were given barely any food.” “Th ey were woken up very early and had
to line up,” etc. Descriptions of horror, with villains and victims, without
providing any logic or rationale behind these actions.
When seeing these groups of children running through the entrance to
Auschwitz or elsewhere, just as they would during a fi eld trip or a visit to the
zoo, the question that arises is, for what? What is the pedagogical purpose
of this visit? Is it a ritual similar to raising the fl ag or singing the national
anthem at ceremonies? It this teaching history? Is it part of civic education,
fostering the development of responsible, democratic, and sensitive citizens?
My idea is not to engage in a debate on why history is taught, or discuss the
relationship between truth and narration. My aim is to approach the topic
through an analysis of a range of extracurricular and outreach programs and
projects connected to memory sites. Are these visits or simply sightseeing?
Should educational materials be prepared? What kind of materials? What for?
Th ese programs include diverse models and proposals, be they implicit
or explicit. One model places emphasis on relaying information about what
actually happened, based on the assumption that knowing has a direct
impact on the subjects’ democratic and civic formation. “Remembering to
not repeat” means putting together a fact-based account of what occurred
and conveying it to those who did not experience it. In the aftermath of
intense violence or social catastrophes, the magnitude and nature of the
events have yet to be systematically organized or framed within a meaningful
narrative. During this period, it is essential to organize factual data and for-
mulate a story of what has occurred. Th e vehicles are diverse: documentary
and fi ction fi lms, educational materials, chronologies, testimony of survivors,
talks and lectures about specifi c topics. Many of the guided tours of former
concentration camps or detention centers are organized to follow “what hap-
pened here” as literally as possible.
Th e relationship between getting information and becoming an active
democratic citizen, however, is not linear or direct. Educators today know
this, and as a result, a transmission strategy anchored in information is

198 • Th e Struggle for the Past
Figure 8.1. School visit and educational activities at the Provincial Memory Archive,
Córdoba, Argentina. Photographs courtesy Archivo Fotográfi co, Archivo Provincial de
la Memoria.

Memory—for What? • 199
supplemented—or even replaced—by another that promotes refl ection.
Depending on the program, this strategy can be referred to as critical refl ec-
tion, memoria democrática (democratic memory), historical memory, autono-
mous local and regional processes to clarify truth and build memories, etc.
An underlying idea-force, that of the “duty to remember,” is shared by all
these programs: the moral imperative or civic duty to remember the horror
as a safeguard against future violence and horror. In addition, that memory
and that moral obligation ensure the formation of citizens with democratic
convictions and practices.
Let’s examine a few specifi c examples.
Since its establishment in 1999, the Memory Commission of the Buenos
Aires Province (Comisión Provincial de la Memoria de la Provincia de Buenos
Aires) has incorporated education and transmission as two central goals.4
One of its fi rst tasks was to prepare informative booklets organized in chron-
ological order, with information about the military regime in Argentina and
the transition. Th e way teachers used these educational materials depended,
at the beginning, on their personal views, experiences, and memories of the
dictatorship, and on those of school principals. At the time, most of the
teachers participating in its programs had lived through the dictatorship
and had relatively strong views either for or against it. As the years passed,
newer generations of teachers who did not have that fi rsthand knowledge or
information made use of these educational materials in a diff erent way. For
these teachers, the materials provided factual information on processes that
were intensely debated in the public sphere. For that reason, notes Sandra
Raggio, these materials prove increasingly useful in pedagogical terms as the
years pass.
Th e program’s objective and focus have gradually changed from the teach-
ing of recent history to a focus on the high school students themselves. Th e
aim is to create spaces in schools and other social institutions where students
can work on questions connected with the dictatorial past, but also where
they can address current-day human rights linked to their local communi-
ties.5 Since the young people themselves draft their own proposals, these tend
to be highly diverse, responding to the specifi c concerns of each student. In
most cases, student projects deal with current aff airs, such as the environ-
ment, police brutality, gender discrimination, sexual violence, and safety.
Experience indicates that in this large-scale program, students connect the
past and present in diverse ways, allowing them to come upon unexpected
links.
On its website, the program reveals a broad and inclusive approach to the
notion of human rights through its mission. Th e diverse themes are bound by
a shared approach, i.e. a human rights perspective, and the local grounding
of student projects. In this regard, the emphasis is on exploring the roots of

200 • Th e Struggle for the Past
current injustices and inequalities in the past, particularly during the years of
dictatorship from 1976 to 1983.
Th ere are many places where societal and government institutions have
introduced pedagogical initiatives and programs that foster the recovery or
construction of memories of violent and repressive pasts. Far from taking a
more mechanical approach to the transmission of information, most of these
programs foster refl ection and youth’s active role as part of the pedagogical
strategy. Th e expectation is that refl ections on the past will contribute to
a learning experience of citizenship geared to the future. Whether or not
answers are found to these open questions is something we cannot know
until the future.
Th e proliferation of pedagogical proposals that promote recovering or
“building” memories can be seen on numerous other website and projects,
where memories anchored in past violence and repression are connected
with present-day experiences. Th e terminology and phrasing varies, with
terms like “democratic memories,” “historical memory,” “social memory,” or
“unspoken memories.” In practice, what is interesting to note is that when
programs create a space for the young and “others” (those not defi ned as vic-
tims) to elaborate their own narratives and interpretations of human rights
violations, the reference to past violence may take a back burner to their own
local, communal, and personal experiences.
Th e basic questions still remain, and with no clear answer: What do we
know about how the past relates to the present and the future? What do we
know about how the horrors of the past relate to the construction of everyday
democratic practices, today and tomorrow? What metrics should be used to
evaluate all of these initiatives and programs?
Closing Th oughts
Th is brief exploration raises more questions than answers, but these questions
point to a research agenda to continue thinking about these topics. To do so,
perhaps it may be useful to go back to some of the issues raised throughout
this book.
First and foremost, it is fundamental to insist on the importance of his-
toricizing memories, of examining the history of memories over time and
not treating memories as crystallized meanings. Th ere is a history of both
institutional and symbolic processes to draw on, a history that is not random
or accidental: each stage and juncture imply new opportunities, though at
the risk of inhibiting or excluding other processes. At the pedagogical level,
some of the programs described herein speak of transmitting “truths”; others
make reference to “building memories,” which suggests pluralistic visions

Memory—for What? • 201
and the possibilities for diverse, even opposing, appropriations. Th is appears
in the model and in the proposal, but it is not always achieved in practice—
especially when a single or dominant story is combined with emphasis on
promoting and expanding a “duty to remember.”
Th ere is no single memory that we know: diff erent actors and diff erent
generations give diff erent meanings to the past. Th ere are also various pasts,
some short and limited (specifi c dictatorial regimes), others seen as processes
that evolve over longer periods. In turn, the emphasis on the “recent past”
can overshadow or conceal structurally embedded violence and discrimina-
tion. It is thus necessary to situate short-term memories within much longer
temporal frames.
Th e past is a source of dispute, one that leads actors to express, silence,
emphasize, and suppress diff erent elements when building their own story.
What we encounter is a struggle for memories, a social and political struggle
in which institutional, symbolic, and social power are at stake. Memory pro-
cesses show up institutionally, culturally, and symbolically in diff erent arenas
of social life. Th ere may be moments of alignment and coherence between
these arenas, and others of disjunction. Alignment, however, will be the
exception, since memories are always fragmentary and never unquestionable,
congruent units or systems.
Finally, those who act in response to a “duty to remember” generally have
one story and one interpretation of the past, and they seek to convey this
version to those who either did not have the experience or do not share their
interpretation. Taken to an extreme, this position may contradict the goals
of social transformation through the construction of an active citizenry, one
familiar with the full spectrum of human rights and committed to refl ective
debate in the public sphere based on democratic convictions. In theory and
in fact, it is impossible to transmit univocal meaning on the one hand and
promote thoughtful, active citizens on the other. Challenging the assump-
tion of a direct, linear relationship between memory and democracy means
acknowledging the complexity of sociopolitical reality and recognizing that
the future is, to a large extent, open-ended and uncertain.

.org.ar/archivos/2271.
2. Continuing with the cases of Chile and Spain, neither the Chilean policies regarding the
country’s indigenous groups nor Spain’s immigration policies embrace an inclusive notion
of democracy and human rights. Pilar Calveiro made this point in a comment on an earlier

202 • Th e Struggle for the Past
version of this chapter. Her remarks can be read at memoria.ides.org.ar/archivos/2271. On

3. Th ese longer-term temporalities also show up in autobiographical accounts, as seen in the
case of Lucía (chapter 7).
4. Sandra Raggio, former director of the “Youth and Memory” Program and, since 2014,
the general director of the Memory Commission of the Buenos Aires Province, shared her

gram was supplemented by the information available on the website (lamemoria.org) and
various publications by the commission. Her critical refl ections provided crucial input for

5. Th e program provides capacity building for teachers and community youth leaders who
train and tutor groups of young people. Th e youth then develop and present projects in a
province-wide competition. Th e selected projects are implemented during several months
of extracurricular activities (research, interviews, preparation of audiovisual materials, and
so on) and all outputs are presented in a weeklong gathering. In the program’s fi rst
seventeen years, more than one hundred thousand young people and more than fi fteen
thousand teachers participated (2019). For more information, see www.comisionporla
memoria.org.
